Modern Hospice Design
The new edition of this acclaimed book comprehensively updates its timely
advocacy of the need for good quality palliative care, today more necessary
than ever. Rooted in the social history of the care of the elderly and ter-
minally ill, Modern Hospice Design: The Architecture of Palliative and Social Care
takes cognisance of the new conditions of social care in the 21st century,
principally in the UK, Europe and North America. It does so with regard to
the development of new building types, but also in response to new philos-
ophies of palliative care and the status of the elderly and the dying.
Benefitting from a clearer methodological approach and conceptual
framework, the expanded book allows a broad section of readers to navi-
gate the text more easily. At its core is a public discussion of a philosophy
of design for providing care for the elderly and the vulnerable, taking the
importance of architectural aesthetics, the use of quality materials, the
porousness of design to the wider world, and the integration of indoor and
outdoor spaces as part of the overall care environment. In doing so it advo-
cates care settings that, in the words of Maggie Jencks whose life and ideas
inspired the Maggie’s Centres, ‘rise to the occasion’. Including new chapters
and new in-depth case studies, complete with full colour illustrations, this
book is for architects and interior designers and their students, healthcare
professionals, social care providers, estate and facility managers, hospital
administrators and Healthcare Trust Boards.
Ken Worpole is the author of a number of admired books and studies on
architecture, landscape and urban design, and an adviser on public pol-
icy to the UK government, the Commission for Architecture and the Built
Environment, and the Heritage Lottery Fund. A former Professor at The
Cities Institute of London Metropolitan University, in the words of The
New Statesman, ‘Worpole is a literary original, a social and architectural
historian whose books combine the Orwellian ideal of common decency
with understated erudition.’ Elsewhere, The Independent newspaper has writ-
ten that, ‘For many years Ken Worpole has been one of the shrewdest and
sharpest observers of the English social landscape.’

‘Like one who breaks up from a hospitable house
where nothing is lacking,
where everything has been put on the table
I then want to express my thanks for the home in which
I stayed as a guest, for the human home.
With my hand on the gate I wish to look back, as if
I could remember what I will leave.
And then I want to look forward again, although homeless.’
Evening Land, Pär Lagerkvist, 1953, translated by
W.H. Auden

William Carlos Williams

Acknowledgements for Second Edition
The original edition of Modern Hospice Architecture could not have been writ-
ten without the initial enthusiasm and support of Mervyn Taylor of Hospice
Friendly Hospices, Irish Hospice Foundation, together with the continuing
intellectual engagement and encouragement of architect, Ian Clarke, of
JDDK Architects, who died much too young in 2015, and whose dedication
to the cause of a humane architecture is sorely missed. Particular thanks
were due to Caroline Mallinder, Georgina Johnson, and Francesca Ford
of Taylor & Francis who, between them, commissioned the first edition
and brought it to fruition. I am glad to thank Francesca Ford once again
for being so enthusiastic about the idea of a new edition, and her colleague,
Hannah Studd, for undertaking the editorial arrangements.
For agreeing to be interviewed for the new case studies, I would like to
thank Kara Bishop, Andrew Burrell, David Scott-Ralphs, Nic Hoar, Rick
Rowbotham (St Wilfrid’s, Eastbourne), Amanda Levete, Dame Laura Lee,
Gilly Howard-Jones, and Sarah Price (Maggie’s Southampton), Rebecca
Gallimore, Kerry Stack, and Hardev Thandy (The ExtraCare Charitable
Trust) at Lark Hill Village, Nottingham, Martyn Craddock, and Alison
Benzimra of United St Saviour’s Charity and architect Stephen Witherford
of Witherford Watson Mann (Appleby Blue Almshouse, Southwark).
Dr Sarah McCloskey, now CEO at Marymount University Hospital &
Hospice in Cork, kindly updated me on how the new hospice and hospital
had settled in, since in the first edition it was only at development stage.
Thanks also to architects Phyllida Mills and Mark Power for spending time to
show me around their inspiring CARE education centre at St Christopher’s,
Sydenham.
I would not have had the energy and enthusiasm for tackling the subject
again had it not been for work of Bernike Pasveer at Maastricht Univer-
sity who with other Dutch colleagues organised an extraordinary five-day
international workshop in the Lorentz Centre in Leiden in September 2018,
where so many important issues relating to ‘Home Making in Care for Later
Life’ were shared and discussed. In addition, Heather Richardson, former
CEO at St Christopher’s, provided introductions and an excellent guiding
hand to the world of post-Covid hospice concerns, and so I thank her as I
do Juliet Davis, John Harries, Daryl Martin, and Julia Park for thoughtful
insights into the philosophy of palliative care. Respect is also due to the
work of Housing LIN (Learning and Improvement Network) in the UK,

doi: 10.4324/9781003306818-1
If the 20th century was ‘The Century of the Child’, then the 21st century
may well become ‘The Century of the Older Person’, the latter the result
of advances in child welfare and education in the previous century. Today,
people are living longer than ever before, and more do so. However, a longer
life can mean a longer period towards the end of life marked by ill-health,
frailty, and the need for greater personal care. Changes in patterns of fam-
ily life mean that care in old age is now largely the province of institutions
rather than a family responsibility, and these institutions may be provided
by the state, by religious organisations, charities, and the private sector.
For a long time, those who did not die at home died in hospital, many of
which were neither designed nor managed to meet the particular needs of
the elderly or the dying. One result has been the development of new kinds
of provision to meet such needs, such as hospices, Maggie’s Cancer Care
Centres, extra-care homes, new iterations of the traditional almshouse, and
retirement villages. In this second edition of Modern Hospice Design, much
greater attention is now paid to this ‘widening spectrum’ of palliative and
social care now being developed today to meet the new demand.
The century of the child and the century of the
elderly
On New Year’s Day, 1900, in a spirit of high optimism, the Swedish designer
and social reformer, Ellen Key, published her design manifesto, Barnets
århundrade (The Century of the Child). The Italian philosopher and education-
alist, Maria Montessori, whose ideas on pedagogy were shortly to circle the
globe, claimed that Key had defined the great social project of their age.
This was, in the words of design historian Juliet Kinchin, ‘that the well-
being of children was the defining mission of the century to come’.1
In 2012, the Museum of Modern Art (MOMA) in New York celebrated
Key’s manifesto in a retrospective exhibition called Century of the Child:
Growing by Design 1900–2000, charting the child-centred ambitions of archi-
tecture and design in the 20th century. The exhibition revisited Key’s
ideas, while provocatively suggesting that it was in designing for children
that enabled the critical breakthrough into modernism. The young were
perceived as having a natural affinity with colour and pattern – as well
1A new world of palliative and
social care

-- 16 of 203 --

2 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
as appreciating the tactile and sensual attributes of design – all of which
became distinguishing characteristics of the modernist credo.
Women designers and educationalists such as Key herself, Montessori,
Jane Addams, Grete Lihotzky, Friedl Dicker, and here in Britain, the McMil-
lan sisters and Dora Russell all played a major part in this stylistic and social
upheaval. According to Kinchin, it was the kindergarten movement that cre-
ated ‘the building blocks of modern design’. Architects such as Frank Lloyd
Wright, Jan Duiker, Kaj Gottlob, Richard Neutra, Eliel Saarinen, Aldo van
Eyck, and Grete Lihotzky were inspired by the new pedagogy, and their
designs for nurseries, open-air schools, and children’s clinics created the
pattern book for a modernist civic architecture. While not everybody was
happy with this fundamental refashioning of childhood, it became a settled
state of affairs and remains an unprecedented achievement.
If the 20th century was the century of the child, then the 21st cen-
tury may well become the century of the elderly or the ‘century of the
centenarian’ – as design professor Jeremy Myerson has termed it. 2 Ageing
populations are causing governments to re-think public health priorities
and resources. Where spending money on the health of children and young
people earlier possessed a simple moral clarity – who could possibly disa-
gree with ‘investing in the future’? – prioritising the needs of the elderly,
particularly in relation to housing and care is relatively new political ter-
ritory, just as living to old age is a recent phenomenon. Both designing for
the needs of children and designing for the needs of older people belong to
something we might call social architecture, an architecture based on ide-
als of comfort, recreation, sociability, and wellbeing (or human flourishing
as the philosophers prefer to call it), rather than workplace efficiency or
public representations of power, status, or authority. It is always necessarily,
as Juhani Pallasmaa has written on many occasions, ‘a choreography of life’.
The two demographic waves are of course connected. The unprece-
dented increase in life expectancy in the first half of the 20th century was a
direct result of the reduction in levels of infant and child mortality brought
about by the campaigns of Keys and others. What they sowed, we now reap.
Old age was once venerated because it was rare; now, the elderly are some-
times regarded as surplus to requirements. Balancing the differing care
needs of the elderly, the infirm and those approaching the end of life, has
become an ethical and financial minefield. The borders between the care of
the elderly and ‘end of life’ care remain strictly demarcated – attracting dif-
ferential status and funding – even though both are clearly part of the same
‘continuum of need’, to use a phrase from the late Mary Warnock. This
makes no sense at all given that, as American physician, Atul Gawande,
suggests, ‘most of us will spend significant periods of our lives too reduced
and debilitated to live independently’.3
What Key’s book did for childhood, Philippe Ariès’ eschatological
history, The Hour of Our Death, has done for the ethics of end-of-life care.
Ahead of his time, Ariès predicted ‘the triumph of medicalization’ as soci-
eties succumbed to the idea that death could be postponed – if not finally
resisted – by advances in medication and prosthetic technology (as Silicon
Valley transhumanists now hope). Governed by a prerogative to preserve

-- 17 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	3
life, medical professionals understandably supported such advances, not
anticipating that other ethical dilemmas would arise once death was per-
ceived as a system failure rather than as an existential inevitability. ‘Mod-
ern society tries to deconstruct death by turning it into a series of medical
battles’ was philosopher Zygmunt Bauman’s opinion.4 Ending one’s days in
hospital may offer reassurance and clinical expertise, but the conditions are
frequently demeaning and occasionally brutal. For increasing numbers of
elderly patients suffering with dementia – and perhaps even more for their
families – it is the ultimate nightmare.
Coming at both ends of life, childhood and old age share a common vul-
nerability: a degree of powerlessness, a lack of agency, and a dependency on
the goodwill, kindness, and love of others. In other words, they need care.
They also share a need for security and a sense of belonging, so they need a
home. All the travails and joys of the human condition, some might say, rest
on these two words. No writer has expressed this profound cartography
better than the story-teller John Berger:
Originally home meant the centre of the world – not in a geographical
but an ontological sense. Home was the centre of the world because it
was the place where a vertical line crossed with a horizontal one. The
vertical line was leading upwards to the sky and downwards to the
underworld. The horizontal line represented the traffic of the world,
all the possible roads leading across the earth to other places. Thus,
at home, one was nearest to the gods in the sky and to the dead in the
underworld. This nearness promised access to both. And at the same
time, one was at the starting point and, hopefully, the returning point
of all terrestrial journeys. 5
We have much to thank those pioneering designers, social reformers, phi-
losophers, and architects – a majority of them women it should be noted –
who created new possibilities for childhood at the beginning of the 20th
century. We can only hope that our century will witness similar ideals and
energies being devoted to the cause of the elderly – in much the same spirit
and with much the same flair and imagination.
*********
A duty of care
The first edition of this book, published in 2009, documented and celebrated
the rise of ‘the modern hospice movement’ in the UK, though case studies
were included from other parts of Europe. I approached the story initially
as a social historian, interested in the relationship between architecture and
public policy, believing that social relations are often structured and for-
malised by the buildings in which they are formed and maintained. This
characteristic of modern industrial and post-industrial societies provides
the psychological experience of ‘modernity’. We live, after all, in a material

-- 18 of 203 --

4 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
world, where buildings powerfully contribute to the shaping of emotions –
and these have real-world effects and consequences.
This is obvious in places where people have been confined and cared
for in sickness or approaching the end of life. The history of the architec-
ture of care – popularly understood to have begun, at least in Europe, with
the monastic hospices of the early middle ages – is now receiving increas-
ing attention. Studies such as Michel Foucault’s 1961 treatise on asylums,
‘Madness and Civilisation’, Erving Goffman’s book, ‘Asylums’, published in
the same year, and Thomas A. Markus’s ‘Buildings and Power’ published
in 1993 all form important milestones in this narrative. Since then, more
historical work has been done on asylums, hospitals, almshouses, and other
building types relating to public or communal care, a number of which are
detailed in the bibliography, though the architectural study of 20th-century
hospices and care homes – and their typologies – is still in its infancy. As a
Dutch researcher Bernike Pasveer suggested in a recent collection of essays
on care in later life: ‘There is very little literature about Dutch care archi-
tecture, and I have not been able to find anything written on the hard-
ware of Dutch hospices, let alone the affordances and moralities of that
hardware’. 6 The fact that this statement is made in the Netherlands, where
designing for the elderly is further advanced, confirms the current need for
more international sharing of good practice.
In this new edition, the concept of what we mean by the word ‘care’ is
given greater prominence, the residential care sector having suffered an
unprecedented crisis during the recent – and for the sector, catastrophic –
Covid pandemic, from which it has still to recover, hopefully with lessons
learned. In origin, the word care derives from the Middle English word of
the same name, care, to denote sorrow, grief, worry, responsibility, and con-
cern but also to the act of paying attention to, looking after, and mainte-
nance. It is often associated with the Christian tradition of caritas, charity,
a concern for the suffering of others and a wish to alleviate that suffering.
As the sector of age-friendly housing, almshouses, hospices, and other care
places continues to diversify, the word care needs to be more sensitively han-
dled: it is not a badge or ‘skillset’, as one of my interviewees insisted, it is a
relationship or it is nothing at all. Even so, as Lisa Baraitser has emphasised
in her seminal book on the relationship between the experience of time in
conditions of care, Enduring Time, ‘care ethics never seems to fully shake off
its entanglement with the feminine’.7 It remains the case that most care staff,
whether in hospitals, hospices, and residential care homes, are women, and
while they are still under-represented in the higher reaches of care manage-
ment, women have today made headway in many architectural practices in
recent years, if not in the ‘design and build’ sector to the same extent as yet.
What we mean by care is the subject of Juliet Davis’s highly original and
thought-provoking study, The Caring City: Ethic of Urban Design, published in
2022. Davis follows Joan Tronto and Berenice Fisher, adopting as her key-
note definition their assertion that:
Care is a species activity that includes everything we do to maintain,
continue and repair our “world” so that we can live in it as well as

-- 19 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	5
possible. That world includes our bodies, our selves, and our environ-
ment, all of which we interweave in a complex, life-sustaining web. 8
Davis observes that as care, especially for the ageing population, is relocated
to the private sector, society as a whole is no longer party to the respon-
sibility which normally accrues to individuals, families, or communities,
to safeguard the interests of elders, so attenuated have the relationships
become. This means that if care is a ‘moral orientation towards the future’,
then that orientation is broken, and society becomes to a degree futureless
in terms of its communal responsibilities and attachments.
While much of the original text of Modern Hospice Design remains, it has
been updated and enlarged to cover developments in ageing and end-of-
life care, notably with the continuing development of hospice design and
emerging new forms and settings for palliative care. As Michelle Knox has
observed, the concept of institutional palliative care is still principally a
feature of Western life and culture, noting that the World Health Organi-
zation has claimed that ‘only 14% of those in need receive it’.9 So, while the
original starting place for the book was what was happening in the UK with
regard to palliative care provision – and to some extent remains so – I have
endeavoured to include initiatives and care programmes from other parts
of the world where possible. To this end, I would point to studies such as
Innovations in Hospice Architecture by Stephen Verderber & Ben J. Refuerzo –
with its examples of hospices across the world – as complementing this new
edition. I am similarly indebted to the richly illustrated book by Charles
Jencks and Edwin Heathcote, The Architecture of Hope: Maggie’s Cancer Caring
Centres, and to Age-Friendly Housing by Julia Park and Jeremy Porteus. The
authors of all of these have been generous with their time and knowledge,
for which I am grateful.
One of the main reasons for the renewed interest in the architecture of
later-life care is that, historically, in Pasveer’s words, ‘health care has only
recently become a publicly acknowledged responsibility of welfare soci-
eties’.10 The paradox is that as the state has taken on this responsibility
in many parts of the world, it has at the same time sought to increase pri-
vate sector involvement, an issue that continues to complicate the degree to
which ‘public’ health is to any degree public any more. The English histo-
rian Peter Hennessy returns to this feature of democratic societies of the
20th and 21st centuries, with the title of his post-pandemic manifesto: A
Duty of Care: Britain Before and After Covid.11 The social democratic ideal of the
state offering its citizens shelter and care ‘from the cradle to the grave’ may
have been found wanting in some regards, yet throughout the pandemic, it
proved to be effective state planning and delivery that saved the day. And as
inequalities in life chances have widened in many societies – to increasingly
destabilising effect as a result of deregulation and privatisation – there is
renewed interest in effective public governance and oversight in health and
welfare to underpin the democratic covenant.
There is much to deal with. Today, over 65s make up 18% of the
UK population, but by 2046, this figure will be 25%. Few anticipated
how improvements in health and welfare would result in extending life

-- 20 of 203 --

6 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
expectancy so rapidly. The UK Office for National Statistics has pre-
dicted that UK females will have an at-birth life expectancy of a hundred
years by 2057.12 Across Europe, half of the population is now over 50, and
population growth is highest in the over-60s age group.13 Many will be
in relatively good health, but the demands on the health services of older
people increase exponentially with each additional year of life, with the
over-85s taking up a quarter of National Health Service (NHS) beds in
the UK.14
By 2040, the population of those of 85 in England will number 2.7 mil-
lion, and by 2025, it is estimated that there will be over one million people
in the UK suffering from dementia.15 As we grow older the longer the time
spent in a condition of poor health: ‘British males can now expect to live
16.5 years in poor health, females a staggering 19 years’.16 Yet, as the num-
bers grow, capacity is in decline. According to the UK King’s Fund report
in 2021, the total number of NHS beds in England has halved over the past
30 years, from 299,000 in 1987/1988 to 141,000 in 2019/2020.17 Even so, a
2015 UK Ombudsman report, Dying without Dignity noted that: ‘The expe-
rience of people who are dying and their loved ones of the care provided
by the NHS is a recurring theme in complaints’.18 Of course, many people
will live to old age without the need for direct personal care – but to do this
their chances of living independently will be greatly improved by living in
homes designed with the ageing process in mind. Such people could well
prefer to move from their present homes perhaps if there were attractive
alternatives, but in the UK at least there is a dearth of ‘age-friendly’ housing
to supply this need. A study in Sheffield estimated that by 2034 there will
be ‘a shortfall of around 5000–8000 purpose-built retirement dwellings in
Sheffield alone’.19
The issue of how to care for people in infirmity in old age is now a major
public policy concern across the world. And while design quality is not a
quick-fix solution to this challenge, it can be the element that makes the
difference between despair and delight, as the case studies seek to demon-
strate. Two trends are clear. There is growing disillusionment with insti-
tutionalised care homes (‘God’s waiting rooms’), and this is producing a
political pushback towards ‘ageing in place’ programmes, where people are
cared for at home as long as possible. The rationale for home-based care is
as much financial as ethical, given the cost to the public purse of residential
care. In the UK, local authorities currently pay on average £700 per week
for a care home place, £890 per week for a nursing home place, and the
cost to the NHS for an elderly patient to occupy a bed in a general hospital
(where many old people spend their last months) is in the region of £2800
per week. 20
The high levels of fatalities caused by the pandemic amongst those in
residential care drew attention to the high staff turnover and poor mainte-
nance regimes in many such homes today. This is the contemporary horror
Nicci Gerrard describes in her book, What Dementia Teaches Us About Love.
Soon, there will be one million people suffering from dementia in the UK,
in what Gerrard says is now known as ‘the disease of the century’.

-- 21 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	7
On being at home
Communal responsibilities will continue to tussle with individual rights in
contemporary social and palliative care, and it is in the ethical and moral
space between the two that solutions are invariably negotiated and found.
Nowhere is this more evident than in the publicly funded care sector,
where a nursing home in the Netherlands has been described by one Dutch
researcher as ‘a unique, hybrid space in which state interests and intimate
activities are closely entwined’. 21 This ‘hybrid space’ is likely to continue to
expand in societies across the world, requiring careful and sensitive under-
standing if it is not to become a battlefield rather than a meeting place on
common ground. If poor end of life care is a major cause of complaint in the
case of mainstream hospital provision, then this has to be counter-balanced
by the loving attention provided in many other care settings, where feelings
of profound gratitude are often expressed by the families of those who were
looked after in their final days.
At the centre of this discussion is the idea, and the ideal, that what is
most needed at the end of life is a sense of being at home. Pasveer and
others argue that the re-location of care away from the domestic setting
to an institutional setting required that the connotations of ‘homeliness’
were carried with it. This has proved almost impossible with regard to
hospitals, but it has become something of the watchword for the residen-
tial care home sector. In the first instance, this was achieved simply with
the re-use of the word ‘home’, as in nursing homes, care homes, and old
people’s homes.
There was a relatively simple demographic reason for this transition from
private domestic care at the end of life to public care: until the last century,
death came to most people at any age and without notice. It is only in the
past 100 years or so that it has been possible to predict with some degree of
certainty when and where people are going to die, and their last years man-
aged medically to well-rehearsed and foreseeable schedules and timetables,
increasingly in collective forms of care. The Ombudsman report, Dying
without Dignity, cited earlier, noted that of the half a million people who
died in England each year, three-quarters of those deaths were expected. 22
Today, death rarely arrives ‘out of the blue’ but is a slow, gradual diminution
of energies and faculties, either borne stoically or with increased anxiety
and frustration.
Yet, even as the tide of institutionalisation sweeps on, there are
counter-currents. ‘Trends in the location of death have come almost full
circle’, according to one recent commentator, ‘from home at first, to hospi-
tals as medicine advanced, to modern hospice spaces, and now back to home
again’. 23 Nitin Ahuja, a medic and writer sees a dramatic oscillation in the
palliative care sector today, between the poles of homeliness and architec-
tural high drama, the domestic and the institutional, observing that:
Early models of hospice rooted in a traditional understanding of home,
adopted domestic ideals of comfort and familiarity. More recently,

-- 22 of 203 --

8 	A N E W W O R L D O F PA L L I AT I V E A N D S O C I A L C A R E
we’ve seen spaces that employ dramatic design elements to facilitate a
more symbolic engagement with end of life. 24
In this shift backwards and forwards, design has come to play a key role in
shaping people’s expectations of later and end-of-life care. Ahuja goes on to
suggest that design strategies in future need to be much more imaginative
and bespoke, in preference to adopting a formulaic, bland model of end-
of-life architecture across the world in enfeebled simulations of Willem
Dudok, Frank Lloyd Wright, or standardised ‘design and build’ templates
for residential care.
In this regard, the description by the late Charles Jencks – who along
with his wife, Maggie Keswick Jencks was a prime mover behind the devel-
opment of Maggie’s Cancer Care Centres – was far-seeing:
If one focuses on the variety of functions then the typical Maggie’s
Centre can be seen as a kind of non-type. It is like a house which is
not a home, a collective hospital which is not an institution, a church
which is not religious, and an art gallery which is not a museum. At
least four different building types are combined in this hybrid, and the
amalgam makes them more effective in carrying forward their work. 25
Thus for Jencks, Maggie’s Centres, like hospices and other hybrid forms
of care and wellbeing, are an ‘emergent building type that is not quite a
museum, church, hospital or home but has aspects of each’. 26 Yet, as Ahuja
has commented, in this definition ‘again we see the tendency of advocates
(of hospice design) to describe palliative space primarily in terms of what
it is not’. 27 This reminds one of the debate about definitions of positive and
negative freedom: that the least harmful forms of freedom come from leav-
ing individuals alone to chart their own destiny, rather than defining what
the good life might be and seeking to mould them in that image. Neither
option is without its benefits nor its drawbacks.
The prevailing 20th-century transition of care away from the domestic
home to the institutional home required health service providers to think
more critically about how the conditions of ‘homeliness’ were constituted
and how they might be replicated away from the domestic setting. As
anthropologists, Dianne Willcocks, Sheila Peace, and Leonie Kellaher have
written: ‘There has been an illegitimate transfer of domestic nomenclature
to the institutional setting without a full appreciation of what is required to
construct an authentic home environment’. 28
This issue is particularly acute when it comes to providing secure envi-
ronments for those suffering with dementia. At its minimum, this can
simply be achieved by decorating a bedroom or living area with famil-
iar objects from the patient’s own home: photographs, ornaments, radios,
and clocks. But at its most extreme, this can take the form of constructing
wholly simulated neighbourhood environments based on typically local
or regional townscape simulations, as is the case at De Hogeweyk near
Amsterdam, one of the case studies in Age-Friendly Housing. 29 De Hogeweyk
is a self-contained small urban settlement for people with dementia, where

-- 23 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	9
low-rise brick buildings flank a boulevard and a variety of public
spaces and gardens, including a town square with a fountain. There
is a supermarket, café/restaurant and theatre, and though the village
is gated to keep residents safe, some facilities are open to the public.30
The whole issue of artificially simulated environments is the subject of
a persuasive essay by philosopher Larissa MacFarquhar on the ethics of
such ‘comforting fictions’ used to trick patients suffering from dementia
into a state of acceptance of their custodial condition, formerly achieved in
some places by excessive medication or in earlier times by enforced con-
finement.31 Interestingly, MacFarquhar comes down finally in favour of
De Hogeweyk, it being a large enough controlled outdoor environment to
attract everyday use from outsiders coming to visit the coffee shop or bring
their children to sit and watch the fountains or run around the traffic-free
streets. In this way, it remains connected to the life of the neighbourhood
and the community.
Stephen Verderber and Ben J. Refuerzo also take up this issue in their
new edition of Innovations in Hospice Architecture, citing Alfredo Botello’s
observation that:
Ordinariness is … the hallmark of successful hospice design. A home
for the dying is not the place for avantgarde experimentation, but,
rather, familiarity and unobtrusiveness. It helps people feel they’re
part of the neighbourhood and the neighbourhood feels that the people
living there are living the same lives that they are.32
This advocacy of ‘ordinariness’ is challenged or gainsaid by the many
uplifting and imaginative exercises in designing for older people which are
detailed in the following chapters, and in which the traditional architec-
tural verities – commodity, firmness, and delight – are as apparent as ever,
even though the imaginative quality of the design transcends the bounda-
ries of ordinariness to produce what Maggie Keswick Jencks once defined
as ‘a building that rises to the occasion’.
It is now accepted that those who use buildings should have a say in judg-
ing how they ‘work’ or ‘fail to work’ in terms of the outcomes prescribed in
the client brief. User experience is a key element in POE (post-occupancy
evaluation), never more necessary than in judging developments in resi-
dential care, though it applies to everything else in the built environment
and the social estate. The public has a clear investment in the quality of the
architecture and planning of towns or cities, including the public aspect of
the individual buildings that form them, though this may be particularly
the case with residential care, as the Covid pandemic revealed.
One of the most intentional of arts, architecture is also one of the most
social, responsible for creating the mise-en-scêne for many of the signifi-
cant rituals of public and private life. Too often, we think of architecture
as a largely visual art, as we do with design, but both are quintessentially
problem-solving disciplines. As Julia Park and Jeremy Porteous write in
Age-Friendly Housing, that ‘while there is no single housing solution, but

-- 24 of 203 --

10 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
many, good contemporary design is not just legitimate, it is part of the
answer’.33 In updating this edition, it has been gratifying to see so much
imagination and care still being deployed in the ‘housing with care’ sector,
not always but often coming from the smaller, more independent providers,
especially charitable trusts.
If, as anthropologist Mary Douglas has argued, the sense of homeliness
arises from bringing space under control, it also involves bringing the expe-
rience of time under control too. The difference between institutional time
and domestic time is that the former is highly structured and regulated,
whereas domestic time is almost always subject to negotiation. One hospice
director told me that if a patient wanted smoked salmon and champagne
at three o’clock in the morning, why not? She was adamant that hospices
should be run to a timetable required by the patients’ needs, not those of
the staff.
Today, most hospices, Maggie’s Centres and almshouses, avoid the
descriptor home, while still insisting on notions of ‘homeliness’ in design
briefs and care programmes. In Spain, Daniel López Gómez and colleagues
at the Universitat Oberta de Catalunya have been studying co-housing pro-
jects for older people, preferring to use the term ‘havens’ to describe these
small urban or rural cooperative settlements. With its recognisable etymo-
logical origins in the notion of a safe harbour or retreat, the word success-
fully embodies the ideal of shelter and safety for all who enter.34
Elsewhere, in the psychogeriatric ward of a Dutch nursing home, the
units of small-scale living accommodation within the main complex are
composed of modules in which six residents live in individual rooms but
with a shared common room and open-plan kitchen. In the words of one
observer, the ‘the layout of the nursing home has been oriented towards the
idea of a household’.35 Atul Gawande found the same system in the Boston
suburbs, where the normal ‘sixty people to a floor in shared rooms along
endless hospital corridors’ had been divided into household pods with a
common living area, dining room, kitchen, and activity room.36 As with
all systems of classification, the planning system struggles to keep up with
changing needs and aspirations in the social and demographic sphere. Park
and Porteus cite Sam Clark’s Retirement Living Explained, which argues that:
‘Planning Use Classes are being tested by contemporary developments that
blur traditional divides between dwellings and care environments, housing
and health, public and private’.37 It is in the imaginative melding of these
different historic typologies of buildings, whether designed for public care,
medical treatment, independent living, private space, and communal sup-
port, that the next section itemises in four modern genres.
*********
An architecture that rises to the occasion
In the pages that follow, I have widened the scope beyond the first edition’s
almost exclusive focus on the modern hospice, to cross-refer to Maggie’s

-- 25 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	11
Centres, almshouses, a new generation of extra-care homes and related ini-
tiatives such as retirement villages. Although each of these models of res-
idential care and ‘age-friendly housing’ responds to specific and different
needs, all share some of the characteristics of therapeutic communities to
a degree. They too have to respond to increasing anxieties about ageing
experienced by their patients as well as the families of those patients.
This sets these places apart from the performative, highly medicalised
world of mainstream hospital in-patient treatment, where the ambition is
curative rather than palliative, and where the physical body is the subject
of intense activity (rather than ease of mind in the face of life’s finality).
One day, public health policy will endeavour to treat both holistically, but
in present times, social/palliative care and medical care remain distinctive
discourses and domains. Chapter 7 is based on case studies of exemplary
new developments in each sector: the new St Wilfrid’s Hospice in East-
bourne; Maggie’s Southampton; Appleby Blue Almshouse, Southwark; and
Lark Hill Retirement Village in Nottingham.
Hospices
When Cicely Saunders first conceived of St Christopher’s Hospice in Syd-
enham, which opened in 1967, it was because she had seen a close friend
approaching death in a state of misery, producing one of those ‘something
must be done’ moments. That person had left Saunders a small bequest in
the hope that she would establish a place of greater care, where his life’s
meaning was to ‘be a small window in your home’. Similarly, when Maggie
Keswick Jencks received confirmation of terminal cancer in 1993, it wasn’t
until she had suffered the days and months of waiting in gloomy, institu-
tional surroundings that, with her husband Charles Jencks, she resolved to
develop a new model for patient support and care.
After continuing and steady growth following the advent of St Christo-
pher’s in 1966, the number of hospices in the UK stabilised during the first
decade of the 21st century and remains around 240, each serving a distinc-
tive catchment area, served by fund-raising charity shops and networks of
supporters and volunteers. The main growth area has been an increase in
the number of children’s hospices in recent years. The move into outreach
and ‘hospice at home’ programmes, along with the development of ever-
extensive programmes of day-patient activities and facilities described in
the first edition continued until the first Covid pandemic lockdown in
March 2020, from which it is only now beginning to recover. In so many
ways, the Covid pandemic changed everything. I had to come to terms with
this ‘black swan event’ in revising and enlarging this edition, and like others
remain unsure whether things will return to the same as they were before,
or whether the changes are irrevocable.
At a stroke, in most hospices, all voluntary support was curtailed, out-
patient services were suspended, and home visits and consultations went
online. The serenity of the hospice was displaced by a sudden sense of
urgency. Hospices struggled to cope with a wave of transfers from hospitals

-- 26 of 203 --

12 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
and care homes of patients dying of Covid and needing support in their
last days. The serene calm of the hospice experience was displaced by an
intensive process of supporting extremely ill patients, and managing and
processing their deaths, while arranging funerals and bereavement sup-
port. All this compromised many of their long-standing and carefully nur-
tured inter-personal skills and relationships. Staff, patients, and families of
patients, as well as volunteers, struggled to keep some kind of control of the
palliative care process, though no fault of their own. At St Christopher’s,
volunteers turned to various community aid programmes in the area, deliv-
ering food, medicines, and shopping to the most vulnerable, including hos-
pice patients now trapped at home.
Heather Richardson, then CEO of St Christopher’s, described the situ-
ation as follows:
It is clear that hospice care looks very different post-pandemic. Despite
sustained demands on hospitals for inpatient care, many hospices note
that their inpatient beds aren’t filling up as they have done previously
and reasons for that are unclear. Referral patterns to hospices changed
significantly during the different waves of COVID-19 and it would
seem that these need redrawing if hospice in-patient units are to serve
their vital role in the health system.
That said, demand for home-based care, including support for res-
idents in care homes, is on the increase. The needs of those living and
dying in care homes appears increasingly complex, not least as a result
of the isolation they experienced during lockdown. Outpatient and day
care facilities, previously dominant facilities in many hospices, have
only recently seen people starting to return, as patients, carers or vol-
unteers. For many there is a fear of being in public places; perhaps for
Figure 1.1 Entrance to St Christopher’s Hospice, Sydenham, London, opened
in 1967.

-- 27 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	13
others simply a loss of habit and comfort of being in the company of
others?
Importantly, new needs that hospices could fill are starting to
become evident as a result of the pandemic. Individuals and families
continue to experience a sense of loss from deaths that were unantici-
pated, in which they could not always participate if in hospital, and for
whom ritual endings such as large funerals have been denied. They
have not had closure and as a result struggle in their bereavement.38
The idea of ‘living with Covid’, Richardson believes, remains replete with
problems, especially in the care sector. ‘The pandemic accelerated the tran-
sition of “care” from a relational undertaking to a wholly transactional one’,
she added. ‘Don’t go too near, don’t hug or touch, don’t return to the close
relationships and rituals of last farewells or the slow unfolding of bereave-
ment’. With such new social protocols, individuals are no longer ‘present’
to each other, only engaged in a formalised negotiation. With even less
interaction with everyday life, it followed that much of the culture inside
the more conventional care homes, however well managed, became that of
waiting, of running out of time as one nears the end. In Enduring Time, Lisa
Baraitser sets out the issue with sensitivity and depth, writing of that ‘time
without qualities’ that characterises too much time in care, in which forms
of waiting, delaying, enduring, repeating, preserving, and remaining, too
often add up to ‘experiences of time not passing’.39 Nevertheless, hospices
are rapidly adapting to the ‘new normal’, and those I visited post-Covid
have been quick to rise to the new circumstances.
Maggie’s centres
The continued expansion of the Maggie’s Cancer Care Centres building
programme – still principally in the UK – represents another significant
development in the care of the seriously ill, offering those facing a trau-
matic diagnosis somewhere to find respite and support in a safe place shared
with others facing similar challenges. The Maggie’s programme has gone
from strength to strength, with 27 Maggie’s Centres now built or in devel-
opment in the UK, and three abroad. Dame Laura Lee, the long-standing
Chief Executive of Maggie’s Centres, says the aim is to complete 60 centres
in the UK, providing equitable coverage of England, Scotland, and Wales.
A distinctive feature of the Maggie’s Centres is that almost without
exception, the site location is pre-ordained. They are built within the
precincts of a large hospital with an established oncology department.
Not only do the architects commissioned usually have to work within a
restricted footprint, they also have to create their own constructed land-
scaped curtilage to establish the distinctiveness of the centre within a
hard-pressed institutional environment – car-parking areas, old and new
out-buildings, and public pathway systems. Lee finds this challenge can be
productive, requiring architects to take a genuinely imaginative approach
in responding to these restrictions. Each new Maggie’s Centre also has to

-- 28 of 203 --

14 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
create a strong working relationship with the larger institution – spatially,
aesthetically, and therapeutically. The case study in Chapter 7 is based on
the new Maggie’s Southampton by architect Amanda Levete of AL_A, with
a landscape designed by Sarah Price Landscapes, which opened in 2021.
Care homes & almshouses
The global pandemic caused by the Covid-19 virus hit those countries hard-
est where the care of the elderly had been institutionalised, often resulting
in far higher death rates than in countries or cultures where the elderly and
vulnerable remained cared for at home. This issue of social separation is
now widespread. In the Netherlands, Pasveer has written that:
Currently, Dutch hospice care is largely institutionalised, architec-
tured and organised, spatially as well as normatively, in ways that
sequester death and dying from other forms of life and livelihood, no
matter whether they are located in city centres or in more rural areas.40
The pandemic highlighted the degree to which the separation and confine-
ment of the elderly away from everyday life had exacerbated the spread of
the virus within the place of care. As a result, public attention was drawn to
the exclusionary design of many care homes, both with regard to location
and their internal spatial organisation and management.
The mid-to-late 20th-century development of ‘old people’s homes’,
‘nursing homes’, and ‘care homes’ in the UK is now looked at with wide-
spread scepticism, if not a sense of guilt. Too many older people suffered,
in the words of the sociologist David Sudnow, a ‘social death’ long before
their physical demise.41 Stories of over-regimentation, or even maltreat-
ment, became associated with social and spatial confinement and isolation.
In the UK, a 2014 Commission on Residential Care organised by the think-
tank Demos reported that: ‘The picture from the research was clear – the
public broadly see residential care homes as places of illness and frailty,
where boredom and loneliness are pervasive, and where you would only go
as a last resort’.42
As the Covid pandemic retreats, it leaves the residential care home sector
in a parlous condition in the UK. At the outbreak of the pandemic in 2020,
there were 465,000 care home beds in England, 55,000 fewer than in 2000,
but with an estimated need for 110,000 additional beds by 2030. According
to a 2020 survey, 78% of existing care home beds are ‘in properties not orig-
inally built with care use in mind or are more than 20 years old’.43 The pat-
tern of provision for ‘age-friendly housing’ and residential care in its many
iterations is fragmenting, even as more positive initiatives come forward.
Not only has care transitioned from the domestic to the public sphere,
but it is in the process – more advanced in some countries than others – of
moving from the public sector to the private sector, to the extent in many
cases of disappearing into a complex tangle of investment arrangements,
some of which have their ultimate financial home offshore and beyond

-- 29 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	15
regulation. There is now a substantial disconnect between financial owner-
ship, corporate management, and workplace management and the philoso-
phy of care as practiced on a daily basis. This does not make for an ethos of
dedicated care in the long term, and is why in the UK there is now a serious
debate about bringing the whole social care sector within the remit of the
National Health Service regulatory umbrella.
The arguments for this move are persuasive. However, one principal res-
ervation is that if national standards across all areas prevailed – including
design and accountability – this might well act as a disincentive to local
variety, innovation, and freedom to experiment, the value of which has
been shown time and again by the imaginative work of the hospice move-
ment, Maggie’s and the not-for-profit sector.
The new almshouse movement
Innovation has emerged in one traditional form of provision. In Decem-
ber 2020, the Royal Institute of Chartered Surveyors (RICS) published a
report, Almshouses: a model of community housing for an ageing population, renew-
ing interest in the oldest model of collective care for the elderly, which
amongst other things, historically developed a distinctive architectural
style or template that has lasted the course of time. As the report, written
by Jenny Pannell and Dr Alison Pooley of Anglia Ruskin University, noted:
Almshouses have existed in England for over a thousand years, and
new almshouses are still being built. Almshouse charities provide
social housing for over 36,000 people in around 30,000 dwellings. Most
almshouse residents are older people (aged 50/60+ years), yet built
environment and housing professionals, politicians and others con-
cerned with the provision of housing for older people are more famil-
iar with other social housing providers than with almshouse charities.
In a few localities, large almshouse charities are still significant providers
of housing for older people in the UK. In the north of England, Durham
Aged Mineworkers Homes (DAMHA) is the largest almshouse charity with
over 1,700 homes. Pickering and Ferens Homes is another large almshouse
charity with over 1,350 homes in Hull and the East Riding of Yorkshire. It is
estimated that there are at least 1600 almshouse charities still operating in
the UK, enabling local people to maintain links with neighbourhood, fam-
ily, and friends. While the much-loved template of the charitable almshouse
across northern Europe – a repeated set of connected ‘domestic collegiate’
frontages surrounding a courtyard or fronted by elaborate gardens – is now
being re-interpreted to make it more porous to the surrounding streetscape,
its resilient and long history gives it a social weight that adds significantly
to its presence within the modern townscape. The case study in Chapter 7
features Appleby Blue, an almshouse in Bermondsey, south London, funded
by the United St Saviour’s Charity, designed by architects Witherford Wat-
son Mann, due for completion in 2023. The project title was ‘An Almshouse

-- 30 of 203 --

16 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
for the 21st Century’, and to declare an interest, I advised on the historical
tradition of almshouse architecture in the early stages of scoping out the
final design brief.
Retirement villages
Modelled on the North American concept of privately funded and managed
retirement complexes, often sited in the sunnier climes of places such as
Florida, the idea of retirement villages is belatedly coming to Europe. Until
recently aimed at well-off couples who have down-sized from private fam-
ily homes, such developments have until now been designed to appeal to
those who seek a degree of security and sociability with like-minded oth-
ers attracted by highly developed ‘lifestyle’ concepts – and usually housed
within gated or enclosed developments rather than in mixed urban or rural
neighbourhoods.
There was an architectural precedent for this model in the ‘full-service
flats’ of the 1930s in the UK, the subject of a detailed study by historian
Genevieve Bovee. Such developments were aimed at rich, childless indi-
viduals and couples, for whom communal facilities such as restaurants,
swimming pools, cocktail bars, and laundry services, were attractive. As
Bovee writes, ‘In a full-service building the ideal home also came with the
ready-made community that surrounded a suburban house; it was a vil-
lage in the city or a town in a single building’.44 Perhaps the most famous
(after the 1934 Isokon building) 45 of these was Dolphin Square in Pimlico,
London, which opened in 1937, with 1236 flats, a restaurant with a dance
floor, swimming pool, library, games room, beauty parlour, theatre booking
office, travel bureau, pharmacy, wine shop, and shoe repairer. The demise of
the model came after the war, largely for changing social and demographic
reasons, and for economic reasons, the communal facilities were abandoned
and converted into residential accommodation in order to generate income.
Yet, the model of apartment living with common services remains
an architectural prototype, more popular in northern Europe where
co-housing projects have been common for decades. One impressive not-
for-profit organisation now operating in this sector in the UK, with a strong
social remit, is The ExtraCare Charitable Trust, established in 1988. The
Trust owns and manages 16 retirement villages and four housing schemes,
mostly in the Midlands and the North of England. They provide more than
4,200 homes, 40% for private sale, 40% for shared ownership, and 20% for
rent.46 However, these proportions are flexible and may change according
to local circumstances, especially in areas of social deprivation. The work
of the Trust is to continue to develop and support new villages based on an
‘extra-care’ model, clearly more than any other private housing development
scheme in the later-life market. Again, the emphasis is on supporting an
open house model, accessible to the wider community, and thus integrated
into the life of the area, in which most residents previously lived and remain
‘entangled’. Lark Hill ExtraCare Village provides a case study in Chapter 7.

-- 31 of 203 --

a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	17
The ExtraCare Trust village ethos is focused on remaining active and
sociable. Each village has a ‘Wellbeing Adviser’ to support residents with
their physical and mental health and offers gyms, sports rooms, clubs and
societies, bars, and a bistro or restaurant. Equally importantly, defining
itself as ‘a social hub for the whole area’, visitors and members from the
nearby community are encouraged to share in the facilities. Furthermore,
the Trust ‘aims to ensure that each village is affordable for every applicant
whatever their financial circumstances’. As residents age, the Trust claims
that ‘we can support significant assessed care needs, including dementia for
nearly all of our residents, and so most will have a home for life’. Thus, the
Trust provides a model combining some of the best elements of ‘lifetime
homes’ and ‘ageing in place’.
The ExtraCare ‘model’ has been the subject of research commissioned by
Aston University and Lancaster University, measuring the degree to which
village life is producing the ‘wellbeing’ outcomes originally established.
That study has found that NHS costs attributed to residents were cut by
over 38% in a year, that General Practice (GP) appointments fell by 46%,
that 75% of residents increased their exercise levels once settled in, and
85% said that they ‘never or hardly ever felt lonely’. Worth noting is that the
ExtraCare Trust grew out of the Coventry Churches Housing Association,
and so, like the hospice movement, has its origins as an exercise in faith and
community.47
Co-housing in Europe
Elsewhere in Europe, co-housing projects for older people are now well
established. As Stig Hessellund writes in the report, Cohousing Communities
for Seniors, published by the Danish social charity, Realdania in 2020:
In 1987, Denmark’s first senior cohousing community was established
in Copenhagen. Since then, a strong culture and tradition for senior
cohousing communities have evolved, and several hundred communi-
ties have materialised all over the country. What perhaps distinguishes
the Danish senior cohousing tradition is that it is deeply rooted in the
social housing sector. To this day, many of the Danish senior hous-
ing communities reside in social housing accessible to tenants from all
walks of life.48
This report describes in detail ten new and very different co-housing
schemes now planned or already operational across Denmark, partly guided
by Realdania’s support and advocacy. Some are wholly private develop-
ments, others mixed tenure, and others publicly funded as social housing
projects. The report cites a survey which claimed that 80,000 Danes have
expressed an interest in joining co-housing projects (often because they are
seeking to downsize from their existing homes), but at the same time, there
were just 6,900 co-housing opportunities available. Yet, once living in such

-- 32 of 203 --

18 	a n e w w o r L d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e
schemes, 93% of residents reported an improvement in the quality of their
lives.
Although catering in very different parts of Denmark to a cross-section
of Over-50s interested in co-housing, the report suggests that the common
factors which got most co-housing projects off the ground were:
 	A period of consultation and engagement with future residents as to
what values were shared regarding the way they wanted to live.
 	A common interest in environmental sustainability.
 	Opportunities to continue working into later age, with shared facilities
and amenities to support entrepreneurship.
 	A common interest in food quality, in food-growing as part of the com-
munity’s functions, and in a social life that included the sharing of food
as part of what they wanted to call ‘everyday community’.
 	That the common spaces of co-housing communities were everyday
spaces, not a separate designated common area, whether indoors or
outdoors.
Recording how frequently people were choosing to downsize to smaller
spaces, the report says that:
In that sense, a senior cohousing community provides the opportunity
to live small and big at the same time. This is especially true if the com-
mon rooms and facilities are designed in a way, so that they replace the
functions that were available in your previous, private home. This also
means that the common areas should not only be seen as places where
you actively spend time with other people in the community but also
as places that you naturally use for your own everyday activities.
According to Spanish researcher Daniel López Gómez and his colleagues,
‘Since 2000, at least ten housing developments promoted for and by
senior-led cooperatives have been constructed in Spain’.49 Growing dis-
illusionment with both public and private residential care had led to an
advertising campaign ‘Si me llevas a una residencia te desheredo’, or in
English, ‘If you take me to a nursing home I will disinherit you’. The trend
towards older people coming together, designing and funding retirement
villages has taken a variety of forms, religious, political, and economic. The
researchers distinguish between havens (‘where older people feel safe and
preserve their identity and autonomy’) and heavens (‘places where people
join together in a collective project with the aim of transforming their envi-
ronment and themselves as they age’).
In their study of two such settlements, re-named for privacy purposes
as El Hogar and La Comunidad, they compare the former – a cooperative
mainly made up of older women with strong religious and political views –
with the latter, a radical group of mostly couples with a strong commit-
ment to more collective living. The first they characterise as a haven, a safe
and welcoming place where individuals continue to live their private lives
but with some shared facilities. By contrast, La Comunidad emphasises

-- 33 of 203 --

a n e w w o rL d o f Pa L L i at i V e a n d s o C i a L C a r e 	19
the communal spirit of the settlement, where shared services and spaces
encourage greater mutual aid and communal identity, characterised as a
heaven. Yet, such a difference of starting points and ambitions have over
time, according to Gómez et al., met somewhere in the middle, with El
Hogar increasingly needing to emphasise its communitarian qualities in
order to attract new members, and La Comunidad having to adapt some of
its communal buildings in order to provide more specialist care to individ-
ual members as they grow increasingly frail.
Nevertheless, such co-housing projects have attracted criticism from
others facing similar problems – with regard to finding places of quality
care and support in later life – who argue that these smaller communi-
tarian experiments fail to have any impact on the quality of mainstream,
‘universalist’ public care provision in old age. This discussion is now to be
had everywhere and not just with regard to palliative and social care. We
need these experimental, independent initiatives, such as hospices, Mag-
gie’s Centres, not-for-profit retirement villages, and almshouses, to explore
the difference between how things are and how they might be.
Experimentalism does not come easy to public, universal provision,
which nevertheless has done magnificent work in the 20th century to
improve the lives of hundreds of millions of people across the world through
public housing, healthcare, education, transport, and personal safety. There
remains much to work to, yet there are many inspiring projects to learn
from, if the 21st century is truly to become ‘the century of the older person’.
