This chapter discusses the growing concern with the conditions in which
many older people die at home or in hospital. It cites a number of medical
and architectural critics on the culture and design of traditional hospital
buildings and settings, particularly where death is diagnosed, announced,
and attended. There then follows a brief summary of the early days of the
‘modern hospice movement’, followed by an outline of the architectural and
design issues involved in thinking afresh about the hospice as a new build-
ing type. As ‘the right to a good death’ becomes a new social demand, and
the UK hospice movement inspires similar developments across the world,
opportunities for maximising the effects of thoughtful and sensitive design
are discussed and evaluated. As people live longer, their needs become
more complex, hence the development of a wider ‘spectrum of care’ across
public, private, and voluntary sectors.
Life and death on the public ward
Where people die and how they die is a subject that is still broached with some
difficulty. In developed countries, the majority of people now die in institu-
tions rather than at home. In the UK at the beginning of the 20th century
about 85% of people died in their homes, with workhouses accounting
for most other deaths. By the mid twentieth-century around 50% of
people died at home, and even in the early twenty-first century, acute
hospitals remained the most common place of death.1
In England and Wales in 2018, 47% of deaths occurred in hospitals, 23.5%
at home, 22% in care homes, and 5.7% in hospices. 2 In Ireland, around 67%
of deaths occur in acute and general hospitals, and in North America, the
figure is just 35%, with home deaths at 31%, care home deaths at 27%, and
‘other’ deaths at 7%.3
Of those in the UK who die in hospitals, such deaths mostly occur in
public wards, where there may be little professional expertise in the care of
the dying.4 At present, just under 6% of people in the UK die in hospices,
though many more would if they could we are told, and availability is still
A house at the end of life 	2

-- 37 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	23
regionally biased. 5 However, even what is classified as ‘home’ for the dying
is debatable, since for those 20% of the elderly population resident in care
homes, the room or ward they now inhabit is de facto their home (technically
their permanent residential address). While dying at home – literally the
familial home or other domestic setting – may be an expressed ideal for
many, as survey after survey appears to show, these attitudes can quickly
change once serious pain sets in, or when people experience good hospice
care on a temporary or respite basis. Sheila Payne, the UK’s first Professor
of Hospice Studies at Lancaster University, has said that
Dying at home is very scary. You might think you want to do it, and
you might want to support someone in your family who says they want
it, but in the midst of it actually happening you may feel you’d be better
off elsewhere. 6
Some have no choice in the matter. A small number of the elderly living on
their own will have outlived their families, or become geographically distant
or even estranged from them, and will die alone. In Britain, up to 60 people die
alone at home each week, leading one Member of Parliament to depict ‘a stark
picture of isolation, loneliness and, in many cases, impoverishment’.7 In such
cases, spending one’s last days in residential care may be preferable. Such has
been the growing political concern over the conditions in which many elderly
people die, whether at home or in a hospital, that an All-Party Parliamentary
Group on Dying Well was established in the UK, which began to reverse the
position described by one of Britain’s leading political commentators that:
…the appalling treatment of too many elderly people is the best-kept
secret in Britain. There is almost no political debate about how to keep
an ageing population from pain and penury, let alone how to provide
what the elderly need and deserve. 8
The very idea that parliament should discuss the concept of ‘dying well’
suggests that we are entering a new era, especially post-Covid, in which the
conditions in which people die moves towards the centre of social policy.
While this may be true in public discourse, both public and private provi-
sion have failed to keep pace, possibly daunted by the sheer numerical scale
of the problem as the older population continues to grow, while spending
more time in a state of poor health. Once considered a wholly religious
concern, today secular societies seek to respond to the bodily and spiritual
requirements of death as a matter of public ethics. Where social policy
leads, architects and builders shortly follow, as we shall see.
That the treatment of the terminally ill is still dealt with unsatisfactorily
is revealed by the fact that many complaints in the UK National Health
Service relate to the care of a patient who is dying. Hospital staff have a
harder time dealing with the dying than those working in other institutions
caring for the elderly, for an acceptance of death appears to go against their
historic raison d’etre which is to preserve life and return people to health.

-- 38 of 203 --

24 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
As such, mortality is regarded as a failure in the system, to be dealt with
quickly, and all traces of which to be quickly erased.
When you consider that in England people aged 65 and over constitute
16% of the population but occupy a disproportionate number of hospital
beds, then you realise the scale of the problem.9 It is apparent that, possibly
as a result of the sheer numbers of elderly people dying in hospitals, close
personal care at this crucial rite de passage is found wanting and inadequate
to the occasion. While the conditions of childbirth have improved steadily
and consistently in recent decades, the same cannot be said of conditions in
which people die.
For many older people, the experience of hospital still comes with a
history of popular memories and mythologies: the high-walled, black-
ened brick building on the hill (in England, often the former workhouse),
sometimes adjacent to the cemetery, though now embellished with a new
corporate notice-board; the labyrinthine hospital complex with buildings
of all periods and designs randomly scattered between service roads and
car parks impossible to navigate; the ominous – because windowless –
mortuary building set in its own forbidding asphalt hinterland, distin-
guished by the double doors and porte-cochère at the rear; the rubber and
plastic door entrances; the corridors partially obstructed by broken medical
equipment or old mattresses; the long corridors smelling of disinfectant;
harsh neon lighting; wards and individuals rooms which are over-heated
and under-ventilated; rooms in which candles and flowers are prohibited
for health and safety reasons; box rooms filled with computer equipment to
which people are called to be told confidentially that little further can be
done; the views from the dirty windows across to a roofline of ventilation
shafts and extractor fans; and so on. While no one hospital contains all these
baleful elements, many retain enough to sink the spirits of even the most
stoical.
Such conditions seem to support the view of French architect, Nils
Degrémont, who has provocatively suggested, ‘In our civilization, a death
outside the home can be likened to a death outside society – because, in
a hospital environment and despite the existence of palliative care units,
death is still something of which to be ashamed’.10
If it is thought that the conditions and attitudes described above are
now rare, belonging to another place and time, sadly while there was some
growth in the NHS budget in the first decade of this century, following the
global financial crisis of 2008, the UK has since registered a decade and a
half of public sector austerity. Here is the architectural correspondent of
The Financial Times, Edwin Heathcote, describing his experience in London
hospitals, having been diagnosed with cancer, an experience that many oth-
ers continue to have unfortunately:
I spent more of last year than I would have liked in hospitals. Once you
become ill and enter the system for treatment, a general assumption
seems to descend that your time doesn’t really matter anymore…
It is an extremely peculiar situation in which you feel suddenly
physically vulnerable at the exact time you are most traumatised. Days

-- 39 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	25
seem to go by in plastic stacking seats and sticky easy chairs, waiting
in claustrophobic booths, perched on the edge of benches and beds,
always waiting for blood tests, for nurses, for doctors, for results.
The bad news was given to me in a room the size of a Photo-me
booth, with my wife and myself leaning precariously against a day-bed
and a bunch of doctors lined up against the wall a foot away looking
morose.11
Now the novelist, Julian Barnes, describing his father’s death. It is taken
from a book written by Barnes about his own, deeply held, fear of dying:
He died a modern death, in hospital, without his family, attended in
his final minutes by a nurse, months – indeed, years – after medical
science had prolonged his life to a point where the terms on which it
was being offered were unimpressive.12
Finally, an account, by the writer, Alan Bennett, describing the aftermath of
his father’s death in hospital:
He died on the Saturday morning when I was already on the train
north, so when I saw his body on the Monday at Airedale Hospital, he
had been dead two days. The mortuary was somewhere at the back of
the hospital; not a facility I suppose they wanted to make a show of,
it was near the boiler room and the back doors of the kitchens. I was
put into a curtained room (called the viewing room) where Dad lay
under a terrible purple pall. There were two attendants, one of whom
pulled back the shroud. It was a shocking sight. His face had shrunk
and his teeth no longer fitted so that his mouth was set in a snarl, a look
about as uncharacteristic of him as I could ever have imagined. It was
the first time in his life (except it wasn’t in his life) that he can have
looked fierce. I noticed that the attendants were looking at me, more
interested in my reactions than in the corpse which to them must have
seemed commonplace.13
Such experiences – my experience of viewing my father’s body was exactly
the same as Bennett’s – support the view expressed by the Dutch archi-
tectural historian, Cor Wagenaar, in his encyclopaedic history of hospital
architecture, which is that while many hospitals have done good work, and
function as landmark buildings in the city, he also suggests that,
Hospitals are also built catastrophes, anonymous institutional com-
plexes run by vast bureaucracies, and totally unfit for the purpose they
have been designed for. They are hardly ever functional, and instead of
making patients feel at home, they produce stress and anxiety.14
While there will always be problems of over-crowding, lack of staff,
inadequate funding for new wards and buildings, and the never-ending
‘re-structuring’ of hospital administration, design also matters. In fact, it

-- 40 of 203 --

26 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
matters very much, as is so patently evident in these recollections, where
it is the ugliness and dereliction of the buildings and their interiors which
have been singled out for contributing so much to feelings of despair and
hopelessness. In this, they echo Susan Sontag’s comments that ugly, dirty
buildings confirm the idea that illness is a punishment, and those who are
ill are simply undeserving.15 How have we ended up with so many hospital
interiors becoming, in architect Ian Clarke’s words, ‘exercises in sensory
deprivation’?16 How is it that in a series of discussion groups organised by
Help the Aged in the UK, older people described hospitals as being ‘like
factories’, or where ‘in the big hospitals, you’re a number’.17
The right to a good death
Attitudes towards death change with historical and cultural circumstances.
Most developed societies are now ageing societies. Today, death is most
commonly associated with the elderly – in comparison with earlier periods
in history with shorter life expectancies, in which death might occur to
anyone at any age and at any time. Then death was randomised; today, it
is more predictable, as highlighted in the first chapter. These demographic
factors have social consequences. One result is that the social status of
the dying has declined. Many today may consider, consciously or uncon-
sciously, that older people no longer fulfil any meaningful roles in life, have
had their time, and therefore their death is relatively unimportant in the
larger scale of things. Steven Verderber and Ben J. Refuerzo wrote of the
dying in hospitals as being ‘often viewed as clandestine beings’.18
In societies pre-occupied with human productivity and economic
growth, older people may come to be regarded as redundant – though par-
adoxically in terms of numbers and voting habits, they still exercise con-
siderable political power. In modern, more future-oriented societies, as
opposed to pre-modern societies where life was more likely to be seen as
cyclical and economically unchanging, the place of death in the cultural
order of things remains in danger of becoming marginalised economically
if not socially. It is not a matter that politicians raise voluntarily or debate
with each other as a policy priority – though this may be changing.
As a result, treatment of the elderly in institutional care has often been
poor, so that when younger people develop life-limiting conditions or dis-
eases in mainstream hospital provision, they too have suffered a loss of
social status which their families and loved ones may have found especially
hard to bear. Hence, the rise of the hospice as an alternative, though over
the past two decades there has been a faster growth of children’s hospices
in the UK compared with adult hospices, in order to provide children and
young people suffering from terminal health conditions the esteem and
care they deserve and warrant.
Institutionalisation per se is not the central problem, however. Indeed,
much of life is institutionalised to little harmful effect. However, there are
issues of scale when dealing with such sensitive issues as the care of the
sick and the dying, that make intimacy and personalised care difficult. The

-- 41 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	27
size and complexity of most hospital buildings and management regimes
inhibits them from being able to focus on a close relationship between staff
and patients. As the philosopher Norbert Elias wrote in his classic study,
The Loneliness of the Dying:
Thus, unembarrassed discourse with or to dying people, which they
especially need, becomes difficult. It is only the institutionalized rou-
tines of hospitals that give a social framework to the situation of dying.
These, however, are mostly devoid of feeling and contribute much to
the isolation of the dying.19
As we shall shortly see, the fear of hospitals as places to die – even though
statistically they are the places where the majority of older people actually
do die – is expressed again and again by the elderly, their families, and the
organisations established to safeguard their interests such as Age UK.
Such anxieties are now leading to organised campaigns. As Professor
Alan Kellehear has argued, ‘the right to a good death’ is emerging as a new
public demand. 20 More educated and rights-conscious citizens, having
grown up in a consumer culture, now stake a claim to a good death for
themselves and their loved ones when the prospect of dying appears on the
horizon. Such demands are not without their ironies, as if for some death
itself was an unnecessary social imposition which, over time, might be abol-
ished or rendered entirely painless and unnoticeable. We have, according to
sceptics such as Dr Michael Fitzpatrick, to guard against a medicalised cul-
ture which suggests that risk, arbitrary unfairness, and human tragedy can
be entirely abolished from modern life – a reservation shared by some about
certain aspects of the contemporary ‘death and dignity’ culture. 21 Never-
theless, it is right that avoidable pain and indignity should be the focus of so
many voices raised in favour of improving palliative care for all who need it.
The movement organised around the ‘right to a good death’ is not
restricted to the UK. Julia Neuberger (now Baroness Julia Neuberger), one
of Britain’s leading campaigners on behalf of the elderly and the dying, has
written,
Across the world we are seeing more and more disquiet about how we
die. Even governments recognise it. So as concerned citizens we should
be doing something to enable our fellow citizens and ourselves to die
well when the time comes. 22
It is within this burgeoning concern with the institutionalisation of death
that the hospice movement gained a foothold and a purchase on the modern
social imagination.
The rise of the hospice movement
The modern hospice movement came into being with the opening of
St Christopher’s Hospice in Sydenham, London, in 1967. Its founder, Cicely

-- 42 of 203 --

28 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
Saunders (later Dame Cicely Saunders), a nurse who subsequently trained
as a doctor, resolved to do something when a patient she had befriended
in 1948, David Tasma, a Polish refugee, discussed with her the idea of
establishing more homely places where people could die in greater tran-
quillity than was currently the case. When he died in February 1948, he
left Saunders a bequest of £500 in the hope that she might establish such a
home, with the now famous appeal: ‘let me be a window in your home’. That
window now exists in the entrance lounge of St Christopher’s Hospice,
designed by Justin Smith (also known as Peter Smith) of Stewart, Hendry,
and Smith. From the outset, the hospice ideal was regarded as requiring a
new and inspiring architectural form – of which the window on the world
was only one element – and ‘Saunders and Tasma imagined a house where
people could find relief from pain, where they could meet with encour-
agement for self-awareness and socialisation, and where the setting would
be uplifting, not depressing’. 23 The first self-designated hospice in North
America, Connecticut Hospice in New Haven, opened in 1974, while the
first hospice in Denmark, Sankt Lukas Hospice in Hellerup, was opened as
recently as 1992.
There had been earlier concerns to improve palliative care, especially
for those suffering from painful cancers. Macmillan Cancer Care was orig-
inated in 1911 by Douglas Macmillan, after being horrified by the pain in
which his father died. Since then, many subsequent innovations in the hos-
pice movement have resulted from people’s direct experiences of dealing
with death, both good and bad. It was the disheartening experiences of art-
ist Maggie Keswick Jencks on being diagnosed with cancer in 1988, along
with the help of her husband, architectural critic and designer, Charles
Jencks, that led to the growing network of Maggie’s Centres, now one of the
most innovative building programmes relating to cancer care in the UK.
The programme is now expanding elsewhere in the world.
The early Maggie’s Centres were commissioned from renowned archi-
tects such as Richard Murphy, David Page, Frank Gehry, Zaha Hadid,
Kisho Kurokawa, and Richard Rogers amongst others, often working for
free. Their distinctive, though very different signature styles, helped bring
the subject of end-of-life architecture enormous public and critical atten-
tion. As Maggie Keswick Jencks wrote, shortly before she died, ‘At the
moment most hospital environments say to the patient, in effect: “How you
feel is unimportant. You are not of value. Fit in with us, not us with you”’. 24
The design of the Maggie’s Centres, by contrast, puts the patient at the
centre of each unique building.
Similarly, it was the unnecessarily miserable death of his father in hospi-
tal which caused American architect and academic, Stephen Verderber, to
undertake with colleague, Ben J. Refuerzo, the pioneering study, Innovations
in Hospice Architecture. 25 In Russia, the hospice movement was principally
initiated by The Guardian journalist Victor Zorza and his wife, as a way of
honouring the care their own daughter had received in an Oxford hospice.
Zorza had been the Moscow correspondent of his newspaper. 26 Finally,
there is also the example of how direct experience of the treatment of the
dying set in train a major new international hospice initiative, evident in the

-- 43 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	29
work of London businessman Graham Perolls, so impressed by the care his
terminally ill father received at St Christopher’s that he became involved
in setting up another hospice in Dartford, close by. Since then, Perolls has
devoted a large part of his life to developing a hospice movement in Roma-
nia, which is now spreading beyond its borders to Moldavia, Serbia, and
elsewhere.
There are now over 240 dedicated hospices in the UK, and around 25,000
hospice or palliative care units across the world: the number is growing
all the time. 27 According to Dr Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, Director
General, World Health Organization in the Foreword to Global Atlas of
Palliative Care in 2020:
The need for palliative care begins early in the course of a life-
threatening illness. More patients are now receiving palliative care
than six years ago, increasing from 16,000 services caring for 3 million
patients to over 25,000 services caring for over 7 million patients. Still,
this is only about 12% of the need being met. Access to essential pallia-
tive medicines, including opioids, remains a major problem, especially
in LMICs. Even with the widespread problems with opioid misuse
in some countries, no one questions the importance of pain relief for
those needing palliative care. 28
In developed countries, the movement began in response to the specific
needs of people dying with painful cancers; in developing countries, it has
been the spread of HIV/AIDs which has precipitated the growth, particu-
larly in hospices for children. All the evidence points to the fact that pallia-
tive care is most needed in low to middle-income countries today.
In many countries, the hospice movement remains largely inspired,
administered, and funded by religious or voluntary organisations – rarely
the state. The majority of hospices in the UK are run and funded by local
charities, giving them additional capacity for innovation in treatment, care,
and indeed the design and administration of the buildings and grounds
they occupy. 29 In addition, charitable organisations such as the Marie
Curie Foundation and Macmillan Cancer Care have both established hos-
pices and in-patient cancer care units.30 Such has been the enthusiasm to
develop hospices in the UK, it now seems that no medium-sized town can
be without its own. Indeed, they now number amongst the larger employers
in some small towns or settlements.31 In America, there are more than 3,200
hospice programmes, though the emphasis there is less on buildings and
facilities and more on providing hospice care at home.32
Unlike most other forms of public health provision, the need for hos-
pice accommodation and hospice treatment in the UK is not decided stra-
tegically from above, on the basis of statistical evidence or demonstrated
need, but has developed incrementally over time, often on the basis of
local demand. It has been a grassroots health movement so far, though this
may change. In Denmark, for example, after a period during which there
was initial scepticism about the hospice movement, the parliament passed
a ‘hospice law’ in December 2004 recommending that there should be a

-- 44 of 203 --

30 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
12-bed hospice in every county as a minimum. In the UK, there are now
suggestions that ‘a palliative unit with a visiting team service and 5.5 beds is
needed per 100,000 inhabitants’.33 So, the movement is gradually becoming
part of the mainstream in public health policy, particularly as the propor-
tion of the population living into old age continues to grow, though this has
been the case in Ireland for some time where the state has for some time
played a central role.
Yet, while most hospices still operate as sovereign organisations respond-
ing to specific local needs, many have joined together as a distinct sec-
tor within the wider public health movement and increasingly operate as
powerful lobbying and advocacy groups for the hospice cause. In the UK,
Hospice UK is an organisation which provides invaluable cross-sectoral
support, particularly in advocacy and raising national public awareness. In
Ireland, the Irish Hospice Foundation, and in the USA, the Hospice Foun-
dation of America, do the same.
After a century or so of slow development, the hospice movement
has become a social phenomenon of our times. It is a movement which
increasingly – though not exclusively – finds symbolic expression in the
development of a new building type, to which few contemporary archi-
tects and designers are able to bring extensive historical reference, let alone
direct experience. This is new territory for many, and because it has death –
that most mysterious and irreducible human fact – at its centre, it is terri-
tory that requires sensitive, reflexive kinds of thinking. Indeed, it requires
a certain piety and humility, not always qualities associated with architects
or indeed the medical professional itself.
Care, architecture, and the passing of time
In essence, the modern hospice is a building, or collection of buildings, with
associated external gardens and grounds, for the care and treatment of those
with life-terminating illnesses. It is principally used to provide residential
accommodation and treatment for small numbers of people – though rarely
the very elderly – usually for a relatively short space of time, measured in
days and weeks rather than months or years. Hospices for children are a dis-
tinct and separate building type, offering different functions and amenities,
emphasising respite care for children and families often over a number of
years following early diagnosis. The hospice priority is symptom control,
pain control and the emotional and spiritual wellbeing of patients and their
friends and families: indeed, as sociologist Glennys Howarth has written,
‘The hospice movement can be seen as part of the resistance to medicalisa-
tion’.34 Hospices also offer day care for non-residential patients, as well as
outreach services to many more who are treated at home, and indeed this is
a rapidly growing area of their work.
While serving different areas of need in the contiguous worlds of pal-
liative, cancer, and social care, each sector of this widening terrain has
contributed something new, with professionals sharing and learning from
each other’s experiences. One lesson is that the architecture of care is not

-- 45 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	31
solely a matter of creating better spaces and places: it has also to figure out
how those spaces – and their management and programming – shape the
experience of time for those whose frailty and vulnerability makes them
dependent on others to give form and meaning to the day and to the time
remaining in their lives. ‘We help people live again’, one hospice manager
told me, ‘no matter how long they have left’.
In their fascinating ethnographic study of a Belgian hospice, K. Coomans,
M. Annemans, and A. Heylighen write that: ‘Hospice care is anchored in
space and spatial practices; however this relation is understudied. It is a
rather new building type, for which architects have few historical refer-
ences, post-occupancy evaluations, or direct experiences available’.35
While much of this is true, as I assumed in the first edition of this book, I
still failed at the time to fully comprehend how much the shaping of time
and its passing had to be at the centre of design as much as the creation of
therapeutic and supportive space.
This time question was only raised in the closing paragraphs of the first
edition, but since then, more of those writing on the psychology of ageing
have come to realise just how important design is as a means of shaping
the experience of time and crucial to developing relationships of care –
palliative and social care most of all. For the fraught care home sector in the
UK, reeling from the pandemic and its many disruptions and high fatali-
ties, what was accentuated was the isolation and social exclusion suffered
by many residents, creating, in the words of Lisa Baraitser, a sense of ‘time
without qualities’.36 A statistic from Age UK published in Nursing and Resi-
dential Care some years before the pandemic stated that ‘60% of people liv-
ing in care homes receive no visits’, reinforcing a general perception that too
often many residential homes appear closed to the outside world.37 Opening
up care provision to the world outside, and helping people re-establish their
old ‘entanglements’ with their former lives, is an important way of helping
people re-establish timetables of their own.
That closure became absolute under ‘Covid conditions’, and the toll was
heavy. But this situation was not without precedent. In an early study of
care home isolation, Private Lives in Public Places, anthropologists Dianne
Willcocks, Sheila Peace, and Leonie Kellaher described a situation in which
neighbours close to one care home complaining of ‘a lack of movement’ in
and around the building, as if it didn’t belong to the street but was its own
world. ‘The inhabitants’, they concluded, ‘come to be seen as a homogenous
group of “old people” lacking personal identity or individuality’.38 This was
the ‘social death’ suffered long before the physical death, in the widely used
language of many sociologists of private and institutional life.
Thus, it is a designed and constructed setting where the quality and
harmonious sequencing of the spaces and functions matters more than for
almost any other building type. Most hospice staff use the metaphor of the
final journey to describe the experience of the patient, and a sense of the
stages and rituals involved in dying is ever-present in the design. The qual-
ity of time is of essence, for time is clearly precious to those who know that
they are dying. This is a major difference to the culture of the hospital.
Edwin Heathcote specifically noted the assumption that once in hospital

-- 46 of 203 --

32 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
‘your time doesn’t really matter any more’.39 Maggie Keswick Jencks wrote
of her early experiences of hospitals that the ‘waiting areas could finish
you off’.
Here is an observer describing how a doctor she writes about understands
the value of giving time as a form of care:
When she looks back on that snowy afternoon, and any number of
patient encounters over her more than twenty years here, she under-
stands that part of what she offers those in her care is time. She works
in the knowledge that time is the finite axis of our lives and that expe-
rience of it matters. Not every minute of a life carries the same weight.
Sometimes weeks, months, years can flit by, light as gossamer, but in
pain, fear or distress, just ten minutes can weigh as heavy as a year.
How you treat people in those minutes makes a difference, the doctor
knows. She is here to help carry the heavy time, so that her patients
may seek out lighter days again.40
This ideal of care as being partly constituted by the sharing of time between
carer and patient is reinforced by Knox’s description of the traditional hos-
pital culture where ‘power relations fix the patient in the role of the recip-
ient of treatment, rather than an equal participant in the care journey’.41
Yet, though waiting can be an exercise in cruel punishment, it can also offer
opportunities for a state of calm self-reflection and a gathering together
of inner resources, both for patients and their relatives and friends. The
design of the places and spaces in which waiting – or it is probably more
appropriate to call it another kind of being – occurs in hospices can tip the
balance one way or the other. In much the same way that we can say that
architecture allows us to shelter in space, it can also allow us to shelter in
time, if the spaces it creates are calm and beautiful, places in which being
can become an act of meditation or inhabitation of a sensuous richness, not
of endurance and anxious distress. It is for precisely this reason that what
is required today is, in the apt words of the Maggie’s Centres design mani-
festo, ‘an architecture that rises to the occasion’.
The poetics of space: studied neutrality or a
sense of occasion?
For there is a poetics of space as much as there is a volume and a shape of
space. There is also a spirit to a building as much as there is a schedule
of works and accommodation. Where death is involved, matters of human
reassurance and comfort surely come to the fore. As will be described later,
the history of the hospice and the hospital is a history of changing human,
spiritual, and religious values, and these buildings have been as imbued
with the values of their era as were the temples, churches, palaces, gardens,
and burial grounds of earlier times. The care of the sick and the dying has,
in most religious traditions, been regarded as a sacred and honourable duty,
requiring settings appropriate to a noble cause. It is the work of architecture

-- 47 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	33
at such times and in such places to inspire and provide solace, even if it
cannot heal.
Yet, the very first architect I spoke to when discussing the idea of this
book, told me that his experience of visiting his mother in a newly built
hospice was that the design was bland and ‘ducking the issue of death’.
The place and the care were superbly well-managed and kindly, he said,
but the building was mediocre. Perhaps this reflects the dilemma that one
hospice director put to me, when she agreed that it remained unclear within
the hospice movement whether their role was in ‘preparing people to die, or
simply caring for them?’
In reality of course hospices do both, though it varies from patient to
patient, often through quite subtle forms of negotiation as and when the
patient chooses to broach the subject, if they do. Even so, there is some truth
in the criticism made by the contemporary sociologist and philosopher
Zygmunt Bauman – not directed at hospices but at modern approaches to
death in general – that there is a tendency to avoid or ‘deconstruct’ mortal-
ity by ‘dissolving the issue of the struggle against death by an ever growing
and never exhausted set of battles against particular diseases and threats to
life’.42 Hospices certainly acknowledge mortality, but do they sometimes
seek to pretend that it can be managed without noticing anything untoward?
This human dilemma carries through to the design and programme of
the building itself, which one often suspects is left deliberately neutral in
order that those managing it and working in it can over time shape and col-
our it to the ethos they feel works best. Even so, something is being missed.
Studied neutrality is not an architectural aspiration, let alone a received
style. Nor does it offer an opportunity to make a thoughtful response as to
how we best find a place and a home for our frail but inviolable human con-
dition. Having started out on this study with a series of visits to a number
of older Irish hospitals and hospices, my initial resistance and dislike for the
traditional Catholic ethos and its baroque eschatology – grottoes, niches
with saints and Blessed Virgin Marys, crucifixes, stained glass windows,
stations of the cross, confessional boxes – mellowed over time to an under-
standing at least to the seriousness of these ‘last things’, even if I found them
personally challenging. It was difficult not to contrast this with the lack of
richness of meaning or sense of occasion to be found in some of the newer
hospices in the UK.
Equally noteworthy was that a similar seriousness was evident in those
hospitals and hospices visited in Scandinavia, though for quite different
reasons. The seriousness and gravity found there was both cultural and
architectural: these were largely state-run facilities, imbued with the spirit
of a long-standing social democracy, highly efficient and modern, but with
a design style based on calm, uncluttered Scandinavian ergonomics and
hygienic efficiency, rather than on chintz and floral curtains. The Scandi-
navians are perhaps too serious about many things, but in social welfare and
medical provision, they seem to have established a uniquely successful mix
of social philosophy and design, management theory and human scale. It
should always be remembered that hospices also cater for young people who
are dying, and who may prefer to be in a building that feels as if it belongs to

-- 48 of 203 --

34 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
the modern world, rather than a place only for the elderly. Similarly, given
how much is known about how good design can help to improve staff morale
and self-worth, then staff attitudes and preferences also count on this mat-
ter, and they may prefer a building with a bit of architectural flair or élan.43
The following chapters examine what elements are involved in the
design of new hospice buildings, as well as the adaptation and refurbish-
ment of existing buildings to meet hospice needs, based on talking with
hospice managers, administrators, health professionals, patients and their
families, as well as with architects and designers who undertake to compete
and carry out such work. The study further endeavours to separate out and
analyse the many different elements in the commissioning process, so that
future hospice projects can benefit from the experience already gained in
the burgeoning building programme in the UK and across the world.
Evidence-based design
But the lessons to be learnt from the modern hospice movement are not
just for future hospice developments but are also relevant to all of those
involved in palliative care and the care of the elderly in mainstream hos-
pitals and other residential settings – hence the wish to widen the scope
of this new edition. Great changes are occurring in hospitals across the
world, of which the growth of the hospice movement is just one element.
There are widespread moves towards providing single room accommoda-
tion for everybody in the hospitals of the future, and there will also be
more strategies for treating people at home. A changing and more fluid set
of relationships between the hospital, the hospice, and the home is now a
key characteristic of modern palliative care. In the last stages of their lives,
many terminally ill people move between the hospice and home as medical
needs require, though often with the intention that death finally occurs at
home, as so many still wish.
Hospitals are beginning to seek the same flexibility, learning both from
modern hotels, as well as the long tradition in which the hospital was by
definition a guest-house, a sanctuary, and a residential retreat. It happened
once before. In the early 20th century, hospital planners, administrators,
and architects throughout the world responded imaginatively to the wide-
spread scourge of tuberculosis (TB), creating buildings and landscape set-
tings of great clarity and clinical optimism. If anything, the functionalist,
pared down minimalism of the early modern movement was ideally suited
to the design of sanatorium buildings, and many of these buildings are now
recognised amongst the greatest architectural achievements of the 20th
century. Aalto’s Paimio Sanatorium (1933) is a key landmark in the archi-
tecture of the modern world, its design and construction paid for by over
50 separate Finnish communities.44 It retains the power to convince us that
architecture can help make the world a better place. Indeed, there is a con-
nection between that idealistic health era and today, with one architectural
critic noting that the design of Our Lady’s Wing at St Joseph’s Hospice in
Hackney (1957) ‘majored on bright spacious bedrooms that had large bay

-- 49 of 203 --

a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e 	35
windows and balconies, echoing earlier exemplars such as the Peckham
Health Centre’.45 That wing had been designed by Stewart, Hendry, and
Smith, who went on to design St Christopher’s and a number of other hos-
pices, in what became a distinctive and attractive style.
However, the hospice movement is as much about an ethos of care as it is
about plans, elevations, and the use of pastel colours in private rooms. There
is already much work going on in mainstream healthcare to do with improv-
ing the material conditions of patients, and the frequent use of the phrase
‘evidence-based design’ tells much of that story. In short, more research is
being undertaken into whether there are measurable (and by implication,
causal) relationships between the quality of design – including the light,
colour schemes, and views of nature available to patients – and their general
sense of self-esteem and wellbeing, if not measurable improvements in their
health. This will be an important element at the new Appleby Blue alms-
house, where Martyn Craddock, CEO of its commissioning body, United
St Saviour’s Charity, told me how a key element in brief was to see ‘how one
can engineer social interaction and engagement into the design process’. To
this end, two small studio flats in the development have been allocated to
researchers to follow through how residents engage with each other and the
neighbouring community as a result both of the building’s design and its
programme of activities as a community hub.
Furthermore, hospices continue to evolve and change as the medical
and cultural circumstances within which they operate change too. They
are becoming more secularised as more public money enters the network.
Improved forms of medication and pain control mean that more people leave
hospices, with their condition manageable locally, to die at home as they
originally wished. The phrase ‘terminal care’ has been replaced by ‘special-
ist palliative care’; the one-way door has become a revolving door. Where
the early hospices were primarily conceived solely as in-patient buildings,
a little like cottage hospitals, their programme is now much more exten-
sive.46 As one hospice manager told me, ‘The number of beds is no longer
Figures 2.1 Staggered window bays at St Christopher’s Hospice.

-- 50 of 203 --

36 	a H o U s e at t H e e n d o f L i f e
the key architectural issue, or of the building plan’. New Farleigh Hospice
in Chelmsford (completed January 2006) was designed and built without
a mortuary, something that would not have been considered appropriate
prior to that time. Architect Ian Clarke suggested that ‘the higher discharge
rate means that hospices are beginning to share some common elements
with Maggie’s Centres – they are on the same spectrum of provision’.47
In the UK, the hospice movement has grown by leaps and bounds since
the opening of St Christopher’s in 1967 and has become a beacon of innova-
tion across the world since. According to Julia Neuberger,
Most people are now convinced that hospices – both the institutions
and the movement – can deal with the pain of dying, which is why the
movement has become one of Britain’s great success stories, an export
in thinking, intellectual and social, that has influenced care of the
dying worldwide.48
In the course of many visits and discussions, I was told that the hospice
movement, nationally and internationally, is good at sharing best practice,
and people are learning from each other all the time. I was also told, on
more than one occasion, that one major area of deficiency is that ‘there
is still too little architectural expertise’. While this book can only outline
some of the many issues facing the architectural and design aspects of the
hospice movement, it is intended to contribute to this culture of sharing and
is an attempt to orchestrate a conversation between those who design and
build the hospices of the future and those whose work it is to make these
special places a true ‘house at the end of life’ for those inhabiting their final
days and hours on earth.

The modern hospice movement grows out of a complex history of build-
ing types associated with the root etymology of hospitium – hospital, hos-
tel, hotel, and hospitality. All are places where visitors, guests, pilgrims,
or those in physical or spiritual need are welcomed and cared for. Since
the 18th century, there has been considerable overlap in architectural form
and ethos between places of care, custody, and even correction as society
became more institutionalised. The modern hospice, along with other set-
tings for palliative and social care, returns the care of the terminally ill
to small-scale, more domestic settings and has lessons for those providing
palliative care in hospitals and residential homes for the elderly.
Beginnings and endings
The most obvious ways in which architecture has historically attended to
the needs of the mortal human body is through the design of buildings such
as hospitals and other places of sanctuary or respite. Such institutions were
found in ancient Greece; in Rome (valetudinaria for the care of sick slaves,
gladiators, and soldiers were said to have existed around 100 BC); and in
ancient India. The Knights of St John claimed to have based their religious
order and provision of hospice services on the apparent existence of a Hos-
pital of St. John the Baptist in Jerusalem in early Christian times.1 Follow-
ing the Crusades, they expanded across the Mediterranean, and ‘by 1113
hospices were already functioning at S. Gilles near Marseilles and at Asti,
Pisa, Bari, Otranto, Taranto and Messina’, serving sick pilgrims and the
poor’. 2 At this time, the word hospice referred to ‘A house of rest or enter-
tainment for travellers or strangers, esp. one belonging to a religious order’.3
Its adoption as a term exclusively denoting a place of care for the dying did
not happen until the end of the 19th century. The earliest dedicated hospi-
tal building of which we still have floor plans is the hospital in Mihintale,
Sri Lanka, dating from the 6th century. A visitor to Persia in 1160 found 60
hospitals in one city. Hospitals were established elsewhere in the Muslim
world between the 8th and 12th centuries, and Baghdad was renowned for
its many hospitals.
In medieval Europe, care for the sick fell to religious organisations, to
the monasteries, as did the provision of almshouses for the elderly. In the
Be kind quickly: how the
modern hospice movement
changed (nearly) everything
3

-- 54 of 203 --

40 	B e k i n d QU i C k LY
Netherlands and in Belgium, the begijnhoven (communities of the elderly
and the sick, and of women in particular) can be dated back to the 13th
century and were exclusive to these two countries. Of 40 originally estab-
lished in the Netherlands, only two now exist, in Amsterdam and Breda,
whereas in Belgium, nearly all remain in existence today.4 In the 15th cen-
tury, Florence became famous for its range of hospitals; in the 18th century,
France had the largest hospital population in Europe, with over 2000 hos-
pitals dedicated to the care of the sick and the dying. The Royal Hospital
at Kilmainham near Dublin (now the Irish Museum of Modern Art) was
opened in 1684 for 300 invalid army veterans and had many rooms in series
(four men to a room), opening onto a gallery at ground level and on to cor-
ridors on the floors above. In general terms, though, hospital building was
still a rarity until the end of the 19th century, and this was particularly the
case in North America. In the United States, there were only two hospitals
to be found in 1800; by 1873, this number had grown to 178, and by 1923,
there were 4,978. 5 Since then, America has been a leader in developing a
wide range of specialised hospital buildings and campuses, though many of
these are privately paid for through medical insurance rather than through
public taxation.
Historian Theodore Zeldin claims that ‘there have been periods when
hospitals flourished, treating their patients with more or less respect, and
others when they could not care less’. 6 Florence Nightingale once wrote
that she ‘looked forward to the abolition of all hospitals’, implying that
they created as many medical and social problems as they solved.7 Dif-
ferent societies alternate between regarding the treatment of the sick and
the care of the dying as something to be done in an institution or some-
thing to be done at home. As Juliet Davis has written in her study of the
ethic of care in relation to design, there have been a number of major
developments in the ‘“relocation of care” from one domain of social life
to another’. 8 Likewise, we might say that the social status of the elderly
rises and falls during different historical periods (as well as separately
within different religious or civil cultures and traditions). Yet, today’s
demographic trends towards a vastly increasing number of older people
across the world is unprecedented.
While the early European almshouses and hospitals were provided by
religious orders, today they are more likely to be provided by the state, or
occasionally by independent not-for-profit trusts or profit-making corpora-
tions. Some of the more experimental therapeutic or care settings, such as
Vidarkliniken in Sweden, are provided by quasi-religious or ethical move-
ments: in this case, the Rudolf Steiner community. In the UK, the expanding
network of Maggie’s Centres and almshouses is based on private and corpo-
rate fund-raising. The ‘mixed economy’ of hospital and hospice provision
is likely to expand, though it is clear that innovation usually comes easier
to the voluntary or private sector or to those organisations which work at a
smaller scale. A frequently overlooked issue in the fund-raising discussion
is the therapeutic value of raising money ‘as part of the bereavement pro-
cess’, to cite the words of Dr Sarah McCloskey at Marymount University

-- 55 of 203 --

B e k i n d Q U i C k LY 	41
Hospital and Hospice in Cork. In her time in palliative care, Dr McCloskey
remembers how important it has been to some people to undertake charity
walks, or unusual acts of service or devotion, to raise money as a way of
remembering or commemorating the loss of a loved one.9
Early European hospitals often shared the cellular structure of monas-
teries, closed off from the world but with ambulatory space and courtyard
gardens. This model was also found in almshouses and in the begijnhoven
in the Netherlands and Belgium. Today, the Amsterdam begijnhof draws
tourists from around the world, attracted to a beautiful sheltered settlement
which still functions in accordance with its original principles. These were
sanctuaries for single women, settlements in which they could live inde-
pendently, working out in the world, but to which they returned at night
to a secure, gated retreat. Variations of such communal settlements for the
poor, the elderly, the sick, and the afflicted have existed in many countries.
The oldest almshouse in Britain still in existence is the Hospital of St
Oswald in Worcester, said to date from 990. In the words of one historian,
As a building type, almshouses must be one of the least altered. After
hundreds of years most are still used for their original purpose, hous-
ing the elderly and the needy. This extraordinary continuity of use
has few parallels with other kinds of historic building … Today there
are nearly 1,800 separate almshouse charities, with 30,000 almshouse
dwellings. Of these 868 are listed. Nearly one-fifth of these are grade I
or grade II* – not surprising when you appreciate they include premier
division examples such as Greenwich Naval Hospital, Lord Leycester’s
Figure 3.1 Ironmongers’ Almshouses in Hackney, London, constructed in
1714, now The Museum of the Home.

-- 56 of 203 --

42 	B e k i n d Q U i C k LY
Hospital in Warwick, Browne’s Hospital in Stamford, Lincolnshire, of
the barely changed Castle Rising almshouses near King’s Lynn, Nor-
folk, where the elderly women still wear 17th century red cloaks and
conical hats on special occasions. Many almshouses are often the most
simple and delightful grouping of buildings to be found in the heart of
historic towns.10
These ‘Hospitals’, as many of the almshouses were often called, would also
function as hostels for pilgrims. The Hospitium at York continues to act as
a lodging house for visitors to St Mary’s Abbey close by, and the Hospitium
at Reading acts similarly for pilgrims to Reading Abbey. It is interesting to
note the retention of the word hospital – with its origins in hospitality –
in Ireland, America, and the UK and elsewhere, compared with the use
of an entirely different term, the sjukhus, ziekenhuis, or krankenhus (sick-
house) in countries such as Norway, Sweden, Denmark, The Netherlands,
and Germany. This might be a rare case where etymology and ethos go
separate ways over time.
Almshouses and hospitals were frequently inter-changeable names for
similar institutions, sometimes also known as Spital Houses. Almshouses
Figures 3.2 ‘Be Kind Quickly’: plaque at cottage almshouses in Reydon,
Suffolk.

-- 57 of 203 --

B e k i n d QU i C k LY 	43
combined the root architectural form of the private dwelling (terraced
house or cottage), replicated in series, to produce a larger collective insti-
tution, often anchored by a chapel or refectory building, with a cupola,
clock, or sundial over the central door. The beautiful almshouses at Reydon
near Southwold in Suffolk are based on a series of cottage-style communal
buildings surrounding a central garden. Each building is adorned with a
distinctive and uplifting inscription, such as ‘Be Kind Quickly’. Elements of
some of such almshouse building details have resurfaced in recent hospice
designs, including in the new Appleby Blue almshouse in south London,
designed by Witherford Watson Mann architects, and the subject of a more
detailed case study in Chapter 7. This is just one of a number of new devel-
opments in the almshouse movement, the subject of a detailed report by
the Royal Institute of Chartered Surveyors, Almshouses: a model of community
housing for an ageing population, by Jenny Pannell and Alison Pooley, pub-
lished in 2020.11
A darker side to architectural history
There is also a darker side to this history, or at least another perspective,
which the French historian, Michel Foucault, once referred to as ‘the great
Figures 3.3 Gatehouse and buildings at Reydon Almshouses, Suffolk.

-- 58 of 203 --

44 	B e k i n d Q U i C k LY
confinement’.12 In his view, the 1656 decree setting up the Hôpital Général
in Paris was the start of a European-wide movement to establish institutions
for those who were not properly settled within conventional family life for
various reasons – old age, infirmity, ill health, madness, poverty, vagrancy,
loss of family, and so on. Although this appeared to be an act of charity, it
was also an early form of social control (if not correction). ‘For the next two
centuries’, in the words of architectural historian, Thomas A. Markus, ‘this
programme consumed by far the largest slice of public building resources
in the construction of poorhouses, workhouses, orphanages, almshouses,
prisons, hospitals and asylums’.13 That architectural tradition is still with
us today, in physical form but still also lingering in institutional mindsets
and practices.
The writer Clair Wills grew up in a family that worked in a large men-
tal hospital in the south of England in the 1950s, and no one has better
described the ‘total world’ of the large asylums which ‘were something
between state villages and prisons’.
Before the Second World War, the social world of the hospitals must
have felt similar to the early 19th-century villages that grew up around
large landed estates. A single employer, accommodation on site, a strict
hierarchy of employment, ranging from boiler stoker and farmhands
at the bottom to doctors and medical superintendents at the top. The
hospital was a self-sufficient community. Netherne had its own vege-
table and dairy farm (with labour provided by the patients), a bakery,
kitchens, laundry, carpentry and light engineering workshops, a print-
ing press, chapel, library, cinema, dance hall, orchestra, choir, sports
facilities and amateur dramatic society.14
There are few grimmer stories of the role that hospitals played in both alle-
viating while at the same time as institutionalising the daily life of the poor
than that told by historian Graham Robb in his history of peasant life in
rural France, recording that,
At Provins, between 1854 and 1859, 1,258 children were deposited in
the rotating barrel built into the wall of the general hospital. (It can
now be seen in the local museum.) These tours d’abandon, which con-
tained a straw bed and some blankets, made it possible for mothers to
abandon their babies anonymously and safely.15
In this period, the hospital was transformed as an architectural type, along
with the prison and the school, becoming what has been described as a total
institution, designed to make surveillance and the imposition of order as
standardised as possible. Many hospitals also functioned as workhouses,
which were publicly feared as places where the elderly – with husbands
and wives sometimes separated on admission – eked out their last years in
authoritarian and forbidding surroundings. Fear of ending life in the work-
house permeated the lives of the poor until only a generation or so ago.

-- 59 of 203 --

B e k i n d Q U i C k LY 	45
The boundaries between care, custody, and correction often became
blurred in such institutions, and ambiguities as to the prevailing ethos of
these hospital types persist amongst many people – especially the elderly –
even today. In Hard Times, Charles Dickens wrote that in his fictitious
northern city of Coketown, ‘The jail might have been the infirmary, the
infirmary might have been the jail, the town-hall might have been either,
or both, or anything else, for anything that appeared to the contrary in
the graces of their construction’. Perhaps the most notorious British insti-
tution of all is Broadmoor, established in the 1850s as an Asylum for the
Criminally Insane. It was sited in open countryside, commanding ‘one of
the finest panoramas in all England’, with extensive gardens, separated
into those for men and those for women. Its Italianate red-brick monu-
mental building was described by journalists at the time as being a cross
between a monastery and a convent.16 However, its isolation was deter-
mined from the start; even today, there are no public transport connections
to Broadmoor.
Indeed, the same institution over time can eventually betray its own
founding principles, as Thompson & Goldin succinctly described:
Hospitals have a way of being conceived in glory, executed with inge-
nuity and humanity, then subjected in use to misuse and abuse, finally
to be overcrowded and always forever plagued by insufficient funds. A
hospital before death can become more decrepit than a man. The man
dies, but a moribund hospital may live on for decades – and, what is so
terrible, filled to capacity.17
The paradox is that these grim places were often beautifully designed and
elegantly constructed – it was the internal regime that marshalled despair
and inculcated a spirit of hopelessness. A Royal Commission on the His-
torical Monuments of England undertook a survey of English hospitals
from 1660 to 1948 and found over 2000 listed for their architectural merit.18
Tony Monk has written of the design process involved in this time,
Hospital architecture reflected the style and design ideas of its time.
These buildings usually owed their external appearance to the design-
er’s preconception of what a building type should look like from the
outside. They were not created as an expression of the internal plan-
ning and room functions which has been the normal generator of the
architecture of modern hospitals.19
Finding the balance between outward form and internal function has been
one of the great architectural dilemmas of hospital design ever since. The
related purposes of hospitality, shelter, and sanctuary created great sim-
ilarities in the design of almshouses, hostels, hotels, and hospitals (and
indeed colleges and prisons), so much so that each could easily be adapted
to serve the needs of another. This has become more evident in recent
years, as a number of redundant hospitals have been successfully converted

-- 60 of 203 --

46 	B e k i n d QU i C k LY
into apartments – the German Hospital in Hackney is just one of many
examples. More astonishingly, perhaps, we have also witnessed former
prisons being converted into hostels or hotels. Langholmen Youth Hos-
tel in Stockholm is a former prison, as is the Malmaison Hotel in Oxford,
opened in 2005. The differences are not always blurred by use. When the
entertainer Michael Flanders returned after the war in 1945 to take up his
disrupted studies again at Christ Church College, Oxford, he wrote ahead
to say that he would need a ground floor room as he now used a wheel-
chair as a result of contracting polio. ‘Christ Church wrote back promptly
saying it was a university college, not a hospital, and could not accommo-
date him’. 20
A pattern book for shelter and care
A number of common design elements can be seen in the building types
or ensembles of buildings relating to the institutional custody or care of
people. This historical pattern book of types exemplifies the blurring of
architectural and institutional ethics mentioned above.

If these building types exist on a continuum, then at one end can be
found the prison, a walled monolith, wholly impermeable to the society
around it, whose only landscaping takes the form of an internal bare exer-
cise yard. At the other end, there is the health farm or retirement village
(such as Lark Hill in Nottingham, described in Chapter 7), an elective
campus or settlement, open to the world beyond but distinguished per-
haps by its architecture and setting, as well as by the ethos of the life
lived voluntarily within. Here, the provision of gardens or communal
landscaping, exquisitely so in some places, is common. Sometimes, the
crossovers between building types and internal regimes are surprising.
Some hospitals for the mentally ill (formerly known as asylums) have been
run as prisons in all but name, while others have been designed and run
as almost self-managing therapeutic communities, occupying village type
settlements.

One thing all of the above share is that they either are established with a
pre-ordained ethos (of care, custody, or correction) or quickly develop one
of their own, informally. This ethos may be for good or bad and has been
subject to much interesting sociological investigation, notably through the
work of Erving Goffman. There is an equally interesting parallel history
of utopian settlements which have sought to bring planning, architecture,
and social purpose together, though as historian Gillian Darley has noted,
‘To design innovatory small settlements which are physically and socially
coherent remains a challenge’. 21 Sometimes, the ethos of an institution and
its staff can overcome the restrictions of the building. This was observed
in a programme of visits organised by the Hospice Friendly Hospitals pro-
gramme of the Irish Hospice Foundation, where it was evident that in some
places dedicated staff were capable of achieving home-like, caring condi-
tions even in the most inappropriate buildings and settings.
For a number of the other institutions represented in the typology above,
the design and regime are consciously intended to produce conditions

which are decidedly unhomely: conditions of isolation, self-reflection, and
