we continue to observe, the boundaries of institutions are too frequently
blurred, especially when the building form has been designed for one pur-
pose but now finds itself used for another.
Hospitals (and hospices) learning from hotels
The blurring of boundaries often creates ambiguities within the public
mind, as well as producing organisational problems. Today, private medi-
cal care promotes its facilities as offering hotel-quality accommodation and
levels of comfort and service. Yet, one administrator in a private hospital
caring for some terminally ill patients told me that leaning too far towards
the hotel model had created problems with hygiene standards, owing to the
design of interiors and the use of materials which were not really suitable
for strict cleaning regimes – and that these have had to be addressed retro-
spectively (or ‘retro-fitted’ as the current jargon has it). By definition, acute
hospitals have to place a priority on efficiency and rapid response clinical
regimes, even though many patients are there for rest and recuperation.
One can only pursue the hotel analogy so far. Even so, it was moving to be
told on a visit to Vidarkliniken that terminally ill patients – who in its early
days were mixed in with other patients – were regarded by other patients as
‘honoured guests’. 22 In the Belgian hospice described in Chapter 2, patients
are described as ‘guests’ too. There is something especially resonant about
the ritual status of the guest that many hospices have drawn upon.
The architectural critic, Edwin Heathcote, has suggested that the ten-
dency towards blurring the boundaries between the hospital and the hotel
only makes sense – if at all – if the hospital designers are clear precisely
as to what kind of hotel they have in mind. There is a world of difference
between a budget hotel which provides little more than a cell with a bed and
en-suite facilities, and a luxury hotel suite designed to make visitors want to
stay for as long as possible. The former might offer a model for a hygienic,
private space for treatment and rapid recuperation – the latter would fail on
many counts of cost, as well as issues of hygiene and maintenance.
The social benefits of nearby hotel-style accommodation might be
offered outside the place of direct medical care. This is now happening at
University College Hospital, London, for example:
Some patients being treated at UCLH on an ongoing basis may be
offered patient accommodation as part of their care plan. There
are two options – The Cotton Rooms and Paul’s House. All patient
accommodation is arranged by a patient’s clinical team. The Cot-
ton Rooms, our new patient hotel, is available for patients who need
ongoing hospital treatment but are well enough to not need a hospital
bed overnight – this is known as ambulatory care. Generally, ambu-
latory care is available to patients being treated for cancer. Patients
receiving ongoing treatment at one of our specialist centres, for

-- 63 of 203 --

B e k i n d QU i C k LY 	49
example at the National Hospital for Neurology and Neurosurgery,
may also be offered patient accommodation. Paul’s House provides a
‘home from home’ for children and young people and their families
diagnosed with cancer. It is run by the UK’s leading cancer charity
for children and young people, CLIC Sargent (now known as ‘Young
Lives vs Cancer’). 23
In St Olav’s Hospital, Trondheim, in Norway, a patient hotel was opened
in September 2004, with 110 rooms. The majority of the rooms are used
by patients who need treatment on a daily basis but not full-time medical
supervision, including those who have come from more remote parts of the
country. The top floor is given over entirely to the mothers and fathers of
new-born babies who are given their own room six hours after birth, where
they can stay for free for several days. Some cancer patients may stay for up
to three months at the hotel while receiving treatment. There are emer-
gency alarms in all rooms and all hotel staff have received basic medical
training. Yet, it looks and feels very much like a good quality modern hotel
(in fact at least 20% of the rooms are let commercially), where people come
and go as they wish.
During a visit to St Olav’s, I was informed that it is cheaper to provide
some patients with hotel accommodation than it would be to look after
them as in-patients occupying a bed on a conventional ward. Such eco-
nomic insights, counter-intuitive though they might be, are now at the fore-
front of what is called ‘evidence-based design’: how can better treatment be
provided in more amenable surroundings, and yet still not cost more? Such
are some of the lessons which hospice designers are learning from models
of residential accommodation and care, other than the traditional hospital
ward or room.
Physical, emotional, social, and spiritual care
In the UK, the term hospice principally defines a building or institution
dedicated to those suffering from life-terminating illnesses, many of whom
may have been otherwise in the prime of life, or are still children. The
treatment afforded to patients in a hospice is usually called palliative care,
but this term also covers the care of many very elderly people in hospi-
tals towards the end of their life and who have lost the ability to live inde-
pendently. Some people also wish to make a further distinction between
palliative care and terminal care, the latter describing the treatment given
in the very last days or hours of life. 24 The World Health Organization
defines palliative care as follows:
Palliative Care is an approach that improves the quality of life of
patients and their families facing the problems associated with
life-threatening illness, through the prevention and relief of suffering
by means of early identification and impeccable assessment and treat-
ment of pain and other problems, physical, psychological, spiritual. 25

-- 64 of 203 --

50 	B e k i n d QU i C k LY
Although this book deals principally with the design and ethos of hospices,
Maggie’s Centres and related Extra-Care provision, such considerations
have vital lessons which can be carried across into mainstream hospital sys-
tems, where palliative care for the elderly and dying still leaves much to be
desired, as has already been seen. Staff in the former sector have described
their settings as experiments in both living life to the full and dying well,
such positive experiences can be adopted and incorporated into main-
stream health provision for the elderly and the dying. Indeed, the hospice
movement itself says that,
Hospice care and palliative care are essentially the same thing. Both
refer to a special kind of care for people whose illness may be no longer
curable. The care supports people to achieve the best possible quality
of life, enabling them to live as actively and creatively as possible after
diagnosis and particularly in the final stages of their illness. Through
hospice or palliative care, patients have access to skilled medical and
nursing care, physiotherapy, pain and symptom control, counselling
and complementary therapies. In considering the whole person, it
offers physical, emotional, social and spiritual care. Families and car-
ers are supported throughout the illness and in bereavement. 26
While the focus in this book is predominantly on the buildings and land-
scapes occasioned by the modern hospice movement, the majority of people
treated by hospices are treated as out-patients in their homes, or as day
patients, rather than in-patients requiring rooms and beds. A typical hos-
pice with 20 in-patient beds may be also treating 100 day patients each
week and up to 500 other patients in their own homes. If anything, day-
care provision for those suffering from life-limiting illnesses is one of the
expanding areas in new hospice designs, compared to residential provision.
Hospices have naturally attracted much favourable attention and are
regarded as sanctuaries of highly personalised care and single-minded
devotion. Yet, while the term ‘hospice’ has generally gained positive associ-
ations, some resistance remains. After the Macmillan Runcie Day Hospice
near London changed its name to Grove House, patient referrals increased
by 65%. It was said by some that ‘the reason for the name change was
the impression that the word hospice had a discouraging effect on many
patients’. 27 However, the word hospice is still used in fund-raising cam-
paigns. In one Swedish hospice, I was told rather mischievously that in Swe-
den ‘to die in a hospice is to be very modern’. Sometimes, they appear to
achieve their reputation at the expense of the general or acute hospital,
where palliative care if it exists is only one part of the hospital’s function.
This is causing some people to demand the rapid expansion of dedicated
hospice provision as a solution to the question of how the majority of elderly
people can spend their last days in dignified, personalised care.
But there are real problems here. The most obvious one is that of scale.
Hospices are by their very nature almost domestic in the size and ambi-
ence of the buildings they inhabit and in the design of their interiors (even
if, as hospice architect Ian Clarke once pointed out, they can be as large

-- 65 of 203 --

B e k i n d Q U i C k LY 	51
as 40 times the size of a typical family house). Many operate as trusts,
funded by donations, bequests, and endowments, and run by staff who feel
a particular calling to look after the terminally ill. They have also tended
until recently to specialise in the care of those with cancer, or some other
aggressive or rapidly debilitating terminal condition. Many will prioritise
providing places for children or younger patients. The palliative care of the
vast majority of elderly people – for whom dying may be a long, drawn-out
process – has never been a principal concern of the hospice movement and
nor is it likely to become so. This leaves the larger portion of elderly people
with care needs in the hands of a poorly organised, often poorly funded, and
increasingly private sector, which during the Covid pandemic struggled to
cope and is now the subject of national concern in the UK as to the future
of social care.
At first sight, it seems that hospices deal almost solely with people in the
very last stages of life. The average stay in many hospices is about ten days,
sometimes even less. But this is changing, as hospices also become places
where the symptoms of those suffering from life-limiting conditions can
be treated and managed, allowing patients to return home in a much more
stable and pain-free condition, to die in greater peace and tranquillity at
home or possibly when re-admitted only at the very end of life. At Trinity
Hospice in London, the Chief Executive once told me that ‘Not everyone
comes here to die; 45% of patients go home again’. 28
Even in a very short time, there has been a significant change in the
expectations of what hospice care can provide. The idea that hospices only
provided terminal care has been strongly challenged as more hospices
discovered that by symptom-management they could return many more
patients to their home. As had been observed in the Danish report cited
elsewhere: ‘A much larger number of beds in English hospices are used for
symptom control than for the final stay at the end of life’. 29 Hospices also
provide respite care for patients who spend most of their time at home, but
whose relatives or carers occasionally need a break themselves, as well as
providing care for growing numbers of day patients or patients treated as
part of extensive outreach programmes. Thus, the hospice is rapidly mov-
ing from being an end destination or place of terminal care to an active care
and treatment centre at the heart of the community. It is also a model of
social sustainability, given how extensive is the involvement of volunteers
in running charity shops (some hospices operate as many as 25 shops in
their catchment area), in undertaking day-to-day activities with patients
and families, in maintaining gardens, and in fund-raising.
Furthermore, the majority of hospices now undertake extensive educa-
tion work, not only for their own staff and volunteers, but for other nurs-
ing professionals, and even for friends and family members of patients who
have had hospice care, and who want to train as volunteers or learn how to
help in other ways. The provision of an education wing, or an education
suite within the main hospice building, is becoming a standard element of
the design brief, as will be discussed later in Chapter 5 with regard to the
ambitious and beautifully designed £6.5 million CARE education centre at
St Christopher’s Hospice in London, which opened in 2021.30

-- 66 of 203 --

52 	B e k i n d Q U i C k LY
The hospice movement is not without its critics. These come particularly
from those providing palliative care in mainstream public health institu-
tions. In Norway, a degree of scepticism about the hospice movement was
apparent amongst some hospital professionals I spoke to on a visit there.
This was not because there weren’t good hospices in Norway, but because
some felt that once a particularly charismatic hospice founder or leader had
themselves died, or moved on, there were dangers that the individual hos-
pice could become isolated from developments in improving palliative care
being pioneered in the mainstream health service. ‘A hospice should not be
a place, but a set of contexts which can be reproduced anywhere’ was the
opinion of one senior pastoral care worker I interviewed. She went on to say
that, ‘In our hospital we try to spread the lessons learnt from palliative care
throughout the whole hospital – and you would not get that if the hospice
and the hospital were separate institutions’. These concerns – that too great
a separation between the hospice and the hospital (or residential care home)
would inadvertently lead to a loss of status or interest in the latter as a place
of dedicated care – remain open to debate.
Even within the hospice movement itself in the UK, one administra-
tor admitted that, ‘There is a danger that we are creating super-benign
environments across the country in which criticism – especially from
outsiders – is impossible’. While many were prepared to admit that there
had been mistakes in the design of the buildings they worked in, they were
reluctant to admit this publicly for fear of upsetting generous donors and
fund-raisers. One senior hospice manager also admitted that in developing
the brief for a replacement hospice building, which had involved many visits
to other hospices, while she ‘could easily recognise bad design, it was much
more difficult to say what good design was’. Many people will be sympa-
thetic to this dilemma, which points up a familiar paradox that if something
works very well it isn’t noticed, but if it works badly, it constantly draws
attention to itself. Is good design essentially invisible? Or are its effects felt
unconsciously?
A strength of many hospices is that staff tend to stay working there for
a very long time, in the process becoming deeply committed to the ethos
of their building, fellow staff, and customary routines. Rather than being
depleted emotionally by the constant presence of death amongst their
patients, nursing staff seem to be strengthened by the relationships they
create and foster. As so often, it was noticeable that by far the majority of
nursing staff in hospitals and hospices visited in Ireland, Norway, and Swe-
den are women, and the degree of domestication in the design of interiors
often reflects this. However, as the Maggie’s Centres have realised, interior
design can too often impose a value-system (bland, official, sterile, inoffen-
sive to the point of becoming oppressive), and this has to be judged rather
more carefully, if there is to be an element of delight, uplift, surprise, and
community. The case study of Maggie’s Southampton in Chapter 7 dis-
cusses this issue in greater detail.
Hospices have also learnt to handle the emotional needs of the nursing
staff better than hospitals, given that their principal role is to care for the
dying. In a general hospital dealing with sudden, traumatic, or any other

-- 67 of 203 --

B e k i n d QU i C k LY 	53
kind of death (especially that of children) can be distressing for younger or
less experienced nursing and ancillary staff. They too need places where
they can gather, sit quietly, or talk afterwards, ideally with skilled help, but
alas there is often nowhere to go.
Yet, despite the reservations of some palliative care professionals work-
ing in more mainstream forms of hospital and residential care, the hospice
movement continues to attract considerable public interest and support,
noticeably so in a sphere of work once considered almost taboo. Of the
many reasons why this might be so, one is almost certain that hospices have
been very successful in assuaging people’s fear of dying.
One element of this has been through its espousal of buildings and set-
tings which do not look like forbidding monolithic institutions. Modern
hospice buildings, whatever their faults, are usually at a scale and level of
visual re-assurance which enables people to enter unafraid, and to find
within them a sense of calm, light, space, peace, and supportive order. How
this has been achieved, and can continue to be achieved, is discussed shortly.

This chapter examines the continuing growth of the hospice movement
across the world and what are increasingly seen as the challenges to archi-
tects and designers to develop more dedicated and sympathetic build-
ings. The development of the design brief is seen as the crucial element
in achieving successful buildings, and a number of case studies are cited,
detailing the process by which the needs and interests of a wide range of
parties – particularly patients, their families, and staff – can be embodied
in the design and consultation process. The hospice movement has to date
been good at sharing good practice in design matters, though there is still
too little attention being paid to ‘post-occupancy evaluation’ procedures,
whereby lessons for the future are learned in detail from each completed
building.
Behind high walls
St Patrick’s Hospital and Marymount Hospice together look down on the
port city of Cork in Ireland from a densely terraced hill of Georgian houses,
high-steepled churches, and ageing grey stone public buildings. Tradition-
ally, the hospital was perceived to be a forbidding place, hidden behind high
stone walls, though many people spoke of the care within as exemplary. It
was, however, known locally as a place where patients ‘went in at the front
door, and out at the back’. The high walls were subsequently reduced to
a smaller boundary perimeter wall of stone piers interspersed with long
stretches of railings, which now offer passers-by views of the original con-
vent, hospital and modern hospice additions, fronted by a pretty terraced
garden, with a figure of Christ as supplicant and healer centred in the gar-
dens looking towards the hospital windows.
St Patrick’s Hospital and Marymount Hospice was the first hospice in
Ireland and was established in 1870 as St Patrick’s Hospital for Incurables,
administered and staffed by the Religious Sisters of Charity. Many of the
early patients suffered from various cancers, some of whom were operated
on successfully but most died in care. However, some lived for several years
beyond expectancy, owing to the ‘care received and the rest enjoyed’.1 The
good reputation of the hospital inspired successors elsewhere: Our Lady’s
Hospice in Dublin was opened in 1879, and St Joseph’s Hospice in Hackney,
The brief is everything 	4

-- 70 of 203 --

56 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g
London, in 1905. The latter of course was a spiritual and an architectural
inspiration for Cicely Saunders when she went on to establish St Christo-
pher’s in Sydenham in 1967, and so the influence of ideas and designs con-
tinues onwards, from one hospice to the next, and one decade to another.
The design of St Patrick’s Hospital for Incurables was solid and
plain-speaking: a four-storey, continuous terraced frontage, containing a
number of communal wards within, together with medical rooms, kitchens,
and service rooms. The repetitive pattern of the Georgian windows and
neat brickwork suggested an almshouse rather than a religious or medical
institution – once the visitor had penetrated beyond the high perimeter
walls. This new kind of hospital was busy from the start. Six years after
opening, it was reported that 190 patients were being treated in the hospital,
including 51 children in separate wards. This provision continued for more
than a century, and the hospice became a familiar part of Cork’s medi-
cal and pastoral life, with the work of the staff increasingly supported and
funded by a growing number of volunteers.
In 1982, an extension was added to provide more single patient rooms,
for which there was increasing demand. Even so, the extension was still not
designed to provide for more ‘retreat rooms’ or ‘sanctuary spaces’ where
families in distress could gather, or places where the nursing staff could
seek peace and quiet at difficult times in their day, one of the particular
needs of this type of care institution. Nor was there adequate additional
provision for therapy rooms, visitor accommodation, or places where chil-
dren could be taken to play or kept busy while their parents or carers took
turns spending time with patients. Much of the funding came from vol-
untary donations, supported by intense fund-raising activities by a large
band of volunteers, many of whom had been recruited from the friends and
families of patients who had been cared for in their final days.
In 2001, senior staff and board members of what by now had become
known as St Patrick’s Hospital & Marymount Hospice began to consider
creating a brand-new hospice on or close to the existing one, a hospice
designed to meet the new standards recommended for palliative care in
Ireland. This was to be a hospice offering not only exemplary medical and
spiritual care but provided in a building which was beautiful and pur-
pose-built in its own right, offering a more supportive environment for
patients, staff, visitors, and volunteers than had been available in the orig-
inal building and its more recent extension. The new building was to be
one which incorporated all the most modern ideas on providing care for
the terminally ill – particularly children – as well as meeting the changing
needs of people for greater personal privacy, dignity in their bodily care,
and with more personalised provision for the families of patients at this
time of great stress in their lives.
This was an architectural challenge as well as a medical and spiritual
challenge, for the functions now embedded in the services, care and treat-
ment provided by hospices are many, varied, and replete with sensitive and
difficult issues of separating conflicting demands between medical obser-
vation and privacy, pastoral care and medical treatment, the separate needs
of patients and visitors, staff and volunteers, the needs of the body and the

-- 71 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	57
needs of the mind and spirit, happiness and grief, and the rituals and pro-
cessionals associated with both the treatment of the bodies of the living, and
the bodies of those who have just died.
The modern hospice presents architects, designers and landscape archi-
tects with one of the most complex building types now being demanded by
modern societies. Yet even today it is still felt that, as Belgian researchers
continue to observe: ‘It is a rather new building type, for which architects
have few historical references, post-occupancy evaluations, or direct expe-
riences available’. 2 For dying increasingly happens within public settings,
yet those settings are required to offer greater and greater personal pri-
vacy, personal attention, and close attention to spiritual as well as physical
well-being, as cultural attitudes change.
The story of how those administering St Patrick’s sat down to create a
new hospice to meet the needs of the residents of Cork – to raise funds,
think about the location and design for the new building, work out a sched-
ule of accommodation and building needs, arrange to visit modern hospices
in other parts of Ireland and beyond, and to embody all their aspirations in
a coherent architectural and design brief – provided a helpful case study in
the first edition of this book. For it exemplified one of the key issues of this
and any other successful construction project: the development of a com-
prehensive and imaginative architectural brief.
The quality and design of the buildings used by local hospices – adapted,
refurbished or entirely new – is a matter of great importance and concern,
not only in Ireland and the UK, but in many other countries too. A pio-
neering audit of the projected building requirements of the hospice sector
in the UK was undertaken by Rachel Bucknall, a former Care Director at
St Richard’s Hospice in Worcester, UK, then working for the architectural
practice, One Creative Environments.3 Questionnaires were sent to Chief
Executives of over 240 hospices, resulting in 146 replies, an exceptional
60% response rate suggesting how important building and design issues
had become. When asked how old the building currently occupied was,
original construction dates ranged from 1780 to 2006. Quite a few however
were built in the mid-1970s, supporting the view that the hospice movement
in Britain really gathered pace in that decade. Then there was a widespread
development of new hospice buildings, a number of which were modelled
architecturally on Our Lady’s Wing at St Joseph’s in Hackney, completed
in 1957, with its distinctive floor to ceiling windows, or St Christopher’s in
Sydenham, with its sawtooth, glazed facade.
When hospice executives were asked ‘Does your current building fulfil
your patient/family and carer needs?’, 86 out of 130 of respondents said
‘No’. When asked ‘Do you have plans within the next five years to under-
take redevelopment/redesign?’, 92 out of 117 who replied to this question
said ‘Yes’. When questioned further on this, 36 respondents claimed to be
at project planning stage, 33 at needs assessment stage, 21 at design stage,
18 were now fund-raising, 17 had commissioned feasibility studies, and 11
were now engaged in construction. From these figures it is clear that the
hospice movement in the UK was then engaged in a continuing building
programme.

-- 72 of 203 --

58 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
Yet, if the strength of the hospice movement derives from its local energy
and ambition – as well as an astonishing ability to raise funds – one possible
weakness is that there is still too little experience in preparing design briefs,
or working with architects and building contractors. There is nothing unu-
sual here, for the same is also true of the well-established public sector. As
the distinguished US health care academic, Professor Roger Ulrich once
noted, even within the mainstream National Health Service in the UK,
79% of hospital trusts at the time ‘had little or no in-house experience of
constructing briefs for development’.4
It is not only hospice trusts which may lack architectural or design
experience. In the course of researching this book, several projects were
encountered where architects commissioned to design new hospice build-
ings seemed not only to lack any real understanding of the unique values of
the hospice movement but showed no evidence of learning from the expe-
riences of any other hospices in the UK, let alone abroad, for guidance or
deeper understanding as to how they might better fulfil their commissions
properly.
Those involved in preparing architectural briefs could be greatly aided
by having access to the ‘post-occupancy evaluation’ studies of hospice
buildings already completed. Alas such invaluable studies are too rarely
undertaken. Luckily one such evaluation of the new building and its
teething troubles was conducted amongst staff, patients and volunteers of
the New Farleigh Hospice in Chelmsford, Essex. Details as to precisely
what users were most or least satisfied with – in how the new building
was working – provided a good guide to early remediation of teething
problems.
The New Farleigh Hospice team had themselves visited a number of
other hospices while preparing their own brief. While gratefully acknowl-
edging the great help and encouragement they had received in their visit,
they nevertheless recorded that,
We were struck by the lack of systematic evaluation (of other hos-
pice building projects) which meant that our learning tended to be
heavily influenced by the discipline/profession of our host on each
visit. We therefore determined that we would carry out a systematic
evaluation of our building three months after occupation so that we
would:
1 	fully determine the extent to which we had met the needs of users
2 	determine plans where possible for action to resolve any issues
3 	offer a well-rounded experience and evaluation report to any col-
leagues who might approach us for advice. 5
If more hospice projects included in their original brief a requirement for a
proper ‘post-occupancy evaluation’, then there would be much useful infor-
mation and advice to share amongst others – as well as being an invaluable
learning tool for themselves.

-- 73 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	59
‘The brief is everything’
At St Patrick’s Hospital and Marymount Hospice in Cork, the Chief Exec-
utive and the Senior Nurse Planner, said when I first interviewed them
that they had been developing the brief for the new hospice for over six
years already. The Project Team consisted of 12 members, from the exist-
ing Hospice, the Southern Health Board (later Health Service Executive
South) and the Irish government’s Department of Health and Children.
This hospice – as is evident from the composition of the Project Team –
was to be largely funded from the national purse. Several years were spent,
inter alia, on an options appraisal of possible sites for the new hospice. A site
was finally agreed upon in 2004, and only then could the design brief be
outlined in site-specific detail.
Prior to, and during the preparation of the Brief, members of the Project
Team visited a number of hospices and hospitals in Europe, including:
Our Lady’s Hospice, Harold’s Cross, Dublin
The Marie Curie Centre, Liverpool, England
The Marie Curie Centre, Newcastle, England
St. Oswald’s Hospice, Newcastle, England
St. Margaret’s Hospice, Clydebank, Scotland
Cliften Nursing Home, Belfast
Saffron House, Bristol, England
Ilford Park Polish Home, Devon, England
Trynwalden, Friedland, Holland
Ros-Anders Gard, Stockholm, Sweden
Vidarkliniken, Jarna, Sweden
Wilhelmiina, Helsinki, Finland
Apart from coming back with a very strong sense of what they liked and
disliked – in terms of appearance, design, ambience, ethos, and organi-
sational culture – the group also returned with a very detailed schedule
of specific functions, spaces, connections, and measurements required
for the new building schedule. Only by aggregating the many functions
and sizes to create a formal schedule of accommodation, could the chosen
architects begin to get a sense of the scale and complexities of the design
needed – and if and how this could be accommodated most effectively on
the chosen site.
It is here that one realises that in modern hospital and healthcare
buildings – including hospices – the plan really is the generator of the
design. As has already been observed, well into the 19th century many such
buildings were designed from the outside, according to conventions as to
what a hospital should look like to the public beyond, or in accordance with
a historic building type. The hospital or hospice of today has to start from
the needs required within, with a programme of functions as well as a hier-
archy of spaces and places to meet both the practical and spiritual require-
ments of the final building.

-- 74 of 203 --

60 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
Throughout this long gestation period, there was much discussion of
ideas and plans amongst hospice staff, architects, Health Board officials and
others. As Professor Bryan Lawson has written on this process, the idea
that there is a straightforward linear progression in the development of an
architectural brief, from drafting an initial set of ideas to analysing the pro-
gramme and then synthesising a definitive plan, is without credibility. It
is, he suggests, like playing a game of chess, ‘…with a board that has no
divisions into cells, has pieces that can be invented and redefined as the
game proceeds and rules that change their effects as moves are made’. 6 In
short, it is an iterative, reflexive process, whereby every new idea or re-
configuration of functions or spaces, changes everything else once again.
The layout of a hospice is noticeably fraught with problems of ‘adja-
cencies’: ensuring that compatible activities are located closely together,
and incompatible ones kept apart. Locating a family rest room next to a
mortuary, or a sluice room next to a kitchen, is obviously unacceptable.
Resolving auditory, olfactory, visual and hygienic boundaries in such
buildings is a major challenge. In a hospice the gradations of compati-
ble and incompatible services and functions are particularly sensitive,
including as they do issues not only of use but of orientation (do most
bedrooms face onto gardens, as well as receiving maximum benefit from
the daylight sun?), controlling and separating out noise, smells, scenes of
public distress, the movement of corpses, and so on. Not only does the
whole have to be greater than the architectural sum of the parts – out of
this collocation of medical and palliative services housed within – but
something uplifting and spiritual has also to emerge to inhabit the spaces
and places of the building.
Furthermore, allowance has to be made for possible expansion at some
future date, and so an understanding of the capacity of the site to absorb
expansion – without destroying the designed landscape – has also to be
undertaken. This has implications for the overall design itself, as it is much
harder to add on or enlarge a building or suite of buildings that has been
designed to appear complete and of itself, than to add new elements to a
free assemblage of elements. It is for this reason that James and Noakes call
for an ‘urban design’ approach to hospital architecture, though the same
approach could also be used for those new hospices which may be being
developed in existing urban quarters.7
The Cork brief
At the proposed new hospice in Cork, the facilities to be accommodated in
the proposed new building were to be divided into three groups: hospice
elements, hospital elements, and shared elements. Here, it should be noted
that although called a hospice, the new campus did incorporate provision
for a large number of frail elderly people with high-dependency needs, a
group not commonly included in the client group of most hospices, espe-
cially in the UK. The opening preamble to the technical brief is quoted at
length, as it clearly illustrates how hospice design is as much a concern with

-- 75 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	61
creating an ethos of care and emotional well-being, as it is a schedule of
institutional services and functions.
Hospice Elements
The hospice was to be made up of the following elements:
(a) 	A 44-bedded inpatient unit to facilitate the care of patients
requiring specialist palliative care, including rehabilitation and
end-of-life care.
(b) 	A base from which the community-based service for the region
was to be coordinated and from where three community-based
teams (of four nurses, with the support of physiotherapists, occu-
pational therapists and medical social workers) would operate.
(c) 	A day care unit for specialist palliative care purposes that would
include outpatient facilities.
(d) 	Medical services.
(e) 	Administration.
Hospital Elements:
The hospital was to include the following elements:
(a) 	A 75-bedded inpatient unit to facilitate care of frail elderly
patients, most of whom will be of high dependency.
(b) 	Day activities area.
(c) 	Medical services.
(d) 	Administration.
Shared Services:
(a) 	Rehabilitation area (accommodating physiotherapy, occupational
therapy, and speech and language therapy infrastructure).
(b) 	Bereavement & Family Support Service.
(c) 	Chaplaincy Service.
(d) 	Pharmacy.
(e) 	An Education Centre.
(f) 	Catering Service.
(g) Administration.
(h) Maintenance Service.
(i) 	Housekeeping Service.
(j) 	Laundry.
(k) 	Portering Service.
(l) 	Volunteers.
(m) Security.
(n) 	Mortuary.
Some difficult, potentially controversial, decisions were made early on
during the preparation of the design brief, notably in the decision to insist
upon single-room accommodation for all patients in the hospice accommo-
dation, even though government preferences at the time were for a mixture
of single and multi-bedded rooms. The Project Team were convinced by

-- 76 of 203 --

62 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
evidence from North America that single-room accommodation was going
to become the norm throughout the world. This issue will be discussed
later.
The following edited extracts from the design brief establishes the tone
of what the Project Team were hoping to achieve:
General Principles for Hospice Design
Implications for Hospice Planning
 Environment. The hospice (and campus) must be designed so as to
create a warm, welcoming, open and bright environment. It must
create a sense of openness and light, with as many ‘normalising’
cues as possible. It must offer peace, serenity, and safety, in an envi-
ronment that is reassuring, non-threatening and healing.
 Home v Hospital. A hospice must create a ‘homely’ type of envi-
ronment, yet it is not home. We are not designing a large ‘home’ but
an acute health care facility that must be capable of dealing with a
complex array of medical and nursing problems.
 Patient Needs. The hospice building must be capable of support-
ing the entire range of specialist palliative care services, not just in
respect of current demand, but for the foreseeable future. In this
regard, we must consider the specific needs of patients, their fami-
lies, and staff members.
Ambulatory Care. Many patients will attend the day unit first.
This may take the form of an outpatient assessment, or day unit
attendance for therapeutic intervention or diversional therapy. Again,
the entrance and waiting areas are very important and will require a
separate entrance for ambulatory activities. Domestic type cues such
as flowers, armchairs, coffee tables, soothing lighting, artwork, etc,
will all help to offer reassurance.
Wards. In the ward areas, all patient accommodation should be in
single rooms. These rooms must be bright, airy and capable of accom-
modating a large number of visitors during the day. Nursing staff must
be able to access both sides of the bed with ease and without shifting
furniture. The rooms must accommodate with ease the range of equip-
ment that will be needed to care for critically ill, high-dependency
patients.
A hospice is a very special place, where patients and families live
lives that are special. In this context, time is precious because it is
scarce and clearly finite. People live until they die and it is the job of
the hospice to support and enable each patient to live their life as fully
as possible. The hospice building must be sympathetic to, and sup-
portive of, our best efforts. The building must be planned to the finest
detail, because we cannot afford to get it wrong.
These introductory protocols to the design brief have been cited at length
as they so clearly articulate the core values of the hospice movement as it
would wish to find expression in a built form. The brief starts from evoking

-- 77 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	63
an atmosphere, along with a set of values, before proceeding on to the tech-
nical details of how this atmosphere is to be achieved. Hospices are first and
foremost places and settings where emotions can be orchestrated creatively
by good design (and even on a ‘minute-to-minute’ temporal basis as far as
Ian Clarke was concerned), hopefully alluding to a greater sense and under-
standing of the intangible mysteries of life and its passing. They have the
potential to become amongst the most important new spiritual settings of a
generally more secular (and increasingly medicalised) world.
The Cork brief was not only for a hospice, of course. The Project Team
were keen to emphasise through the external look and design of the cam-
pus, that this was not solely a residential ‘home’, but a place where acute
medical treatment was also offered. Selecting the architects involved adver-
tising a design brief, for which 22 different firms applied, of which 6 were
short-listed for interview. These six gave 20-minute presentations each, and
from them two distinct architectural practices who had prepared a joint
bid were chosen. The firm, Scott Tallon Walker were selected for their
well-established track record in Ireland in designing larger-scale hospital
buildings, and the UK firm Jane Darbyshire & David Kendall Architects
( JDDK) were chosen to work with them, on the basis of their expertise
in designing detailed, intimate hospice buildings and interiors. Both firms
collaborated equally on the overall design as well as the landscaping and
work started on site in Autumn 2008.
The hospice opens: squeezing the very last drop
from life
The wholly new Marymount University Hospital and Hospice opened in
September 2011 on an 11-acre greenfield site in Curraheen on the west-
ern outskirts of Cork in Ireland at a cost of £56 million, half of which was
acquired by voluntary fund-raising. 8 Interviewed a year after opening the
then CEO, Kevin O’Dwyer, was full of praise for what had been achieved:
The architects (Scott Tallon Walker and JDDK) did a fantastic job in
getting light into the building. We wanted to dispel the sense of fore-
boding people have when they come to a hospice. People associate it
with darkness and bad news. We wanted a hotel feel to the place so that
when people come in, it has a welcoming and relaxing feeling.
The new facility greatly increased the number of beds for hospice care
for Cork city and county. It was originally planned that there would be
44 hospice beds, with a further 63 beds for care of the elderly, the major-
ity of patients inhabiting a single bedroom with en-suite facilities. Initially
economic circumstances dictated that only 24 beds could open. However
additional funds were made available, and the second specialist palliative
care ward opened in 2014, so that the original 44 beds in two specialist
wards came to pass. As Dr Marie Murphy, one of two consultants working
in Marymount Hospice, has said:

-- 78 of 203 --

64 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
It has been fantastic to move to the new facility and be able to look
after people in single rooms. What we are trying to do here is squeeze
the very last drop from life. We try to get people as physically well as
possible (but) people need spiritual support as well.
The 107 patients are accommodated in five wards centred around an atrium
which acts as the unifying heart of the building: ‘crai’ in Irish. The scheme
takes advantage of rural views and interaction with the natural environ-
ment of which, with its organic philosophy, it now forms part. The form of
the plan ensures all bedrooms have sun for some of the day, with the longer
stay elderly care bedrooms having south-easterly outlook which is particu-
larly beneficial. Within this overall format, the design of individual bed-
rooms has been a key issue both in terms of its quality of environment for
patients and its operational functionality. Each bedroom has either direct
access to a garden terrace, or to a balcony overlooking landscaped gardens.
In addition, the building provides accommodation for a range of specialist
services including the hub for community outreach palliative care and a
specialist education centre with auditorium. Such was the interest in the
new building that more than 1800 people signed up for a tour of the campus
shortly before it opened.
In October 2022, I interviewed Dr Sarah McCloskey, CEO of Mary-
mount University Hospital and Hospice, who had first worked at the old
Saint Patrick’s Hospital and Marymount Hospice as Director of Nursing in
2010. She led all clinical elements of the move to the new campus – which
opened on 17 September 2011 – including involvement in most tendering
processes, staff training and patient/family liaison, three years before she
was appointed CEO in 2014. The change of atmosphere she described as
‘dramatic’. The old building had been beautiful in its way, with its rich

with core and radial wings.

heritage and home to the hospice Sisters, and with a fine location over-
looking the city of Cork, but in other ways had outlined its purpose. The
building for some reflected more difficult and sad times of poverty, poor
health and taboos about ‘incurability’ and dying.
The modern, medicalised purpose-built architecture gave hope and
a promise of the very best of care, irrespective of diagnosis or age. She
recalled that when the first patients were transferred with their families
and with staff from the old building to the new, staff cheered as they arrived
one by one before escorting them to their brand-new single rooms. The

66 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
modern design and facilities are just as important to the staff as they are
to patients and their families, providing a positive, more productive and
supportive environment, which poor, inadequate buildings fail to do. Staff
turnover is extremely low in Marymount, and Dr McCloskey believes from
experience that palliative care nurses find much more that is meaningful in
their work than those in general nursing. ’Hospices’, she told me, ‘typically
have a compassionate and caring culture – important values for nurses’.
Dr McCloskey believes from experience, that ‘single-room nursing is a
very different type of nursing from ward-based nursing’, and for staff and
patients this was a major shift in the culture of care. ‘It was somewhat eas-
ier to keep an eye on patients in a ward, and quite often patients, or their
families or visitors, would also keep an eye on other patients, exchanging a
few words of interest or greetings’. Nevertheless, in a public ward, patients
had to share toilet and bath facilities, and sometimes be witness to each oth-
er’s intimate frailties, conversations and traumas, whereas there is far more
personal and bodily dignity in a room of one’s own, with en-suite ameni-
ties and privacy of conversation. Dr McCloskey is impressed by the design
of the single-room complexes, though in retrospect thinks that putting
Figure 4.4 Interior first floor atrium at Marymount University Hospital and
Hospice.

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	67
patient accommodation on two floors slightly disadvantaged those not on
the ground floor – where the rooms enjoy direct access to the gardens –
without the need for wheelchairs or the necessity to use lifts. She thinks it
is important that patients can get out into the gardens and grounds if they
can, as ‘our ultimate goal in palliative care is to create comfort and enable
people to carry on living’.
Enjoying a greenfield site in farmland, the hospital and hospice campus
is nevertheless very much part of Cork still, only 2 km from the city centre
and providing the end bus stop to the popular No 8 bus. The patient catch-
ment for those needing specialist palliative care is around 550,000, with
around 500 patients receiving palliative care at home or in nursing homes
at any one time, as well as delivering palliative care expertise in Cork acute
hospitals and enabling complex inpatient care on site in the 44 patient beds,
as inpatients or at home at any one time. ‘Today we are dealing with over
3000 families each year in all capacities’, Dr McCloskey says in conclusion,
and with pride.
New Hospice of St Francis, Berkhamsted, UK
This purpose-built hospice opened in January 2007. A ‘Clinical Design
Statement’ was drawn up by the Medical Director of St Francis, in antici-
pation of the construction of the new one. The new building occupies the
site of a former brickworks in a rural setting near Berkhamsted, at the end
of a narrow country lane. The Design Statement encapsulates the ethos and
intention of the building, rather than the detail of its floor plans and public
elevations, as is evident in the following extracts:
As with the current building (the former St Francis Hospice), the new
building places all of the patient care on the ground floor – a smaller
courtyard for the inpatient unit with the main courtyard providing
day-care and outpatients. The main reception is located within the
main courtyard, adjacent to the carpark. The farmhouse appearance
will create a domestic and welcome feel for all patients and visitors
when they arrive at the Hospice. The kitchen and dining areas are
also at the heart of the new hospice – a central feature of our service.
The main reception will provide access for all visitors, outpatients,
day patients and staff. Only disabled and ambulance transfers will go
directly to the Inpatient Unit. The sickest patients, in the inpatient
beds, will all have personal access to the garden and the aspect of each
bedroom has been carefully chosen for patient comfort. For those
patients who are simply not well enough to be in the garden, the design
will bring the outdoors directly to them…
The Hospice garden is a crucial aspect of our care. Many people
value the garden as an escape, and a therapy from the anguish in the
building. The grounds will be carefully landscaped to offer appropri-
ate havens and views for our inpatients and day patients, getting the
best from the natural beauty of the site…

-- 82 of 203 --

68 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
As the internal form has been dictated by the detail of the clinical
function and the ambience we are trying to create, the external design
has been developed to fit with the rural setting of the site. The con-
cept has been to design a collection of traditional buildings, which add
definition to the different functions of our care and move away from
a monolithic modern structure, which we were keen to avoid. Tradi-
tional timber and brick finishes will be used – we have actually identi-
fied a brick very similar to that which would have been manufactured
at the original brickworks. We believe our concept will fit well with the
site, with the mixture of adjacent buildings (nursery school, haulage
yard and golf driving range).9
The Medical Director and other senior staff worked closely with a Project
Manager and the selected contractor to ensure that the spirit of the brief
was fully embodied in the new campus. The hospice is now working well
with few teething problems and fits into its rural landscape comfortably.
Other design guidelines
More general design guidelines for hospices and palliative care settings
have been drawn up nationally in recent years. In 2004, the Irish govern-
ment’s Department of Health and Children published its Design Guidelines
for Specialist Palliative Care Settings. This was largely a schedule of services
and functions now to be expected in a modern palliative care setting,
though it opened with some thoughtful observations on the role that archi-
tects might play:
A hospice is a busy place that supports a great deal of activity; yet it
must also be a calm, safe and tranquil place that facilitates quiet per-
sonal reflection. A hospice must be able to meet the needs of men and
women, young and old, in-patients and out-patients, patients and fam-
ilies, those with cancer and those with other conditions, those who will
be discharged home and those who will not, paid staff and volunteers.
A hospice is a place where people can live, truly live, until they die.
The design of a hospice unit is an extremely complex, challenging
and rewarding undertaking. It does not simply involve the design of a
building. It involves creating a most special place that will support the
efforts of countless thousands of patients, families and staff over many
years.10
In the UK, NHS Estates, a department of the government-run National
Health Service, issued some general guidelines for those working in pal-
liative care: A place to die with dignity: Creating a supportive environment (2005).
This was intended not to set down architectural or design principles so
much as to stimulate debate about what kind of environments were needed
to support patients, staff and families close to the time of death. It acknowl-
edged that in the wider health service death could occur in a number of

-- 83 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	69
places: an ambulance, A&E Department, multi-bedded ward, operating
theatre, or even in a corridor on the way from one health facility to another.
Whatever the physical context, the skills of all staff in dealing with death
were paramount, though too few had been adequately trained for this.
In a short guide to ‘Creating a strategic brief’, the NHS Estates paper
broke the physical context down into three main elements: building (shell
and services); fit-out (scenery): and settings (fine-tuning), elaborating upon
the requirements of each. This was a short introductory discussion docu-
ment for internal use only, but it did establish some key parameters for the
whole health service. The UK Department of Health Estates and Facili-
ties Division (formerly NHS Estates) together with The King’s Fund have
collaborated on a successful refurbishment programme called Enhancing the
Healing Environment Programme 2003–2005 (2006). The report details 23 case
studies in hospital and hospice improvements to existing buildings, using
imaginative art, architectural, design and landscape initiatives – often at
relatively low cost considering the inspiring results achieved. It is now
regarded as an exemplary programme.
In North America the Hospice Education Institute published its Hospice
Design Manual For In-Patient Facilities in 2006, written by British-born archi-
tect Timothy Moorhouse, and based on many visits to hospice in the UK
and the USA. This is precisely what it says: a schematic A – Z design manual
which also contains a number of helpful flow charts illustrating the various
sequences of use of buildings and facilities by patients, visitors, staff, and
other professionals from arrival to departure. It also outlines floor plans based
on principal zoning areas, along with templates for connecting the different
hospice elements with minimum disruption or conflict. Of particular interest
is a list of commonly found problems reported by staff at the hospices visited
by Moorhouse in his researches, the most notable of which were:
 	Inadequate storage space allocated, particularly for large equipment.
 	Integrating the living room with patient rooms, if it is to be used.
 	Over-long corridors.
 	Difficulty for visitors in finding the main entrance.
 	Reception areas too large with too little seating.
 	Open plan administration areas noisy and lacking privacy.
 	Inadequate security for staff at night, along with clear night entry
arrangements for visitors.
 	Large ‘shopping mall’ type parking lots.
Moorhouse also makes the eminently sensible suggestion that all briefs
should at least consider the possibility of later enlargement or additions,
and how these would be integrated were they to be commissioned. The
manual is an excellent A – Z checklist of design issues to consider, though
it periodically evinces a slightly different attitude to the status of the body
after death (something to be whisked away quietly and quickly it seems in
American hospices), in comparison to the more ritualistic traditions asso-
ciated with the newly dead still evident in many British hospices, and cer-
tainly most of those in Ireland.

-- 84 of 203 --

70 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
In June 2008 the Hospice Friendly Hospitals Programme at the Irish
Hospice Foundation working with architect, Ian Clarke, published its Design
& Dignity Guidelines for Physical Environments supporting End-of-Life Care, an
illustrated guide to all aspects of designing high quality environment for all
end-of-life care wherever it takes place, a clearly written and attractively
illustrated aide-memoire for all those involved in building and refurbishment
programmes. This could be read and applied in all care contexts, and is
admirably concise and thought through, from generic principles to detailed
application.
Programme for The Good Hospice in Denmark
The first fully developed national set of design principles for hospices
has now been published in Denmark.11 It was developed in response to
the rapid growth of the hospice movement at the beginning of the 21st
century when, ‘never before have so many hospice buildings been on the
Danish drawing-board as there are now’.12 Yet, the hospice movement was
late in coming to Denmark, where it was often said that ‘“hospitals of
death” did not fit into the Danish mentality’. The first Danish hospice,
Sankt Lukas, was opened in 1992, and in December 2005 there were seven
hospices already operating, with more being planned on a county-by-
county basis. In December 2004, the Danish Parliament passed legislation
obliging all counties to develop plans for a minimum of 12 hospice beds
in each county.
The ‘Programme’ is explicitly about the commissioning, planning,
and design of the building element (usually referred to as ‘the physical
framework’).
The programme for The Good Hospice in Denmark is aimed at consultants,
decision makers, architects, engineers, project leaders and steering
groups for local hospice projects, and it is intended to serve as an inspi-
ration, manual and checklist to ensure that sufficient thought has been
given to the intention, function and expression of the physical frame-
work in connection with planning and establishing new hospices.13
A statistical profile of hospice patients drawn up in 2004 revealed that the
average stay in a hospice in Denmark was 25 nights, the average age of
patients around 67, slightly more women than men, and 95% of patients
diagnosed with cancer. There were at this time no hospices designed spe-
cifically for children. The report draws government attention to the signif-
icant increase in the proportion of the population who will be elderly, many
of whom in the latter years of their extended lives will be suffering from
chronic illnesses linked to heart, lung, kidney, and neurological conditions
as well as cancer. In other words, ‘the potential to expand palliative care
and make it more available to patients suffering from incurable, chronic
diseases is vast’.14 It can be already seen how this top-down overview of
national need and provision differs greatly from the culture of hospice

-- 85 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	71
provision in the UK and Ireland, for example, where provision is still being
provided as an act of local concern and initiative. At some point in the near
future these two trajectories may well meet.
The Danish report is based on a number of visits to hospices in Denmark
and the UK, and a detailed summary of the functional elements required
by each, as well as thorough investigation of what patients, families and staff
mean by the ethos and ambience of a successful hospice building. It con-
cludes with a schematic set of design for the ‘ideal implementation process’
in the commissioning of new hospice buildings. At the heart of this pro-
cess is the establishment of a board of interested parties right at the outset,
including public authorities, architects, and engineers, financial and legal
advisers, volunteers, relatives, landscapes architects, artists, support organ-
isations. This leads eventually to the creation of a professional building
committee, and then to the election of a client consultant to act as project
manager. Meanwhile fund-raising continues, links and study visits are made
to other hospices, arrangements are made to inform and consult the local
population, and processes are developed for possible architectural com-
petitions and invitations for competitive tendering for construction itself.
The whole process is broken down into clear stages under three rubrics:
Interested Parties; External Activities; Internal Activities. The process is
Figure 4.5 Ankomsten (‘Arrival’): montage designed by Signal Architects,
Copenhagen, for the Realdania report on ‘The Good Hospice in Denmark.’

-- 86 of 203 --

72 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
reflexive and continually being monitored for feedback and adjustments to
the design, as new information or new needs emerge.
Establishing a dedicated board or working party of key interests, whose
skills, expertise and experience represent medical and nursing staff, patients
and their families, funding bodies, architects and designers, is clearly the
first priority. From that everything else flows, if not naturally, then cer-
tainly organisationally and in terms of wider consultation.
Maggie’s architectural brief
The Maggie’s Cancer Caring Centres have attracted widespread architec-
tural and public interest, though unlike hospices or hospitals involved in
palliative care, they are not residential buildings. Nevertheless, real and
significant thought was given to their design by Maggie Keswick Jencks
herself, to whose memory they refer. Her husband, the late Charles Jencks,
continued to be centrally involved in the architectural and fund-raising
programme, and himself designed several of the gardens. It is extraordinary
how coming from a different tradition entirely – spontaneous, humanistic,
emotional as well as intellectual – so many common concerns are shared
with the lessons hospices have learned themselves about the critical impor-
tance of design.
The introduction to the Maggie’s Architectural Brief states that:
We need our buildings to feel safe and welcoming. They need to be
small, and domestic in scale. On the other hand these little buildings
should not pat you on the head, patronise you by being too cosy. They
should rise to the occasion, just as you, the person needing help, is
having to rise to the occasion of one of the most difficult challenges
any of us is likely to have to face. At the very least they should raise
your spirits.15
The architectural brief itself is precise, and specific in its spatial and func-
tional details: a building of approximately 280 square metres, a welcoming
entrance (no reception desk), a large sitting room area with information and
small library provision, from which the rest of the building can be viewed,
closely linked to a kitchen area with a large table to sit 12 people. The
table, fireplace, and constant supply of tea and coffee, all combine to create
a domestic ‘hearth and home’ or ‘kitchen table domesticity’ in the main
ground floor area.16 Everything else is subsidiary: office, meeting rooms,
counselling rooms, toilets, rest room. The garden is vital and it is hoped that
there would be ‘a continuous flow between house and garden space’.
Sociologist Daryl Martin undertook a pioneering and invaluable eth-
nographic study of Maggie’s staff, volunteers and visitors, and how they
perceived and used the common space of each Maggie’s Centre, published
in 2017.17 He firstly draws analogies with the ‘affordances of cafes’, in their
informal, come and go sociability, allowing both privacy and companiona-
bility as is needed at any one time. He understandably focuses on the kitchen

-- 87 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	73
table as the emotional heart of the building, comparing this re-location as
being revolutionary in terms of traditional domestic architecture with its
‘design conventions that situate the kitchen as an inward-looking room to
the back of domestic sites’. This re-location rescinds the 20th century deci-
sion to displace the kitchen as the historic locus of working-class life and
sociability, in favour of a separate dining room on the grounds of hygiene
and moral improvement (and in doing so render the women in the kitchen
invisible yet again). Martin goes on to itemise the signs of informality in the
absence of staff uniforms, badges, notice-boards, timetables, rotas, and even
clocks. The ‘logic of choice’ now the mantra of public health provision, is
replaced by ‘the logic of care’.
Nevertheless, life around the kitchen table is discreetly managed and
supported by staff trained in group work and inter-personal relationships.
As a staff member in one Centre told Martin:
If you leave the kitchen table to its own devices it will grow arms and
legs and it will come back to bite you on the bum. You have to manage
the kitchen table, it requires facilitation … There needs to be a sense
of freedom about it, but if you are not there to make sure that things are
staying relevant then they can quickly go off on a tangent.18
Echoing this emphasis on the domestic is this extract from an ethnographic
account of a small Belgian hospice cited earlier:
Other aspects of care – Also aspects related to mental, social and emo-
tional care are inspired by a homelike atmosphere and supported by
spatial cues. At the entrance of the building, like at home, there is no
reception desk. Instead, caregivers leave the office or kitchen and greet
visitors face to face, often guiding them personally to their destination.
Relatives are invited to pick up coffee in the kitchen, as an implicit
invite to come in and talk. It is a very small space and volunteers
comment that the impractical layout makes people feel as if they are
standing in the way. At the same time, it is exactly this small scale that
offers intimacy. Volunteer RC1: “the kitchen is an amazing place […]
we should keep that, a place people spontaneously come to, and where
there’s always someone present” Volunteer RC3: “they almost crawl
into the pots and pans, and then the whole story comes out, because if
you make it too big, where also others can sit, I don’t think they’ll tell
their story”.19
Despite the minimal requirements of the brief – in technical terms at least –
the different response by different architects have been extraordinary. Per-
haps the key insight developed by the Maggie’s programme is that cosiness
and domesticity are not enough at this most difficult time in a person’s life,
and that in Amanda Levete’s words, design must ‘bring a little magic to the
place’. Though few people now believe that monumentalism is the most
appropriate response to the mystery of death, neither is total domesticity.
Many of those using Maggie’s Centres are in the younger age range, and

-- 88 of 203 --

74 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
for them style and panache are still lifestyle elements which support their
self-confidence, which an over-emphasis on cosiness might undermine,
reinforcing the notion that they are no longer independent individuals with
lives to lead, but dependent clients or helpless patients.
Lessons for hospitals
Yet is this understanding of the importance of building space and qual-
ity appreciated in decision-making circles within the mainstream hospital
building sector? One architect commented in a private paper written for
the Hospice Friendly Hospitals seminar in Dublin mentioned earlier that,
Departmental briefs are very mean on space, and areas which do not
have defined clinical uses are minute – foyers, waiting rooms, inter-
view rooms are all too small. There are also very defined ratios of wall
to floor and wall to window, none of which contribute to good archi-
tectural quality … Good design needs space and daylight first and
good materials second. If you haven’t got the first, the second cannot
really solve the problems caused.
He was referring to national hospital design guidelines which were still
taking too little notice of the specific needs of caring for people towards
the end of their lives. It also brings into question the crucial relationship
between capital costs, revenue costs, and lifetime service, all of which var-
iables inter-act with each other. A high capital investment is capable of
delivering a building which lasts longer, is more easily adaptable to new
services and might even be cheaper to maintain in the long term, compared
with a cheap building which may produce continuing repair bills, high
annual maintenance costs and a much shorter life-span. These long-term
financial equations, or life cycle costs, really do have to be fore-grounded
in any project planning process. Sadly, still too often politicians and com-
missioning bodies still focus on the initial outlay costs – the capital sums
involved – leaving the legacy of high maintenance costs and poor quality
buildings to the next generation.
The same architect singled out services engineers in particular for giving
too little thought (or being too poorly briefed) to understand the impor-
tance which staff, patients and their families attach to the quality of, and
personal control over, heating, lighting and ventilation, especially in wards
or rooms in which people may spend the final weeks or days of their lives.
It is not surprising that in a consumer society, personal control of circum-
stances and private choices should now be ascendant – yet many hospitals
have yet to come to terms with this enormous cultural shift. Choice is now
inextricably linked up with ideas of self-worth and the liveliness of individ-
ual identity, as well as being an important ingredient in the avoidance of
unnecessary stress.
Nursing staff and patients (and their families), should be amongst the
first to be consulted in the drawing up of design briefs for refurbishments

-- 89 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	75
or new build proposals, especially with regard to the care of the terminally
ill or dying. This does not seem to be the case at present, certainly in the
mainstream hospital sector. However, it is not enough to consult on the
details; patients, families and staff need to be consulted on the larger vision,
raising expectations beyond matters of curtains or blinds, to real design
issues of circulation, privacy, access to natural light and uplifting views,
communal meeting places and other substantive design matters.
In the Hospice Suite in the Ersta Sjukhus in Stockholm, the Director
of Nursing described how – in the wholesale conversion of a set of tra-
ditional Nightingale wards into an independent hospice suite within the
hospital – the nursing staff insisted on having the final say over the design
and furnishing of the new suite of rooms and communal spaces. What was
achieved was an exquisite atmosphere and sense of hospitality like the very
best country hotel. In this sense the hospice offered something more than a
home, domestic in feel perhaps, but with a more powerful sense of honour,
dignity, and occasion.
For at the end of life, hospitals catering for the ageing and the terminally
ill also need to transform themselves into homes, offering a wider range of
spaces than the functional ward, patient room, or medical consulting room.
There will need to be rooms where patients and family can be taken to hear
the clinical diagnosis, and be given time and space (in calm surroundings)
in which to absorb the implications of bad news when it comes. There will
need to be informal gathering places and spaces where families can meet
and confer, and talk with care staff. Whether these places are called ‘quiet
rooms’ or ‘comfort rooms’, the status and dignity of such rooms need to be
respected at all times, otherwise they will quickly fill with administrative
detritus, as I so often saw.
There will also need to be guest rooms where close family or friends can
stay overnight if need be; some may be able to stay in the patient’s room if it
is big enough and can still guarantee the privacy of the patient. Basic cater-
ing facilities and telephone services need to be provided for those keeping
vigil. And, after death, there need to be respectful procedures and places
provided during which the deceased can be washed, dressed and laid out for
viewing by family and close friends and relatives.
The provision of these places and facilities is not expensive in capital
terms, and can make an enormous difference, but unless such family rooms,
ceremony rooms, or quiet rooms are respected in their own right, they will
quickly be colonised for administrative, medical or storage purposes, and
lose their rationale. They do not always need to be rooms. The design of
entrance halls, corridors, congregating areas, can offer opportunities for
providing niches, setbacks, alcoves, smaller, more intimate places, where
a table, chairs and tea-making facilities can be situated, not only providing
meeting places for families and friends, but also enlivening the appearance
of otherwise featureless corridors and halls.
One person at the Dublin seminar said that,
I would add that facilities for the dying must be inherently valued by
staff in order for them to prevail. To have rooms for the dying included

-- 90 of 203 --

76 	t H e B r i e f i s e V e r Y t H i n g
at the design stage is not in itself enough – you must embed the behav-
iour that will support the use of the room for its intended purpose very
early on when the building is occupied. This is our greatest challenge.
She was referring to the common default position in the use of apparently
‘unused space’ in many hospitals, which was to quickly fill them with
equipment, or convert them into cloakrooms or lunch-rooms, rather than
seeing the need for quiet sanctuary space as a basic need (and right of visi-
tors and patients). The experience of so-called ‘common rooms’ in many big
hospitals provides a case study in what can go wrong when design and use
are not effectively managed and monitored. Any visitor to a large hospital
will usually encounter on each floor an empty featureless room containing
a wide-screen television, often switched on though there is nobody there to
watch it, with a number of out-of-date lifestyle magazines scattered around,
plus one or two potted plants on a window ledge, unwatered and dying.
There may be one or two broken toys to add colour. These are negative
spaces, draining energy and hope from everywhere else in the building, and
undermining the ethos of care and rehabilitation.
Architects and designers can make an enormous difference to the culture
of the hospital. However, it was sobering to be told by one senior hospital
administrator in Norway that, ‘The problem is most architects are secular
and they simply don’t understand how deeply religious hospital buildings
can be’. This wasn’t a claim for overtly religious places and spaces, but it
was a claim for a strong sense of ritual, of processional, of sanctuary and
quietude in buildings and rooms that the newly born, the sick, the men-
tally distressed and the dying, inhabit in ways which we may never fully
understand – until it happens to us.
There is also the issue of time and time pressures on staff and resources,
especially in an era of hospital targets and bed managers. ‘Aren’t they dead
yet?’ has been heard in mock-ironic tones from a bed manager desperate to
find new beds for new patients in a busy general hospital. This was a com-
mon complaint in a number of hospitals, and was one of the reasons why
so many years ago Cicely Saunders said that St Christopher’s was called a
hospice because ‘there is no parking meter by the bed’. 20 As general hospi-
tals are managed to provide an even faster turnover of patients than ever
before, the culture of the institution is in danger of becoming pre-occupied
with bed management rather than looking after people. As was noted by one
participant in Focus Groups conducted at Our Lady of Lourdes Hospital,
Drogheda, ‘time for dying patients and their families is a real issue’.
One workshop member drew attention to the difference between the
ethos of the general hospital and that of the psychiatric hospital. In the case
of the latter, the patient is ideally given as long as they need to stay and be
cared for, whereas in hospitals the pressure for beds often means that time
and space for the patient is kept to an absolute minimum. The psychiatric
hospital is a sanctuary or asylum in its true sense of the word, the gen-
eral hospital a high turnover clinical machine. As general hospitals become
more like short-stay hotels, psychiatric units become more like residential
communities.

-- 91 of 203 --

t H e B r i e f i s e V e rY t H i n g 	77
Thus, there is much that mainstream hospitals – and indeed residential
care homes – can learn from the very detailed and serious thinking now
being given by individual hospice committees and boards to the design of
new buildings. In this sense hospices not only show the way in which end-
of-life care can be provided within an ethos of spiritual support and lov-
ing care, but what arrangements of design, form, function, colour, warmth,
volume and light can bring peace and serenity to those who dwell however
briefly in the spaces within.
