This chapter discusses the location of hospices and other care settlements,
looking at both the advantages and disadvantages of urban or rural settings,
as well as city-centre and suburban locations. It looks at the relationship
between the hospice and the acute hospital in terms of access to medical
facilities, at the same time as needing to preserve a sense of independence
and distance institutionally and culturally. There follows an examina-
tion of the many elements which make up the public face of a building or
institution – architectural style, perimeter boundaries, landscape settings,
territorial location – and discusses the differences which have emerged in
different countries and cultures towards the value of ‘domesticity’ in the
external appearance and interior design of hospice buildings.
Fitting into the landscape
While writing this book, I spent a number of days walking London’s ‘Green
Chain Walk’ with a friend: a circular footpath which circumscribes the
whole of suburban London. The route passes through relics of ancient
woodland, as well as threading its way through housing estates, parks,
cemeteries, small-holdings, riding schools and farms (with distant views
of the great white skyscrapers of Canary Wharf and Docklands). Leaving
the ancient Bostall Wood one day, we came across a sign pointing to the
‘Greenwich & Bexley Cottage Hospice’, located down a narrow lane tun-
nelled through thick broad-leafed woodland. This, we were soon told, was
locally known as the ‘Cottage in the Wood’.
What a contrast in setting to that of St Joseph’s Hospice in Hackney, just
a few miles away. That hospice is located on a noisy, often grid-locked main
road in one of the poorer parts of east London, surrounded by warehouses
and factories, car showrooms and housing estates. Planes leave and arrive
at the nearby City Airport, providing a perpetual drone overhead, while
police and ambulance sirens add an even shriller note to the ambient urban
noise. St Joseph’s is however equally embedded in its environment, as the
Greenwich and Bexley Cottage Hospice is in its delightful woodland glade.
(It is said in its early years, parents instructed their children to cross the
road rather than walk close by the entrance gates of St Joseph’s, as if death
were somehow infectious.)
Public faces and private places 	5

-- 93 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	79
Thus, two much-admired hospices, each operating successfully in entirely
different locations and settings. Yet, this is not unusual. On a visit to Sweden
some years ago, I took a train into the rolling farming countryside 50 kilo-
metres to the south of Stockholm to visit the hospice wing of Vidarkliniken
Hospital, surrounded as it was by a sunlit landscape of forests and lakes. Later
that day I returned to the city to talk to staff at Ersta Sjukhus hospice, located
in the busy streets of Södermalm, close to Stockholm’s ferry terminal. Again,
the contrast in settings could not be more different, and again both operated
entirely successfully in their given locations.
There is no set rule it seems as to where formal provision for palliative
care should ideally be located – no perfect site or combination of location
conditions. This is in marked contrast to the Maggie’s Centres, all of which
with one exception are located within the grounds of a general hospital
with an oncology department. It is clear that all such decisions – as with so
many other decisions regarding the location of residential care and respite
facilities – will involve difficult trade-offs which will have to be made on
a case by case, project by project basis. Occasionally there may be local
resistance to the siting of a hospice, though this seems rare. In his history
of the Greenwich and Bexley Cottage Hospice, Ron Roffey noted that the
original petition to establish a hospice in or near Eltham in south London,
did meet with some public hostility.1
Nevertheless, as the Project Director for the New Farleigh Hospice in
Essex, told Danish visitors, echoing a familiar estate agent’s maxim, ‘When
designing a new hospice, there are three key crucial factors to be aware
of: location, location and location’. 2 There will be advantages and disad-
vantages to be had for patients, visitors, staff and support services in the
decision to locate hospice services either in urban or rural settings – or in
between, as was apparent when Cicely Saunders chose to site St Christo-
pher’s in suburban Sydenham, where she later wrote that, ‘We wanted to
be somewhere where there were stations at each end of the road and buses
going up and down. Patients want to see that, not lovely green fields’.3 The
new Marie Curie Hospice in Glasgow occupies a site with views of the
Campsie Fells to the north, and the design by Jane Darbyshire & David
Kendall ( JDDK) takes full advantage of this with an elongated single-storey
patient wing looking on to this view.
While a greenfield site in a rural setting can seem the ideal place to
design – as if on a blank sheet – for architect Ian Clarke ‘it’s probably true
that only awkward sites produce architectural innovation. The most inter-
esting buildings are often on the most challenging sites, including the mid-
dle of busy towns and cities’.4 Another architectural colleague, describing
the site of a hospice he had recently visited, thought that because it had
been developed on a large, greenfield site, the designers had simply over-
laid it with ‘bland, modernist planning, with too much space to sprawl, and
with little or no attention to the history or topography of the site itself’.5 As
we shall see in Chapter 7, when United Saint Saviour’s Charity decided to
commission a third almshouse, having acquired their second in a relatively
distant suburban setting, they chose to return to their inner-city home ter-
ritory in Southwark.

In some places, there are additional factors to be considered. In Belfast, for
example, location issues have also to take into account religious and polit-
ical loyalties embedded in particular areas of the city and the surrounding
suburbs. The existing Northern Irish Hospice at Somerton House in North
Belfast, is housed in what was once a preparatory school (and before that a
large private family mansion) in one of Belfast’s more expensive and, per-
haps most importantly, largely non-sectarian districts.
It should also be remembered that staff and visitors come and go 24 hours
a day, for, in the words of Dr Julian Bion:
Hospitals are rather like ships; the psychology of their design is very
similar. There is the same sense of purpose and ceaseless activity
throughout day and night. 6

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	81
Perhaps it may be more appropriate to think of the hospice as a small ship,
but nevertheless one that ploughs the seas whatever the time and what-
ever the weather. The Design Guidelines for Specialist Palliative Care Settings
produced by the Irish Department of Health and Children, is one of the
few documents that treats location decisions as central to the whole design
ethos of palliative care services. Not all of their criteria apply to small, ded-
icated hospices in the voluntary sector – even so they make pertinent read-
ing. The report devotes a short but incisive opening section to the issue of
‘Site Selection’, including the following desiderata:
The following criteria will assist in the assessment of sites for the pos-
sible development of new specialist palliative care in-patient or satel-
lite units, and will also be relevant to the assessment of existing sites,
where proposals are made to extend the facility and expand existing
services. It will normally be a requirement for project teams to under-
take a detailed and formal “Option Appraisal” exercise in order to
identify the most appropriate site for development. Such an exercise
will be expected to take account of all relevant issues, including the
following:
 It is essential that close, collaborative, service and operational
linkages can be readily established between specialist palliative
care settings and an acute hospital facility. Linkages of particular
importance will be in the areas of pharmacy, radiology, and treat-
ment interventions.
 Insofar as possible, the choice of site for a new specialist palliative
care in-patient unit should take account of road networks and pub-
lic transport facilities, in order to ensure optimal accessibility.
 Units should have a recognisable physical presence in the commu-
nity, to strengthen their identity, to develop familiarity, and to ease
anxiety or disorientation on admission, for both patients and their
families.
 Designers should ensure compatibility with the adjoining neigh-
bourhood, to ensure that a unit harmonises with its environment, as
far as possible.
 Orientation and views should provide interest and stimulation, and
enhance the environmental character and quality of the unit.
 Consideration must be given to the technical suitability of the site,
especially in relation to geotechnics, public utility services, site
area, capacity for expansion, etc.
 The cost of acquiring a site, and of preparing it to facilitate devel-
opment will be a key consideration in respect of any proposed
development.
 The capacity of the site to accommodate the unit itself and any
projected expansion, together with associated circulation patterns,
vehicular and pedestrian (e.g. staff access, patient transport, visi-
tors, delivery of supplies, disposal of refuse, traffic associated with
removals, etc.) will also need to be taken into account at the outset.7

-- 96 of 203 --

82 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
The reference to removals in the last consideration refers to the removal
of bodies, a sensitive issue to do with the siting and access to mortuary
facilities at such buildings, as well as to the rituals involved in attending a
body at the place of death, especially strong in Ireland, as well as elsewhere.
Some new hospice projects have been considering whether it is possible to
phase out mortuary provision on site, partly for the reason that in small
in-patient settings there would be insufficient use to justify the space. 8 Fur-
thermore, as hospices themselves now return many patients to their homes,
the removal of mortuary provision reinforces the public notion that hos-
pices are no longer just places of terminal care.
Not everything about location and site details can be anticipated in
advance. When St Francis Hospice in Berkhamsted finally decided upon
their rural site, they had not reckoned on the high cost of providing mains
water to the proposed development – in the region of £250,000 alone. Quick
thinking and an element of luck, resulted in a trial bore hole being dug on
the site, which found that water could be provided directly on site almost
for free – and that is what happened.
Locating hospices close to acute hospitals
Like Maggie’s Centres, some hospices have been sited within the grounds
or close to an acute hospital. This was the case at New Farleigh Hospice in
Chelmsford, within the grounds of Broomfield Hospital, though it has its
own entrance road quite separate from the main hospital, though all vehi-
cles exit the hospice through the hospital service roads. One of the obvious
advantages of such a system is that hospice visitors can take advantage of the
frequent bus service to the main hospital complex. Certainly the Maggie’s
Centres’ programme is based on this principle of creating a strong relation-
ship between its cancer support centres and oncology departments in major
hospitals.
Maggie’s London, designed by Rogers Stirk Harbour & Partners, and
completed in 2008, is located in the grounds of The Charing Cross Hospital
at Hammersmith, squeezed into a tiny former car park area, but bridging
the space between the rather monolithic tower-block of the main hospital
building and the tree-lined Fulham Palace Road. It has been designed to
deliberately distinguish itself from the otherwise highly institutionalised
setting. As one of the project architects told me, ‘Most new buildings have
to orient themselves and refer to their immediate neighbours – this dif-
ferentiates and isolates’. Maggie’s London is already surrounded by trees,
which provide an attractive approach from the main hospital entrance area.
The building – painted in a colour similar to Falun Red, with larchwood
door and window fittings – and the surrounding silver birches now contrib-
ute a small slice of rural Sweden to a dense and often grid-locked London
quarter. In Chapter 7’s case study of the new Maggie’s Southampton, which
opened in 2021, I discuss in detail the architectural, design and landscape
aesthetics which inform these striking buildings, which act as such a coun-
terpoint to their traditional hospital settings.

-- 97 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	83
All location decisions involve trade-offs, as we have seen. One of the sen-
ior staff at the Hospice of St Francis in Berkhamsted, expressed this neatly:
You can find solutions, where the hospice is closely connected phys-
ically to a local hospital, and you could find arguments for a location
further away. The further away, the easier it is to create that special
ambience and feel, closer by, and you could more easily share and draw
upon the hospital’s medical expertise.9

Harbour & Partners, opened in 2008. Landscape by Dan Pearson Studio.

-- 98 of 203 --

84 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
There is still an argument that general hospitals with a palliative care unit
offer benefits to the sick and the terminally ill which may not be available in
the dedicated hospice building. The general hospital is a microcosm of the
wider society, treating and accommodating the young and the old, the rich
and the poor, the seriously ill and the short-term clinical patient. Large urban
hospitals can resemble small city-states, architectural critic Edwin Heathcote
has written, offering the vibrancy and cultural mix of a city, as well as the divi-
sion of labour between service staff and doctors, residential accommodation
for nurses, cafes, shops, and so on. Not all large hospitals achieve this urban
energy however. Heathcote, like others, finds something still vital and attrac-
tive about the general hospital with its more varied social flows and connec-
tions to the wider world. In Denmark there still pervades a strong sense that,
‘The hospice is intended as an integral part of the complete package available
to the terminally ill and their relatives. The hospice cannot stand alone’.10
Others will disagree, arguing that matters of scale are related to patient
confidence and sense of intimacy. In the 1960s the ‘Balanced Teaching Hos-
pital’ was proposed as a model for a new building offering services to accom-
modate every population need. Professor Thomas McKeown wrote that,
…hospitals of the future should be large hospital complexes each
wholly serving the hospital needs of the population depending on it.
These shall replace the varied separate hospitals for the acutely ill,
the chronically ill, the mentally ill and those suffering from ‘special
diseases’ which we have inherited from an unplanned past. One such
hospital should provide all the beds and out-patient clinics and all the
attendant services that its population requires.11
Location efficiency
This ideal came with a plan revealing a small city in which it was certainly
possible for the individual to get lost, even if others might enjoy the bustle
and activity of such a honeypot. It is of course possible to create a simi-
lar open-house culture in a hospice. The modern restaurant wing at Our
Lady’s Hospice, Harold’s Cross in Dublin, serves patients, visitors, staff and
other casual users, including members of An Garda Siochhana (the Irish
police). The light, airy design of the building, the quality of the food and
reasonable prices, have all helped create an unexpected bridgehead or con-
nection back into the wider community, as the restaurant is also used and
enjoyed by working taxi-drivers, and people living on the local housing
estate. The advantage of city-centre hospitals and hospices is this ability to
overcome the problems of social and physical isolation from the rest of the
world. This problem is being addressed directly in new developments such
as Appleby Blue almshouse in Southwark, the Lark Hill retirement village
in Nottingham, St Wilfrid’s Hospice in Eastbourne, all of which feature in
the new case studies in Chapter 7.
The same principle is at work in a pioneering 120 bed old people’s home
in Soest near Utrecht in The Netherlands. Described as a ‘nursing home

-- 99 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	85
without walls’ it is located in the middle of the town, so that patients and
visitors if they are able can use the shops and services of the local high
street, and the canteen is in fact a café-bar open to everybody in the town,
providing a meeting place where the elderly no longer feel isolated. The
staff at St Gemma’s Hospice in Leeds stress how close their hospice is to
local shops and services (including pubs and restaurants), which are used
by patients, staff and visitors. At Horizon House, the Children’s Hospice in
Belfast, older teenagers can be taken out to the local pub for a drink, or for a
pizza, or to borrow a DVD, all of which facilities are close by.
These are good examples of what modern urbanists define as ‘location
efficiency’, where additional benefits are provided by the setting itself.
When the New Farleigh Hospice in Essex was opened in January 2006, only
a mile or so away from the original hospice (housed in yet another former
Victorian family mansion), the project leader for the new building admitted
that ‘there was some concern about accessibility, as the new hospice is not
easily accessible by public transport, but it was not considered wise to say
no to the building site on offer’.12 In fact, hospice managers realised that
the shuttle bus service to the adjacent hospital did offer a convenient form
of transport for those visitors who chose to use it, though agreeing safe and
attractive pedestrian links to the hospital bus terminus was still being nego-
tiated two years after the hospice opened.13
This close proximity to other lives and communal goings-on is stressed
by the staff at the Kamillianer Gardens Hospice in Aalborg, Denmark,
located in what was a former 19th-century eye hospital in the centre of
the city, the 12 wards all face on to a central courtyard adjacent to school.
They specifically mention that, ‘Sounds from playing children have been
mentioned in particular as something positive by the relatives, as these con-
tribute to creating the impression of being in touch with life outside, in spite
of ill-health’.14 Rather surprisingly, staff at Trinity Hospice in London were
anxious about forthcoming building works on site to create a new residen-
tial unit when I first visited it, and considered decanting patients elsewhere
during construction. However, it was soon evident that patients found the
noise and bustle of the site fascinating and enjoyable, and said they wel-
comed all the busy activity in and around the buildings and gardens.15
It was noted earlier that the establishment of St Patrick’s in Cork (with
its forbidding high walls), and at St Joseph’s in Hackney (the first religious
hospice in Britain), initially created a degree of initial public apprehension
and misunderstanding. When eventually St Patrick’s took down the high
perimeter wall, and opened up the pleasant terraced gardens to public view,
attitudes changed. Likewise, the modern wing at St Joseph’s constructed
in 1957 allowed the creation of a pleasant landscaped courtyard, creating
a non-intimidating connection between the reception areas, the gardens
and the street. The new wing with its large windows further created links
between the hospice, its residents, and the life of the community beyond. A
nurse from that era, Sister Margaret Deegan, said at the time that,
What I liked about it was the patients could look out and see, they
would have commented about that. It was the big glass windows and

-- 100 of 203 --

86 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
the big panels, and it was really lovely for them. They didn’t feel closed
in like the old hospice … They could see the people passing on Mare
Street and the staff coming and going over to the nurses’ home, and
all that life. They were still part of life and that meant a lot to them.16
The demolition of one of the 1957 wings in 2005 to create a new building
with much improved facilities, also allowed for the creation of a second
small courtyard at the entrance to the hospice complex, described apprecia-
tively by architectural critic, Jonathan Wilson as, ‘one of the most interest-
ing aspects of the scheme. In fact I think it is its coup de grace’.17 The spaces
between buildings can either create a sense of welcome or can be allowed to
become overcrowded car-parks, real barriers to a sense of human sanctuary
and ease.
The public face of hospice architecture
The public face of a building is made up of a number of elements: loca-
tion, perimeter boundaries, entry points, names and sign-boards, and of
course the architectural style of the building itself. And first impressions
count, particularly for the vulnerable. There is a moving scene in Lasse
Hallström’s celebrated film, My Life as a Dog, where the husband of a woman
dying of what we assume to be TB, takes his two young sons on their first
visit to see her in hospital.18 The scene opens with the gloomy five-storey
institutional building looming in the foreground at the end of a short drive,
and then the camera switches to the apprehensive, bewildered faces of
the young boys as they seek to comprehend its size and scale as they walk
towards it. In a few seconds the director has given this building an ominous
presence of its own, and implied much about how many buildings make a
lasting impression in a single glance.
Even names can still cause public apprehension. This was demonstrated,
as has already been mentioned, in 2000 when the Macmillan Runcie Day
Hospice decided to change its name to Grove House and the number of
patient referrals increased significantly.19 Staff at Horizon House, the
Northern Ireland Children’s Hospice in Belfast, agree that lots of families
‘have a problem with the word “hospice”, as it implies a place of terminal
care’. Yet many of their life-limited patients come in and out of care over a
period of time – sometimes for a number of years. As one of them poign-
antly told me, ‘Our young people are growing up and dying at the same
time’.
Nevertheless, the majority of hospices retain the word in their institu-
tional title, and feel happy with the general public approval of the term and
the now well-established concept behind it. Even so, the building itself, as
well as its location and setting, also has a key role in creating public confi-
dence and trust. As the introduction to Maggie’s architectural brief clearly
specifies, ‘Maggie’s asks a lot of its buildings and hence of its architects.
We expect the physical space to do a significant amount of our work for
us’. 20 This is particularly true of buildings which are serving those who are

-- 101 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	87
terminally ill, where patient anxiety leads to finding meaning everywhere,
for good or bad.
Buildings, therefore, should not only function but mean. A lot of the work
of creating meaning relates to the specific style of architectural grammar.
As Thompson and Goldin pointed out in their history of hospital architec-
ture, early hospitals were either designed from without – as grandiloquent
palaces for public displays of benevolent munificence – or from within, as
exercises in sanitary and social reform. They called the former, the ‘derived
plan’, where the external architectural form took precedence over the func-
tional efficiency of the interior within. What they termed the ‘designed
plan’, referred to those cases where sanitary demands and organisational
efficiency dictated the final design of the overall building. 21
Ideally attention should be paid to both, assuming that external appear-
ance and internal efficiency complement each other. In the case of the new
Marymount Hospice in Cork, already mentioned, two architectural firms
were chosen, one to design a more traditional hospital exterior, and the
other to design a more informal interior. As one of the senior staff told me
with regard to this unusual decision, ‘Some hospices we visited looked too
domestic on the outside. We wanted the inside to look domestic, but not the
outside’. When asked what he thought the outside should look like, he took
some time before answering: ‘Possibly a hotel’. Now completed, it has been
in use for more than a decade, and is much admired.
Others may feel that the hospice should try to replicate the appearance
and ambience of a private house set in its own grounds, a place of shelter
and peace. As James and Noakes argue, ‘the psychological message of the
pitched roof is of shelter from the rain and other elements and is a compel-
ling one in all but very dry climates’. 22 It is not only the hospice which is
moving away from a modernist pre-occupation with the efficiency of verti-
cality and monumentality:
The past 20 years have seen a steady move away from hospitals organ-
ized primarily around vertical circulation systems. This trend has
been most marked in the UK, where the Nightingale-inspired hori-
zontal pavilion never died out, as it did in many other countries. 23
In Romania, the organisation Hospices of Hope has been developing inter-
est in hospices for several decades. When it came to designing a new hospice
building in Brasov from scratch, the city planners rejected the first design
because, in the words of Graham Perolls, ‘it did not look like an institution.
They expected, and wanted, an imposing portico and high-pitched roofs’. 24
When I visited it in 2010, the dominant portico and pitched roofs of Hos-
pice Casa Sperentai (Hospice House of Hope) were much in evidence, but
inside much of the caring culture of the modern hospice movement was in
evidence too.
At St Francis in Berkhamsted, local authority planners more or less
insisted upon ‘something agricultural with farmhouse courtyards’ to fit
into the surrounding landscape. This rural site was chosen after looking
at more than 50 other potential locations, so it can be seen that finding the

-- 102 of 203 --

88 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
ideal location is never easy. The resulting hospice appears as a collection
of connected low-key buildings with wings and out-buildings, timbered
gable ends, courtyards and gardens. Its seemingly random assembly makes
it impossible for anybody to ‘read’ the building as an institution of any kind.
One slightly unusual feature was the inclusion of a round tower connecting
two wings, which gives the outward appearance something out of the ordi-
nary, and inside had proved a great success. It is certainly not an institution,
either in design or appearance, but it does feel separated from everyday life
by its rural location, and something of a retreat.
The preference for a domestic, vernacular architecture emphasising a
horizontal nestling into the landscape – sometimes known as ‘romantic
pragmatism’ – seems particularly English, and is not shared elsewhere to the
same degree. 25 Christopher Richards of Acanthus LW Architects, designers
of South Bromley Hospice and The Hospice on the Weald in Kent has said
that, ‘People tell us that they want these buildings to feel domestic and
traditional, rather than institutional and clinical – though not architectur-
ally challenging’. 26 In Ireland – perhaps because much hospice funding still
comes from government funds, and also because of the stronger Catholic
tradition which established so many hospitals and hospices – there remains
a preference for buildings which are more formal, and which refer back to
long-established architectural traditions to do with charitable institutions
or public health reform. In Scandinavia, there remains a greater attachment
to an enlightened tradition of modernist design. A Danish report on hos-
pices in Britain noted that,
In the English hospices strong colours on the walls are more common,
as are bolder patterns on the curtains and an overall more romantic
Figure 5.3 Approach to the Hospice of St Francis, Berkhamsted, Hertfordshire:
‘Something agricultural with farmhouse courtyards’ was required by planning
authorities to fit into the surrounding landscape.

-- 103 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	89
style than the minimalist Scandinavian style we know. Nonetheless,
the hospices we have visited share the specific intention of not wanting
to look like a hospital. 27
There are exceptions to the English attachment to cosiness. The new wing
at St Joseph’s in Hackney, designed by Greenhill Jenner Architects, is con-
sciously minimalist, as was insisted upon by the staff who apparently stated
that they ‘were not interested in simulated jollity, or pretend homeliness’. 28
It has also been said of the rooms in the new wing that, ‘These spaces do not
pretend to be domestic. They do however communicate comfort and the
highest level of professional care, with tasteful restraint’. 29
The degree of ‘domesticity’ in the look and feel of the modern hospice
is a sensitive issue, and one again where there is no self-evidently right or
wrong answer. The Maggie’s Centres say they endeavour to combine the
best of both domestic scale along with challenging design:
We need our buildings to feel safe and welcoming. They need to be
small, and domestic in scale. On the other hand these little buildings
should not pat you on the head, patronise you by being too cosy. They
should rise to the occasion, just as you, the person needing help, is hav-
ing to rise to the occasion of one of the most difficult challenges any
of us is likely to face. At the very least they should raise your spirits.30
Architect Ian Clarke, of Jane Darbyshire and David Kendall Architects,
project architect on several hospices, gave much careful thought to this
issue of domesticity versus a degree of impersonality or neutrality in
design, writing that,
I think all healthcare environments should promote an instinctive
sense of ease on a moment-to-moment basis … It has often been pre-
sumed that the road to an instinctive sense of ease is provided by the
replication of domestic character in the architecture (an understanda-
ble assumption), but that deduction may not be entirely correct in my
view – it is more complicated than that.
Not all domestic environments promote instinctive ease, some
can be oppressive, but I do agree that “domestic” as a reflection of
“human-centred” and “anti-institutional” motives is right. These days
the terminally ill will frequently spend only short periods of time as
a hospice in-patient, having spent lots of time at home, getting sicker,
in a small-scale domestic environment. The hospice might offer an
equally re-assuring and hopefully uplifting atmosphere but perhaps
with a slightly different ambience to home and offering different
things – such as a good view and access to well managed gardens, for
example – more about human experience, less about domestic visual
clutter perhaps. It is a very interesting design issue.31
One final point on this issue of the necessity for a sense of homeliness in
supporting the serenity of a dying patient is made by Knox observing that,

-- 104 of 203 --

90 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
some patients may not wish for family members to witness their bodily
decline, and instead, prefer to receive palliative care to manage their
ailments. In such a situation, the dying person’s sense of dignity and
autonomy may necessitate deliberate dissociation from the overly inti-
mate spaces of domesticity, instead favouring a move away from home
at the end of life.32
A key element in the initial impression given by a building is its degree
of ‘massing’ – the immediate impression it makes on the townscape or
skyline, or the degree to which it appears large and monumental rather
than small and knitted into its surroundings. There is often quite a cru-
cial architectural trick involved here. Some buildings can be small but
be designed so as to appear almost monumental at first sight; others
may be designed to achieve the opposite effect. Many 18th- and 19th-
century churches and chapels were designed with formidably grand clas-
sical facades, with high porticoes and columns and pedimented roofs,
though once inside visitors found themselves in a modest plain box. At
the opposite extreme, a lot of high street shops (or storefront churches
in North America) may have a small ornamental frontage at street level,
but possess a deep floor plan inside, as well as several floors above which
are anonymous outside above ground floor level. You can either hide the
scale of your ambitions or operations, or you can magnify the scale by
artful design.
It is surprising how few, if any, hospices in the UK have experimented
with creating sheltered porches at the front entrance where patients, visi-
tors or staff could sit. There may be all kinds of logistical reasons why this
should not happen, but it would be one way of tempering the formality of
the entrance, and photographs of some American residential care homes
with attractive porch areas where people sit and drink coffee and talk, sug-
gest that it could work in some circumstances. (One can see how people
might instantly reject such an idea, based on the common experience of
having to run a gauntlet of desperate smokers gathered outside the entrance
of general hospitals – but there might be ways of arranging and managing a
different kind of gathering place.) At present the front of hospices are often
entirely unpeopled, and most sitting-out areas at the rear. Does it always
have to be this way?
Even more sensitive is the issue of removal of bodies from the hospice
mortuary, and the degree to which this operation should be disguised.
Most mortuaries are located at the rear of the building and the entrance
bay screened from sight by planting. While this is the most common
design option, not everybody in the hospice movement subscribes to this
victory of institutional discretion over human ritual. In the worst-case
scenario, which I have witnessed in Ireland, the mortuary exit at the rear
was immediately adjacent to the waste bin storage compound, providing
a sorry visual metaphor of human remains being collected from the same
place as the garbage. Neuberger cites the attitude of a group of people in a
care home she visited – admittedly not a hospice – who strongly resented
the fact that the bodies were taken out the back door. ‘We came in at the

-- 105 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	91
front door so we want to go out by the front door’, they told her in no
uncertain terms. 33
Car parking
There are other difficulties too. Hospices, with their continuous flow of
staff and visitors throughout a 24-hour operational day, often at some dis-
tance from public transport connections, are sometimes almost engulfed
by car parking problems. The largest hurdle to overcome is avoiding sur-
rounding the building entirely with car parking, as is now so often seen
with large general hospitals (where parking difficulties and parking costs
have become one of the most contentious issues in hospital policy and the
subject of more public complaints than any other issue).
There are a number of options for the designer, none of which is entirely
satisfactory. One can provide most of the car parking at the front, as happens
at the Northern Ireland Children’s Hospice, at St Richard’s in Worcester, at
New Farleigh in Chelmsford, and at St Francis in Berkhamsted. As a result
the initial impression given to the visitor is that one is approaching a very
familiar institutional setting. Sadly the effect is worsened where electronic
barrier gates are also in operation to prevent illicit car parking. The only
thing these places can hold in reserve is the enjoyable surprise of the rear
gardens which are only disclosed to those who use the building.
While car parking can be tempered by artful structure planting between
bays, nevertheless it is difficult to avoid a familiar business park site typol-
ogy. Some hospices provide the bulk of car parking at an adjacent site,
reserving vehicle entry and providing restricted parking for key visitors
and services – ambulances, doctors, visitors called in at times of crisis –
close to the main entrance. The worst scenario of all it to allow cars to
park right up to the windows of the building, giving those inside views of
reflected windscreen dazzle and shiny metal shimmering with engine heat.
Verderber and Refuerzo strongly advocate decentralised clusters of
parking. As they have written:
The proper location and design of parking areas is critical to the
success of the hospice. Too few spaces in too close proximity to the
building can be a source of stress in patients and families. Parking con-
figurations worthy of study include multiple smaller clusters, adjacent
perimeter parking for staff and volunteers, or underground parking.
Do not locate parking adjacent to the views from patients’ windows,
dayrooms, meditative spaces, and living rooms. Similarly, incorporate
buffers such as lattice fences, walls, shrubs, berms, and trees to screen
autos from direct view from gardens and quiet outdoor spaces.34
The irony is that car parking can occupy half of the total site, and in sheer
physical terms, the mass of the cars may appear to outweigh the mass of the
buildings. Thus the architecture – certainly in low-rise campuses – is often
overwhelmed or undermined by the scale of provision for the private car.

-- 106 of 203 --

92 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
First impressions
Architecturally, much imagination has been exercised in the design of hos-
pices for children. Robin House is the second CHAS (Children’s Hospice
Association Scotland) children’s hospice in Scotland, designed by the late
and much-admired Gareth Hoskins, it opened in 2005, set on an isolated
farmland site. The hospice caters for eight children at any one time, stay-
ing with their parents and siblings for between three and ten days, staying
in their own suites of rooms. It is more about respite breaks for children
and their families, rather than long term care, hence its distance from the
nearest town, and its country hotel approach road and entrance lobby. The
curved roof ‘exudes a sense of quirkiness and playfulness’. The building
mixes activity areas, spa pools and play areas, with a bereavement suite and
quiet rooms.
Horizon House Children’s Hospice in Belfast is shaped like a gentle
arc, curving into a landscaped garden, with all of the patient rooms facing
south-west onto the garden, each with its own private patio. A single cor-
ridor links all the patient rooms, though the pronounced curve avoids any
feeling of an institutional corridor. At the heart of the hospice is a large café
area with breakout play areas, and this is where for a lot of the time staff,
patients, family and carers gather to sit and talk, the focus on the children
bringing them together. A children’s hospice feels much more sociable than
an adult hospice where the emphasis is on personal privacy.
From the road, Horizon House Children’s Hospice is barely visible. The
hospice is a ten-bedded unit with family accommodation and administra-
tive and fund-raising functions, plus a hydrotherapy pool and a number
of other therapy rooms (art, massage, audio-visual). The entrance slip-
way rises sharply into a series of car parking bays (with some decorative
Figure 5.4 Entrance façade to Robin House Children’s Hospice, Scotland,
designed by Gareth Hoskins Architects, opened in 2006.

-- 107 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	93
planting), beyond which a low-key set of entrance doors can just be seen
to what appears to be a small clinic, or possibly even a modest business
enterprise. In fact, the front of the building has actually been constructed
on the end of a long, gentle horseshoe design, which stretches back into the
far distance, most of which is hidden from view by the small reception and
administration block which fronts and conceals the larger complex. A sim-
ilar effect was achieved earlier at Martin House Children’s Hospice, Bos-
ton Spa, West Yorkshire, designed by Wildblood MacDonald Partnership,
where the entrance hall is located at one end of long building, so that the
bulk of the hospice is hidden from the front view. A ground floor corridor
contains 10 single rooms, eight of which face on to a broad terrace.35
If anything, the entrance design of Horizon House is the weakest part
of the overall plan, as it almost belies the importance and quality of the
service embodied within. On the other hand, it does not in any way suggest
to a family attending for the first time that their child is about to enter into
an institution of any kind. This anti-institutional look and ethos is vital to a
children’s hospice where, as staff told me,
We can’t pretend or want to take ownership of the child. We are here to
fit in with the needs and timetables of the 190 or so families who at any
one time want to use us in whatever way they need.
An ability to come and go quietly and easily is crucial. Once beyond the
reception area, however, the building opens out into an extensive suite of
rooms and gardens, given added status by the fact that the hospice occupies
a very large site with open views from its hillside location across to Belfast
Lough.
By contrast, approaching St Francis in Berkhamsted, most of the build-
ing can be seen directly from the front, as it presents its two-storey length,
with entrance doors and canopy, chapel, wards, tower, and wings in one
elongated façade. There is a long circular access drive through landscaped
gardens, and the scale of the building – largely because the whole of the
upper floor is devoted to administration and fund-raising – appears much
larger than the functions it serves, notably 14 bedded rooms and a day hos-
pice. A nursing home with 14 bedrooms would probably be only third of the
size. To this extent, the hospice seems to be moving away from its cottage
hospital scale, or former family home origins, towards becoming something
like a stand-alone campus, and though it does not look like an institution in
any way, it nevertheless appears to be much larger than its core functions
would require.
This raises an interesting dilemma. Do the ‘back-office’ functions of
administration, management and fund-raising which today occupy increas-
ing amounts of space in the building specification, now distort the scale
and intention of the original ethos of hospice design? An initial answer to
this would be to suggest that these back-office functions could be housed
elsewhere more cheaply, without encroaching upon the core functions of
hospice accommodation and care. Yet when this argument has been put to
hospice managers, they point out that if you locate fund-raising away from

-- 108 of 203 --

94 	P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s
the hospice itself, volunteers quickly lose motivation. They need to feel
part of the hospice service directly, not located at arm’s length. In a way,
fund-raising and volunteering has to be seen, it is argued, as also at the
heart of the hospice service.
So too is education, which now takes up a significant amount of space in
any new hospice building, as they become centres for training volunteers
or providing professional development courses for those already involved
in palliative care. In 2021 St Christopher’s opened its new CARE building,
a £6.5 million state of the art education centre, designed by MillsPower
Architecture, shortlisted for the 2022 New London Architecture Awards.
A handsome and quietly imposing three-storey building. It incorporates
a number of flexible learning spaces, including a lecture theatre, library,
digital support centre, skills lab and roof garden. The centre is skilfully
linked to the main hospice buildings by an elegant canopied walkway, that
demarcates and distinguishes one activity (hands-on palliative care) from
another (thinking, talking, planning and remembering). The warm brick
interior with heavy brick pillars supporting a double height atrium, with
recessed window seats, a grand piano en-suite, lit from all sides, together
provide a ceremonious and uplifting space in which to think not only about
the past but also about the future.
One other interesting point of difference between the public face presented
by Horizon House and St Francis relates to the positioning of the spiritual
room or chapel element. Both have chosen small but distinctive brick or ren-
dered towers to embody the space which can be used for sanctuary, prayer,
and on occasions the viewing of a body. (Frank Gehry’s design for Maggie’s
Dundee also employs a two-storey tower element). Yet. in the case of Horizon
House, this is invisible to the visitor arriving at the front and is principally
Figure 5.5 The new CARE building – a state of the art education centre – at
St Christopher’s Hospice, Sydenham, designed by MillsPower Architecture,
and opened in 2021.

-- 109 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	95
accessed from within the main corridor as an element of architectural sur-
prise. At St Francis, the round chapel is located at the front close to the main
entrance doors, and with its stained-glass vertical window slots, evokes con-
sciously or unconsciously feelings of commemoration and the passing of life.
To my mind the most effective hospice entrance can be found at St
Oswald’s in Gosforth, Newcastle, designed by architect Jane Darbyshire
and winner of RIBA’s ‘Building of the Year’ in 1989 – the only healthcare
building then to have done so. The visitor enters a small mews courtyard
through a low brick porte-cochère (which deliberately prevents any large

Figure 5.6 Maggie’s Dundee, designed by Frank Gehry, opened in 2003.

vehicles trying to get to the front door). Thus, the entrance area is already
shielded from the noise of passing traffic, through the provision of a quiet
courtyard which doubles as a pocket garden. In one corner of the courtyard
a pair of double entrance doors are recessed into the building unobtrusively.
Such a series of transitional zones or architectural sequences eases the pas-
sage from the busy street to the landscaped car park, and then to the court-
yard and finally into the building itself. A similar courtyard approach works
equally effectively at St Joseph’s in Hackney. Buildings which open directly
onto the street or car park give the visitor no time or space to re-adjust
themselves as they move from one world to another.
The entrance to St Oswald’s – and once inside, the inner plan – reminded
me very much of Aldo van Eyck’s Orphanage in Amsterdam, commissioned
in 1954 and completed in 1960. The classical model of the orphanage had
been a single monolithic building of several storeys, or converted grand
house, with an imposing front entrance, designed to create a threshold of
uncertainty and even fear. Abandoning this template completely, van Eyck
designed a series of linked single-storey buildings, in the form of a ‘path-
based’ labyrinth, the irregular form creating a series of inner courtyards, so
that each small dormitory had its own outdoor courtyard to play in or get
access to fresh air. Aldo van Eyck claimed to be much influenced in this
design by a picture of a North African kasbah, with its uneven distribution
of dwellings, alleys, courtyards, inter-penetrating each other. As has been
written of this pioneering building,
Aldo van Eyck’s Orphanage is not simply a major period building. It is
more than just a historical monument forecasting and finally signify-
ing a turn in the post-war mentality that characterised the tumultuous
Figure 5.8 Inner courtyard, St Oswald’s Children’s Hospice, Gosforth, New-
castle upon Tyne. Architects JDDK (Jane Darbyshire & David Kendall), com-
pleted 1986.

-- 111 of 203 --

P U B L i C fa C e s a n d P r i Vat e P L a C e s 	97
1960s. Because of its emphasis on the ideas of community, reconcilia-
tion, rethinking and renewal, because of the ingenuity with which it
confronted problems which are still with us, it remains not only a mon-
ument in memory of the crisis of its time, but also a powerful model of
architecture’s competence and responsibility.36
From above the Amsterdam Orphanage looks like a painting by Mondrian,
a series of randomly distributed cells and pathways, and in some ways the
pinwheel design of Maggie’s Southampton echoes this constructivist effect.
From within, its design aspired to the ‘starry sky’ effect which van Eyck
adopted as his own metaphor for his programme of 700 playgrounds created
across the city after the Second World War. Walking the inner pathways
of St Oswald’s reproduces this feeling again, with bursts of light and views
randomly occurring as one circulates the interior and its connecting pas-
sages from one building to the next.
There is no one simple answer to the question as to the ideal location of
a building providing palliative care, nor to the issue of what kind of archi-
tectural face it should present to the public. Local circumstances and inter-
ests will in the end determine what decisions are made, and how they are
arrived at. Nevertheless, these should be informed decisions, and require
fairly sophisticated programmes of option appraisals, site visits, discussions
with existing hospice interests, and of course as many visits as possible to
other hospices, not necessarily occupying the same social or demographic
profile. Just because the new hospice finds a suburban site, it doesn’t neces-
sarily mean it has to have a series of pitched roofs and mock-Tudor detail-
ing. Too many of the hospice projects visited to date have had to make
rushed decisions according to timetables decided elsewhere, or agreed to
Figure 5.9 Garden Pavilion at St Oswald’s Children’s Hospice, Gosforth,
Newcastle upon Tyne. Architects JDDK.

develop on a site not because it was exactly what was wanted but because it
was offered cheaply, or was within the gift of some local well-meaning dig-
nitary. Even so many have shown real imagination and ingenuity in creating
places of real meaning and care in the most difficult of sites and surround-
ing conditions.
