The fear of dying on a large public ward was one of the driving forces in
the creation of the hospice movement, and the early adoption of the single
patient room was one of its most distinctive features. However, even single
rooms can lack character and can make their inhabitants feel isolated and
cut off from life. Getting the shape and feel of a patient room, its orientation
to the world outside as much as its connection to the life of the rest of the
building, remains a crucial architectural challenge.
One of the greatest fears concerning dying is that it may occur on a
noisy public ward, or in a room where the patient has been moved and then
forgotten, sometimes to die alone. There is a long history of discussion as
to the most appropriate setting when death comes, ideally occurring in a
quiet, familiar room, with few distractions, where the dying person, con-
scious to the last, calmly contemplates their imminent demise in the com-
pany of family, friends or the religious faithful.
Yet, there is very little written on the phenomenology of the room, par-
ticularly as a designed space which allows death to happen meaningfully,
even if we think such a thing is possible. As Nitin Ahuja has written, ‘It
should go without saying that there are no universally advantageous cir-
cumstances for death. The rooms that soothe one person may alienate
another. As such I submit that hospice design and practice are necessarily
and perpetually unstable’.1 Most people appreciate what the worst circum-
stances are – a large open ward echoing to the cries and groans of sick or
distressed people, with a television set on full volume in one corner, and
ambulance sirens rising and falling in the background – but there is little
to be found in the architectural or medical literature on the spaces which
consciously endeavour to provide the ideal setting for the end of life. This
is somewhat strange when one considers how much has been thought and
been written on the nature and spirituality of space in religious or sacred
architecture. Temples, monasteries, chapels, churches, synagogues and
mosques have all been constructed and designed in accordance with the
most erudite principles elaborated to produce and structure emotions and
feelings of piety, awe, collectivity and even grace in the various spaces
within. Such principles have been admirably delineated in Lindsay Jones’
recent taxonomic analysis of the hermeneutics of sacred architecture. 2 The
same is true of the domestic house, the differing rooms of which derive
Everything gathered in one room 	6

-- 115 of 203 --

e V e rY t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	101
from very powerful rituals reflecting changing social relations around hier-
archy, economy, family and the affective life.3
By and large, the architectural principles informing the design of mod-
ern medical care remain largely functional, with the efficient delivery of
medical and technical services being given priority over the well-being of
the patient – certainly as far as hospitals are concerned. Yet the two are
clearly intertwined.
Only now are those involved in the provision of palliative care giving
consideration not simply to the issue of single room or multi-bedded space,
but to the very size, shape, and ethos of the individual room itself. In brief,
its room-like qualities. In the case of the hospice, the patient room is the
most important space of all, around which everything else has to mesh
appropriately. If the atmosphere and gestalt of the rooms themselves don’t
offer support, comfort and even beauty, then the overall design of the hos-
pice itself could be said to have failed. The same is also true of care homes
and other places of residential treatment or therapy.
From the cell to the open ward
The history of the room in those institutions taking care of the dying
is based principally on the typologies of the cell, the cubicle, the public
ward and the private room. The following definitions come from the New
Shorter Oxford English Dictionary:
Cell: Any of a small number of chambers in a building; spec. a room for one
person in a monastery etc.; a room for one or more prisoners. A humble
dwelling; a lonely nook; an isolated retreat. Also, the grave.
Cubicle: A bedroom. A small individual sleeping compartment.
Room: A compartment within a building enclosed by walls or partitions,
floor and ceiling, one set aside for a specified purpose; a person’s pri-
vate chamber within a house, workplace, etc.
Ward: A room in a hospital etc. containing a number of beds, or allocated
to a particular class of patients; the smallest administrative division for
in-patients in a hospital. Also more widely, a dormitory in any building
providing temporary accommodation.4
Some would say that everything to do with medical or custodial architecture
begins with the notion of the cell. The Latin origin is cella, a small chamber in
a temple or storehouse. The use of the word ‘cell’ to denote a unit of accom-
modation in a building long predates its adoption in the scientific world as the
basic unit of a living organism. It was not until 1665 that the scientist Robert
Hooke first used the word ‘cell’ in its biological sense in his Micrographia.5
Nevertheless, words such as cell, cubicle, room or ward quickly divide in
terms of their historical associations into things of which people approve,
disapprove, and even fear, even though they have often been used almost
inter-changeably in the design of accommodation for the sick.

-- 116 of 203 --

102 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
A cell suggests an austere, monastic space, devoted to retreat and con-
templation; later it would come to denote a place of enforced custody (but
without quite the same forbidding implications of the dungeon or the oubli-
ette). Even so, the most eminent architect and architectural theorist of the
20th century, Le Corbusier, was more than happy to think about and write
upon the modern domestic bedroom as a version of the monastic cell: an
unadorned space of flat surfaces and windows, simply furnished, given to
sleep, quiet, and sombre reflection. Indeed, at the height of the modern-
ist movement in its most puritanical phase (along with its obsession with
human hygiene), the architect Karel Teige denounced the traditional mar-
ital double bed as a catafalque, and urged that mature adults ought to sleep
in single beds, preferably in separate rooms. 6 This was designed to strike
another blow in the cause of modern individualism, and the development
of ‘hygienic man’. For some zealots the modernist home and the hospital
or clinic became almost inter-changeable in their aesthetics and design
intentions.
When Le Corbusier was commissioned to design a new hospital for
Venice – which was never built – his space capsule designs for the individ-
ual rooms, known as the ‘care unit cells’, were uncompromisingly austere.
He had been much taken by a painting by Carpaccio of the funeral of Saint
Ursula, who was carried to her grave on a raised bed, her body ‘elevated
over the mundane’, and in this spirit designed a high bed platform in a plain
cell with no windows to look out of, though natural light was allowed in
through a deeply recessed skylight above and behind the patient’s head.
The famous plan and cross section of the care unit was widely published in
architectural journals, though James and Noakes rightly described it as ‘a
uniquely awful and inhumane environment for care’.7
Nevertheless, while the modern movement in architecture is today fre-
quently castigated for its failed attempt at large-scale social engineering, a
number of architects aside from Le Corbusier were genuinely innovative in
their contribution to hospital design – along with other buildings associated
with public health and pastoral care. The Zonnestraal Sanatorium in The
Netherlands, designed by Jan Duiker and Bernard Bijvoet, and opened in
1931, along with the Paimio Sanatorium in Finland designed by Alvar Aalto
in 1933, established entirely new ways of thinking about what part architec-
ture could play in creating buildings and spaces which offered light, fresh
air, and views of natural landscapes, giving patients hope and a sense of
optimism about the future. All these architects paid particular attention to
the volumetrics and ambience of the ward or room.
Now, whether the adoption of a cell-like space is self-imposed, or
enforced, there are nevertheless strong associations with the eschewal
of any kind of luxury or needless decoration, few if any distractions, and
much time for thought. The French philosopher and historian of medicine,
Michel Foucault, once commented that, ‘In the first instance discipline
proceeds from the distribution of individuals in space’, and that, further-
more, ‘disciplinary space is always, basically, cellular’. 8 Therefore, to some
a monastic cell could be a worthy place for the sick or dying individual to
contemplate their imminent release from life, in conditions of undistracted

-- 117 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	103
simplicity. After all, a monastery has its etymological origins in the sense of
a place where one lives – though not necessarily dies – alone.
Thus, many of the early spaces in which religious organisations cared
for the sick and the dying, were located in single rooms or cells, away from
the press and clamour of public life. The Knights of St John of Jerusalem,
founded in the 11th century to care for sick and dying pilgrims on the jour-
ney to and from the Holy Lands, created tented hospitals, including some
with individual cubicles, thus curtailing the spread of infection. A painting
of a nursing brother of the Order of St John tending the sick, all of whom are
shown to be resting in curtained cubicles, comes from 13th century Lima.9
Likewise the arrangements at Ourscamp Abbey, founded in France in 1129,
divided its large vaulted interiors into dozens of single curtained vestibules.
As Thompson & Goldin write,
It was too cold in the great stone halls to lie in an open bed. Such was
not the custom even in homes. With inadequate heating, a person in
bed was safer from draughts behind curtains, where he could warm up
his cube of air with his own breath if nothing better offered.10
This same arrangement of cubicles can be seen in the famous 1778 painting
by Johannes Beerblock, View of the Sick Ward of St John’s Hospital (Bruges).
Cavernous stone buildings were thus often sub-divided by wooden or
tented cubicles, a design which in some ways continues to this day in the
large open ward with its many single bays which can be curtained off indi-
vidually as and when required. The individual cell or cubicle becomes a
common form for the sick and the dying in the early monastic tradition,
for reasons of the need for spiritual self-composure but also because such
smaller spaces were able to be made more comfortable within the envelope
of a monumental religious building. Slowly, other models began to develop.
As populations grew and demands for respite from poor health grew equally,
the smaller institutions expanded their scale, and with it came the ubiqui-
tous and enduring public ward. This is how Thompson and Goldin put it:
Works of charity grew very numerous. A small foundation might be
small indeed, merely an almshouse for twelve aged paupers, in which
case an ordinary house could be taken over unchanged. Many a large
foundation began in such quarters and expanded later. For certain
patients, special enclosures that revealed their functions might be
designed: leprosaria, plague houses. But the solution generally adopted
for the hospice-hospital – that catchall for the poor and assorted cases of
illness and decrepitude – was the open ward, taken over from the mon-
asteries, adapted, enlarged, refined, and eventually mass-produced.11
In some ways, the modern hospice movement is re-playing some aspects
of this early period of institutional change. It too has its origins in services
offered by religious organisations based in private houses, of which many
subsequently discovered the need to commission dedicated new buildings
(though, thankfully, not on the mass production model suggested in that

-- 118 of 203 --

104 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
earlier period). Nevertheless, the hospice movement is at a similar stage in
its development, as it continues to grow as increasing numbers of people
express a wish to die in hospice care.
From mediaeval times onwards, the public ward became the dominant
architectural form for the care of the sick, the elderly and the dying, espe-
cially of the poorer classes, and remained so for centuries after. There are
frequent adaptations and innovations in the detailing of the public ward –
in ventilation, in sanitation, but most especially in the elongated pavilion
shape of the ‘Nightingale Ward’, with windows on both sides of the ward
offering natural light and ventilation to every bed. From then onwards the
public ward becomes the prevailing hospital space of treatment or pallia-
tive care. Where there are single rooms, these are for isolation purposes
only, especially for those suffering from infectious diseases. In recent times
these isolation rooms have also been used to accommodate patients in their
last hours of life, to avoid them dying on an open ward. However, patients
quickly get to know the implications of being moved to such a space, as
does everybody else on the ward. The isolation room possessed its own
grim mythology in older general hospitals still catering for large numbers of
elderly patients, especially for those most fearful of eventually occupying
‘the bed next to the door’.12
The open ward has been espoused and abominated in equal measure.
Depending upon the actual conditions of hygiene and care, public wards
have been portrayed in history either as convivial gatherings of differ-
ent people with different conditions gaining support and comfort from
each other’s company, or bleak netherworlds of the sick and the dying,
offering neither privacy nor emotional sanctuary from the world and
its suffering. For centuries arguments have raged over the clinical and
therapeutic advantages and disadvantages of public wards, and although
the trend today in now almost uniformly towards providing single rooms
as the basic unit of accommodation in hospitals and hospices across the
world, these arguments are not likely to go away – indeed they remain
fiercely debated.
As Dr Sarah McCloskey at Marymount University Hospital and Hospice
in Cork suggested in Chapter 4, ‘single room nursing is a very different type
of nursing from ward-based nursing’. When the new Marymount Hospice
opened in 2014 for staff and patients this was a major shift in the culture of
care. ‘It was always much easier to keep an eye on patients in a ward, and
quite often patients, or their families or visitors, would also keep an eye on
other patients, exchanging a few words of interest or greetings’. Neverthe-
less, she went on to remind me, in a public ward, patients had to share toilet
and bath facilities, and sometimes be witness to each other’s intimate frail-
ties and traumas, whereas there was far more personal and bodily dignity
in a room of one’s own, with en-suite amenities and privacy of conversation.
There are still mixed-sex wards in the UK, as my own wife discovered
when hospitalised during the Covid pandemic in 2021, a situation which
most people today find unacceptable. Although NHS policy ruled that in
future mixed-sex wards were prohibited in 2010, there continue to be con-
siderable breaches of the regulations.13

-- 119 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	105
When it is remembered that two-thirds of those in UK hospitals are over
65, and that many of those will die in hospital, on open or in mixed wards,
it is not surprising that the matter gives rise to so many complaints. One
of the early promises of hospice care was that when the time came, people
would die in conditions of privacy and individual care, certainly in rooms
containing no more than four beds, but increasingly in a room of their own.
From the open ward to the private room
The modern hospice movement has accelerated the move towards single
rooms for all patients wherever possible, and certainly wherever desired.
This was not the case at the outset, when the design for St Christo-
pher’s, Sydenham (1967) by Stewart, Hendry and Smith put the majority
of patients, as required by the brief, into four-bed units, as did the same
firm’s design for St Luke’s in Sheffield (1971) and for St Gemma’s Hospice
in Leeds (1978), along with most other hospices well into the 1990s. Even
where hospice designers and providers are today retaining the option of
providing multi-bedded accommodation that could be used by more than
one person – and some patients still prefer this on occasions – then these
are decidedly rooms, not wards. As Mervyn Taylor of the Irish Hospice
Foundation once memorably and emphatically declared: ‘The basic unit of
provision in a hospice is always the room, even if that room is shared with
other people, it is still a room – never a ward’.
The trend towards single rooms is now occurring in hospitals and hos-
pices across the developed world, and is more established than many people
realise. The King’s Daughters Hospital in Temple, Texas, was opened as
long ago as 1908 with 75 patient beds, all in single rooms.14 In North Amer-
ica the argument for single room accommodation in hospitals was ‘won’ by
the time John Thompson and Grace Goldin published their seminal history
of hospital architecture in 1975, as they acknowledged in their concluding
chapter.
The literature on the medical and therapeutic advantages of single rooms
is extensive and can be summarised as producing the following benefits:
 	A reduction in the brisk of cross-infection.
 	A reduction in medical errors due to personalisation.
 	Greater privacy for patient, medical staff and family.
 	Longer and better physical care from relatives and friends.
 	Greater patient and medical control of environment – fresh air, ambi-
ent heat, light, noise.
 	Better access to bathing facilities and therefore better suited to differ-
ent ‘ethno-cultural’ groups.
 	Greater flexibility in operation and higher bed occupancy rates.15
In Ireland it is suggested that in future hospital provision there should be
at least 50% of single unit accommodation in acute hospitals, and a min-
imum of 80% single rooms in newly built residential care homes.16 The

-- 120 of 203 --

106 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
Department of Health and Children’s set of Design guidelines for specialist
palliative care settings, already mentioned, recommends 25m2 for a single
room, including en-suite bathroom and toilet.17 As we saw in Chapter 4,
at the new Marymount University Hospital & Hospice in Cork, opened in
2014, all rooms are now single rooms. In the UK the Department of Health
published its best practice guidance on this matter in August 2008, recom-
mending a minimum of 50% single rooms in future, with planning options
for 80% and 100% also to be considered.18 It also reported that agreement
had recently been given for the construction of the first NHS hospital in
the UK with 100% single rooms.
Providing single rooms is certainly more expensive in terms of capital
outlay on building costs. Research in America by Mahlum Architects and
Davis Langdon Adamson into the ‘first costs’ of designing single rooms
or mixed room units (double and single) based on floor plans concluded
that at the time the research was conducted it cost approximately $180,000
per patient to provide single rooms, and $123,000 to provide mixed room
accommodation.19 Note that the idea of costing patient provision in a large
open ward was never considered. Roger Ulrich, Professor of Architecture
at Texas A&M University, has been the most determined advocate of single
room accommodation as undoubtedly the most important design contribu-
tion to patient dignity. In the UK, Professor Bryan Lawson, Dean of Archi-
tecture at Sheffield University, has been equally forthright:
On patient satisfaction, reduced infection and ease of management,
single rooms win hands down. But, unfortunately, all the emphasis with
PFI (the Private Finance Initiative) is on keeping capital costs down
rather than operating costs. But the capital cost of any hospital will be
exceeded by the running costs within two years. Hospitals with single
rooms are slightly more expensive to build, but are cheaper to run. 20
While it will be many years in the UK before the NHS is able to provide
single room accommodation on demand, UK hospices are increasingly in
the position where they can provide this. The refurbishment by Dever-
eaux Architects of the Princess Alice Hospice in Esher, Surrey, originally
designed by Hutchison Lock and Monk (1985), increased the occupancy
rate from 70% to 90% as a result of providing a majority of single rooms. 21
Nevertheless, while most hospices still have to cater for patients in both
single and multi-bedded rooms, a degree of selectivity (or ‘favouritism’ in
the words of one researcher) occurs when it comes to choosing who goes
where. 22 An early 21st century study of 116 hospice patients found that 80%
of all 20–59 year old patients were in single rooms compared with 50% of
those over 60. 23 For the researcher concerned this was clear evidence that
older people ‘were being denied resources and opportunities that others
enjoy’, or, put more simply, what was discovered was clear evidence of age-
ism. A difficult issue to resolve, though not untypical of the hidden forms
of rationing which inform the allocation of limited public resources in the
health service and elsewhere.

-- 121 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	107
Rooms: coming, going and inhabiting
But what is this room? After all, Louis Kahn was adamant that, ‘architecture
begins with the making of a room’. Is it a medical room of singular auster-
ity and efficiency, what the poet Thom Gunn once described as ‘the far
Canada of a hospital room’? Or is it, in the hospice, a place beyond medical
reach, a final private space that feels like home? And is the bed itself a place
of rest and sanctuary, or is it in the words of Ian Clarke, ‘too often simply
considered as a workbench for care delivery?’ 24 The obsession with ‘the bed’
as the locus of all end of life policy, whether in terms of building design
or care programmes, has been disastrous, according to Mervyn Taylor of
the Irish Hospice Friendly Hospitals programme. 25 While it is possible to
conceal much of the conventional medical apparatus needed to be deliv-
ered to each bed behind false wall panels – oxygen lines, heart monitoring
equipment – other requirements are now being demanded. Dying people
often lack mobility, and manual lifting and carrying creates problems of
its own. As a result many new hospice rooms are designed with large hoists
and ceiling tracking, so that patients can be lifted and carried to an en-suite
bathing area for washing and toilet functions. While the equipment is admi-
rable for the job it does, it nevertheless also appears somewhat ungainly and
even menacing – if not to the patient and staff then certainly to visitors.
And while great advances are being made in producing hoists and carrying
systems which are minimalist and as unobtrusive as they can be, the best is
always the most expensive.
One of the great design innovations initiated at the Sloane-Kettering
Cancer Hospital in New York, was the deliberate separation of medi-
cal facilities and treatment areas, from consultancy rooms. It was rightly
argued that the sight of large amounts of medical equipment and apparatus
might not only frighten patients and their families, but even deter patients
from returning for treatment. How often have many of us, wandering the
long and badly sign-posted corridors of a major hospital, been disturbed by
the accumulation of poorly stored, and often broken, medical equipment, as
if we had stumbled into the torture rooms of a secret society? In a remarka-
ble essay on growing up in a family that lived in the grounds of a large men-
tal asylum in the 1950s, writer Clair Wills described the fear of wandering
through buildings – where both her parents worked – ‘scared of opening the
wrong door by mistake’. Most people have felt that same fear at some time
in their lives.
Such issues raise an ever-present concern of those working in the hospice
movement – the degree to which hospice provision embraces or resists the
continual pressure to medicalise the services offered to patients, particu-
larly as it is clear that even the most terminal conditions can be extended,
however briefly, or patients discharged to die at home if sufficient medical
interventions are undertaken. It has been said on a number of occasions that
as hospices move from almost solely treating cancer patients (whose med-
ical and mobility needs are sometimes minimal, other than in pain man-
agement) to caring for people with other life-limiting conditions requiring

-- 122 of 203 --

108 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
extensive physical care, then the design of hospice rooms is likely to become
much more technology-oriented.
When I first visited someone dying in a hospice, there was consolation in
realising that the room they occupied in their last days was intimate, homely,
decorated with a few personal belongings and photographs, and as utterly
unlike a hospital room as it was possible to be. There was no evidence of any
medical equipment to be seen. The room looked out onto a garden terrace
where the hospice cat lazed in the sun. There were flowers in a vase by the
Figure 6.2 Viewing room at St George’s Hospital, London, after refurbishment.
Figure 6.1 Viewing room at St George’s Hospital, London, before refur-
bishment as part of The King’s Fund ‘Enhancing the Healing Environment’
programme.

-- 123 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	109
bed, and candles on the window-sill (often banned in general hospitals on
grounds of hygiene or health and safety concerns). Yet rooms are now being
designed in newly built hospices that are being kitted out with very large
hoists and other items of support apparatus, that re-introduce the look and
feel of a medical treatment room. These trade-offs between architectural
intimacy and the medicalisation of palliative care are necessarily difficult
to reconcile. Dutch researcher Bernike Pasveer describes one hospice she
studied in which a patient given three months to live, nevertheless commis-
sioned an ornate metal sign reading ‘Home’, which he screwed to his door,
demarcating the point at which he was in control of his room, rather than
the staff. 26
It is vital to acknowledge how important the life of a room can be. It is
not simply a shell, but has a life of its own, reflecting back thoughts, expe-
riences, memories and changes of time and season. A beautiful room allows
our personality to expand and exercise its; a grim room makes our inner
self shrink into a hard, resistant stone. Here are the thoughts ascribed to the
painter Pierre Bonnard on these qualities of a simple room:
There is more than enough in just one room to keep us occupied for
a lifetime. Think of all the different hours of day and night which a
room experiences. The silence and darkness of the night. The moment
before dawn, before the birds break out into song. The slivers of light
in the corners, on the table, touching the fruit in the bowl. And all
the seasons, late afternoons in Autumn and Winter mornings and that
period after lunch in high Summer. And then the ultimate mystery, the
fact that a room has been constituted as it has by people whose lives are
passed in them. 27
Of equal importance to the ethos of the room is the ability of those spend-
ing time in it to control vital matters of temperature, access to light when
wanted, and of course fresh air. The more this can be done from the bed
itself – still the ‘locus of patient care both day and night’ according to light-
ing specialist Glenn Schweitzer – the more the patient feels in control. 28
This wish to control the mood and comfort of a room affects visitors as
much as patients. In both cases people rightly regard it as a basic right to
manage their own environment, rather than find themselves having to
spend time in a space that is wholly controlled from outside.
Even as simple an architectural element as the window is replete with
issues of power and control. The historian, Christine Stevenson, has written
in connection with the wider architectural history of hospitals: ‘There have
been fights over being able to open windows for centuries’. As an exercise
in architectural and theological symbolism, Ourscamp Abbey was designed
with three horizontal rows of windows, each above the other. The lower row
was designed to allow air into the building, the middle row was to add light,
and the upper row was designed to provide a glimpse of sacred light. 29 The
design of the patient rooms at St Christopher’s, with its sawtooth façade
and projecting window bays had the advantage of allowing light to enter
from two different directions, generally always more interesting than only

-- 124 of 203 --

110 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
having flat glazing on a single wall. As Christopher Alexander and his col-
leagues demonstrated many years ago, people always prefer rooms which
have light entering from two sides.30 In one of the prototype models for
hospices designed by Timothy Moorhouse for the American Hospice Design
Manual, patient rooms are V-shaped at the end which intrudes into the gar-
den, allowing windows facing out in two directions.
In the end, however, it has to be admitted that in prioritising the single
room over the shared ward, the hospice movement may be contributing
to the contemporary culture of dying alone – or at least not as part of a
social setting. Philosopher Norbert Elias wrote in his book on death, ‘Never
before have people died as noiselessly and hygienically as today in these
(more developed) societies, and never in social conditions so much foster-
ing solitude’.31 While the consensus is now for single room accommodation
throughout the hospice and hospital sector where feasible, not everybody is
entirely convinced that this is the ideal; at least, they argue, there should
be some provision for multi-bedded rooms where patients and families pre-
fer this. There is some evidence that witnessing the quiet, painless death
of another can actually be beneficial to other patients, assuaging some of
their own fears for their final moments, which would not be the case if
everybody occupied single rooms.32 The new wing at Trinity Hospice –
designed by Jonathan Darke and Polly Barker of TP Bennett – also contains
two three-bedded bays in addition to the single rooms, after consultation.
‘Although most wanted to be in single rooms, a small proportion expressed
a preference to be in a room with others’, according to its Chief Executive.33
The reason that the bays contain three beds rather than just two ‘is to help
soften the blow if one dies; the impact of a death is far greater for one patient
left alone in a room’.
Figure 6.3 New wing at Trinity Hospice, Clapham, London, designed by T P
Bennett (Project Architects: Jonathan Darke, Polly Barker, Gurdeep Panesar).

-- 125 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	111
Single rooms do not necessarily mean isolation. A retired care-home
manager told the organisation Help the Aged about a different approach –
though this was not a hospice:
We prepare for everything except the one thing that comes to all of us.
People used to say when someone in the care home was dying, ‘We’ve
got to shut the door’. But I encouraged people to keep the door open
and people would sit with the dying person. Later, (the dying per-
son) told me, “Thank you for that. It’s taken away the fear”. You see, it
opened the way for them to talk and to expose their fears.34
Even single rooms can be designed to be more or less private as required –
unlike hotel rooms where privacy is sacrosanct. The new single rooms
at Trinity Hospice mentioned above have been designed to allow strong
visual connections between the patient and the life of the public spaces
(not corridors) outside. There are large glazed areas on both sides of the
door, each of which can be closed off by interstitial blinds set into the win-
dow frames; on one side of the door a window looks down into a light well
and the life of the ward below, so there are tremendous opportunities for
people-watching and feeling part of the flow of the hospice. By contrast,
the new single rooms at St Joseph’s in Hackney have been designed to offer
absolute privacy to the patient and visitors, the room being designed in sim-
ilar fashion to that of hotel rooms where the door opens into a short, narrow
‘hallway’, on one side of which are the en-suite rooms, after which the room
opens out into the patient area. Even if the door is open passers-by can
only see the foot of the bed and part of the end-wall. This latter room plan
was common to all the international hospitals studied in a survey by NHS
Estates published in 1998.35
However, significant changes are in the air, with much more thought and
experimentation now being given to the design of single room accommo-
dation. At Hillingdon Hospital in London, the Hillingdon Hospital Trust
in partnership with MAAP Architects, have commissioned a Pilot Ward
which will test out three different designs for single rooms in a ‘continuous
programme of evidence-based design’.36 The first room has an inset door
and short corridor provided by the en-suite facilities located immediately
to the left of the room as one enters – in the common hotel style; the second
room has the door opening immediately into the room with en-suite facil-
ities set back into the room next door (and vice-versa); the third option has
the door opening straight into the room with en-suite facilities provided in
a small room at the window end directly opposite the door. All three offer
different aspects of the patient bed for staff observation, while some give
the patient much more visual privacy and others appear more like a normal
bedroom.
While en-suite facilities are now de rigueur in single rooms, privately
some architects and hospice managers admit that they can take up a lot
of space, are often not able to be used by patients independently (which
somewhat defeats the original purpose), can be disliked (or even feared) by
patients for not having any windows or sources of natural light, and are not

-- 126 of 203 --

112 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
the panacea they are supposed to be. On the other hand, they offer visitors
a sense of personal comfort and security.
The flexible room
It is of course possible to design rooms which cater for more than one
patient, and which allow for the sociability which some people still wish
for, as well as the possibility of converting such rooms quickly and easily
into single units as and when required. But before we enter such rooms, how
do we get there and what do we see and feel as we step over the threshold?
There is another scene in Lasse Hallström’s film, My Life as a Dog,
already mentioned, when the two young boys enter the hospital. Father
and children are shown walking towards the camera down a long, wide
corridor, entirely without any decorative features on the walls, no side-
lights, no paintings, or wall hangings. The floor is polished linoleum. On
one side is a row of arched windows, facing a row of blank doors on the
other side of the corridor. Fluorescent strip lights run the length of the
corridor ceiling. At regular intervals there are hard wooden benches for
visitors to use. The most shocking feature, however, is that the door to
each room is painted grey, and is perfectly flush to the surrounding white
wall, giving the impression of a door leading into an airtight bank vault.
Worse still, is that when the father goes in first, he opens the door out-
wards, a clear indication that all the power of the institution lies with those
outside the room.
Talking to an architectural friend about this film, we both remem-
bered precisely this corridor scene in all its architectural detail, though
neither of us had seen it for more than 20 years. ‘How a door opens, and
how it reveals a room, says everything about how that room is occupied’,
he told me. The French architect and specialist in palliative care, Nils
Degrémont, has suggested that, ‘Physically enlarging the doorway, for
example, preserves privacy while establishing a hierarchical relation-
ship between the inside of the room and the inside of the hospital’.37 The
smaller the door the less significant appears to be the room (and therefore
its inhabitant) to the outside world. Ideally the entrance to a patient room
should be recessed, thus creating a proper threshold between the public
and the private domains.
In Roman mythology, the door of any room or building was guarded by
Janus, a god who faced both ways, allowing him to represent both inside
and outside worlds simultaneously. Janus was also the god of entrances and
departures.38 Thus the threshold and the door represent perhaps the most
powerful symbolic element of any room, as it is the thing the inhabitant or
visitor first sees on arrival, and the last thing they remember as they leave.
Closing the door of a room one may never enter again, is one of the more
poignant moments of adult life. The threshold to a room is thus a momen-
tous inter-penetration of both time and space, of beginnings and endings,
and is therefore of vital architectural and emotional significance, as the
following writer, Richard Lang, notes:

-- 127 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	113
This threshold, where I hospitably receive others into my personal
domain, is a place of remembrances. Here, at the site of my reunions
and farewells, the persons and events of my past quietly continue to
dwell … At a doorway there is an intensification of life; I become truly
aware of shifts and turns in life, of openings and closings, of beginnings
and ends. Here, I live my transitions concretely.39
Yet, there are places of care where it is now a matter of policy to liter-
ally hide doors – not single room doors, but doors to the outside world.
This has become practice in some residential provision for those suffering
from dementia, in order to avoid the distress caused by any reminder that
there is another world outside the safety and security of the home itself.
This is a ‘design solution’ that should cause some concern amongst those
who feel that we can go too far in providing a protective shell around those
suffering from neurological conditions, though the arguments for such
well-meaning deceptions appear evenly balanced on both sides. The ethics
of what has become known as the ‘artificial reality industry’ are considered
in a thoughtful essay, ‘Beyond Façade: Home Making and Truthfulness in
Dementia Care’ by Ike Kamphhof and Ruud Hendriks.40 Given that home-
liness is often symbolised by a familiar front door, Kamphof and Hendricks
describe the work of a Dutch company called ‘True Doors’, that photographs
and reproduces full size dementia patients’ own domestic front doors which
are then used to overlay institutional doors. By erasing the appearance of
exit doors is another device altogether, for as the authors write, ‘concealing
the way home, in the hope of erasing people’s feelings of homelessness by
hiding the door’, nevertheless disguises the truth that moving home is also a
part of life’s common experiences.41
Two other design issues arise here which are central to how people feel
about the location and setting in which a room for a sick person is placed.
The first is the corridor. In Vidarkliniken, in accordance with the over-
all philosophy of those following the anthroposophical precepts of Rudolf
Steiner, there are no straight lines in the plan of the corridor spaces, each
is obliquely angled every so often to break up the possibility of long, con-
tinuous and uniform sight-lines, as well as being painted in warm, mood-
softening colours. They are described as:
…interior pedestrian streets. These circulation spaces are broken vis-
ually into short segments by means of subtle changes in direction and
frequent widenings into alcoves, some of which have windows. The
patient’s increased activity is continually and intentionally aroused by
the corridors’ rhythmically varied, naturally flowing spaces.42
At Horizon House in Belfast, the principal corridor which connects all
of the children’s bedrooms follows a slow curve, again painted in warm
colours, and which draws you down the length of the building without
the wearying feeling induced by a long, straight corridor. In her design
for St Oswald’s Hospice in Newcastle, Jane Darbyshire not only punctu-
ated all corridors with windows looking into gardens and small courtyard

-- 128 of 203 --

114 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
spaces, but broke the lines on both sides of the corridors with setback
window seats, small retreat areas capable of holding a sofa and a couple of
armchairs, so although it is possible to follow a straight line with a wheel-
chair or gurney from one of the corridor to the other, both sides are full of
incident and interest. Architect Ian Clarke who worked on the building,
likened it to a fast-flowing river with eddies and small reaches on both
sides of the banks.
Figure 6.5 Viewing Room & Chapel at Vidarkliniken, Sweden.
Figure 6.4 Corridor at Vidarkliniken, Sweden, designed by Erik Asmussen
in the 1960s: a large hospital and community campus created by the Rudolf
Steiner Movement.

-- 129 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	115
In the design for Marymount University Hospital and Hospice in Cork,
the same architect, Ian Clarke, told me that collectively they have tried to
ensure through the unusual starfish plan of the main building and its five
wings, that ‘You can go from the front door and reception area to every part
of the building within 20–30 paces’. So although the corridors are straight,
they are very short, and punctuated by window views and window seats. In
this way hospice design can quickly break the pattern of the monotonous
institutional corridor so typical of the large hospital template. Clarke admits
that the Cork starfish bears some historical resemblances to the Benthamite
‘panopticon’, though in this case the patient gains the advantage of having
fore-shortened sight-lines and ease of way-finding.
Clarke has also pointed out that the plan of most hospices is largely dic-
tated by the fact that every room ideally should face out into a garden, that
it is ‘all edge and very little interior’. Furthermore, it is clearly desirable that
patient rooms should face south-east or south-west, so that their view and
the room itself is lit by the sun as often as is possible, while able to be shaded
when necessary. There is even research which claims, perhaps not surpris-
ingly, that ‘Connections have been made between north-facing rooms and
increased rates of depression and pain’.43 Thus on the plan, the twin pri-
orities of avoiding creating long institutional corridors as well as trying to
ensure that all patient rooms face in an easterly or southerly direction, to
enjoy the early morning and daylight sun, provide a design challenge that
everything else has to work round.
At Trinity Hospice in London, the design of the new wing – based on
long discussions between hospice staff and architects – is that while nearly
every in-patient has their own room, all the other space is open plan. What
functions as a corridor on each of the two floors, is so wide and irregular
in shape, that it provides lots of niches and setback areas, as well as light-
wells connecting the two floors. There is no ‘corridor effect’ but a feel-
ing of a long room connecting two larger public areas at each end. They
have tried to quietly challenge the privacy and isolation of a series of single
rooms by making the public areas busy, colourful and supportive. In this
the design supports the view of architect Nils Degrémont that the space
immediately outside the room becomes a place for ‘walking and gather-
ing, which would then take on the function of an urban street, (providing)
the missing link between the patient and society’.44 This ideal is in sharp
contrast to the experience I encountered in one large Swedish hospital,
beautifully designed and expertly managed, but whose long corridors were
utterly silent and forbidding, and even the hospice unit which joined this
arterial corridor, was itself totally silent, with all rooms closed, and which
felt as if one had entered a space station whose inhabitants had disappeared.
It has long been argued that the proportions of a room are one of the main
elements which guarantee their sense of occasion and structural beauty.
From Palladio onwards, the rooms of great houses were often designed in
mathematical proportions equal to musical intervals: 1:1; 1:2; 2:3, and so on,
borrowing from earlier Greek architectural theories of harmony. Such pro-
portions can still deliver powerful emotional effects when handled singly
or in sequence, providing a strong sense of what is sometimes termed ‘a

-- 130 of 203 --

116 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
noble room’ – one with a very strong sense of inner strength and propor-
tion. What is today paid too little attention to are ceiling heights, which are
also a major element in fine proportionality. According to one recent critic,
Small variations in the height of a ceiling seem to have the power of
altering a space much more than small variations applied to the width
and length of rooms. It also seems true that the vertical dimensions of
rooms, since they are relatively free of “functional” imperatives, are
able to carry more than their share of emotional content.45
In the case of a hospice room, the ceiling does sometimes carry some quite
specific and major functional imperatives, in the form of supporting hoists
and tracking systems. But the point about ceiling heights is well made.
Architects have long insisted that any building in which the ceiling heights
are uniform throughout is an institution, not a place of comfort or elective
habitation.
For the most important user of the room, the patient, a room is largely
viewed from the position of lying prone. It was the experience of being
ill that made the Finnish architect Alvar Aalto bring a wholly new sense
of what was needed in a hospital room to his revolutionary design of the
Paimio Sanatorium:
When I took on the planning I was ill myself, so I had a chance to do
a few tests and find out what it was really like to be ill. It irritated me
to lie horizontal all the time and the first thing I noticed was that the
rooms were designed for people who spend their days in the vertical
position, not those who have to spend days on end in bed. Like moths
round a candle my eyes were constantly drawn towards the electric
light. A room which is not designed specifically for people lying hori-
zontal has no internal equilibrium or real peace. So I tried to design
rooms for non-active patients which gave the bed-ridden a tranquil
atmosphere.46
Aalto went further in his understanding of how a room might be designed to
provide tranquillity and comfort. He was especially keen on lighting as much
human interior space from above, through rooflights and clerestory win-
dows, and he also strongly believed that the room should possess an archi-
tectural integrity even when empty, that ‘the architecture – the plan and its
volumetrics – are the permanent form, and the disposition of furniture is
variable, like that of figures in a landscape’.47 In the design of modern health-
care buildings, including hospices, top-lighting or corridor clerestories are
still too little used, probably for reasons of cost, yet as we have seen earlier
in this chapter in relation to Ourscamp Abbey, that light which comes from
above is quite different to the light which comes through a side-window.48
For the patient in particular, the quality of the room in which they are
likely to spend most of their time at the end of their life, is vital – literally
life-enhancing rather than life-denying. The writer Alan de Botton percep-
tively notes that,

-- 131 of 203 --

e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m 	117
Figure 6.6 The prow of Maggie’s Highlands, Inverness, designed by David

118 	e V e r Y t H i n g g at H e r e d i n o n e r o o m
An ugly room can coagulate any loose suspicions as the incomplete-
ness of life, while a sun-lit one set with honey-coloured limestone and
lend support to whatever is hopeful within us.49
Given that a favourite credo of the hospice movement is the idea of liv-
ing life to the full to the very end, then the hospice room carries all the
major responsibility, in its light and proportion, elegance and comfort, for
whether the hospice overall succeeds or fails.

Open to the world and to life 	7
What once were quite separate generic institutions with different names
and frames of reference, are now learning from each other and ‘mixing
and matching’ operational functions and treatments under the broader
heading of modern palliative and social care. Today, there is a greater
range of providers: public, private, religious, independent, and charitable,
with income streams coming from a variety of sources. In this chapter,
recent projects in the UK – a new hospice in Eastbourne, a new Maggie’s
Cancer Care Centre in Southampton, a retirement village in Notting-
ham, and a contemporary almshouse in south London – demonstrate the
diversity in contemporary care provision. Each scheme exhibits tremen-
dous vision, as well as close team-working and long-term co-operation
between care practitioners, architects, landscape architects, and funding
bodies, all working together to achieve exemplary projects which in turn
inspire others to do better.
Case Study 1: St Wilfrid’s Hospice, Eastbourne
Building type: 	Hospice
Location 	Eastbourne, Sussex
Size of plot 	1.37 hectares
Footprint of building 	Gross internal area (GIA)
3495 m 2
Client 	St Wilfrid’s Hospice
Architect 	RH Partnership Architects
Landscape Architect 	Rick Rowbotham (Studio
Loci)
Completion date 	2013
Number of beds 	20 inpatient bedrooms

-- 136 of 203 --

122 	o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e
The new St Wilfrid’s Hospice replaces an earlier hospice of the same
name which opened in a former private house in 1982 on another site in
Eastbourne, a seaside resort on the south coast of England. That hospice
had ten inpatient beds in a mix of single rooms and multi occupancy wards.
In 2009 a new greenfield site was acquired, funds were raised by the now
well-established St Wilfrid’s Hospice, and in September 2013 the new hos-
pice was formally opened. Today it serves a population of 235,000 people
covering an area of 300 square miles. The new building employs 207 staff
(approximately 158 full time equivalents), and at times nearly 700 volun-
teers. St Wilfrid’s raises some 70% of its total annual revenue, the rest paid
for by the NHS, and manages eight charity shops in its catchment area.
The new St Wilfrid’s has 20 inpatient beds, with 250–300 admissions a
year. The average length of stay in the IPU (inpatient unit) is 20 days, with
around 30% of in-patients discharged back to their home or to a care home,
their symptoms having been brought under control. In addition, nearly 1500
patients are currently supported at home, whether this is a domestic resi-
dence or a residential care home.
The decision to commission and oversee the construction of a new build-
ing happened under the watch of the then Chief Executive, Kara Bishop,
who occupied that post from 2006 until 2016. Recalling the development of
the project brief, she wrote:
A study into the future for hospice care identified that an increase in
in-patient provision would be required to meet growing demand from
an ageing population. At the same time the Board approved a new vi-
sion for the service that reflected an emerging commitment within the
hospice movement to a public health approach to palliative care. The
hospice was now envisioning a future where people talk openly about
dying. This would require changing attitudes to death and dying. The
Figure 7.1 Level-surfaced approach to St Wilfrid’s Hospice, Eastbourne.

-- 137 of 203 --

o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e 	123
adoption of this vision would influence both the selection of the site
and the architectural solution.
It was clear at this time that the tide was turning in the way that hospices
needed to be integrated into the life of the community – the theme of an in-
fluential conference on palliative care titled, ‘The Space Between’, held in
Inverness in October 2009 which I attended. A public appeal was issued to
find a new site, and the one eventually selected was not, however, without
some critics at the time. Sited close to a main road, adjacent to a large super-
market and college, this was not the ‘sheltered, rural retreat’ that tradition-
alists hoped for or expected ‘but it was perfect for a new hospice wanting to
actively engage the community and change attitudes’, Bishop told me. Once
the site was finally agreed the design of the building had to follow the same
philosophy, which was in the words of her successor as CEO in 2016, David
Scott-Ralphs, that ‘a hospice is a community asset’.
That it clearly embodies an ‘open-house’ philosophy is evident as soon
as one enters the building. Those expecting a quiet subdued retreat, may
be surprised to find a busy public gathering place. The central feature of
the new hospice is a large internal ‘street’, a place where the public are wel-
comed in. This is a long, double-height linear atrium which runs from the
entrance to the far end of the building, brightly and naturally lit in daylight
hours by large rooflights, giving it a conservatory feel as much as an atrium.
The wayfinding is clear and simple: those visiting the IPU (inpatient unit)
are drawn down towards the end of the ‘street’ by a large picture window
revealing the extensive garden beyond, at which point visitors turn left into
the inpatient area.
Figure 7.2 The central feature of the new St Wilfrid’s Hospice is the large
internal ‘Street’, unashamedly a public space, open to all.

-- 138 of 203 --

124 	o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e
The main thoroughfare is unashamedly a public space, open to all, pro-
viding a café/bistro with a large number of place settings, set-back areas
with armchairs and sofas. What distinguishes the new St Wilfrid’s in par-
ticular is that when first suggested, this highly contentious decision was the
clearest signal possible that there was no hierarchy of permission to enter,
nor gate-keeping. ‘Removing the reception desk and opening the building
to the public required significant changes in both attitudes and working
practices, and an extensive programme of staff and volunteer involvement
influenced the management of these changes’, Bishop remembered.
The main public concourse could easily be mistaken for the atrium of a
concert hall or arts centre, though the emotional atmosphere is warm and
kindly rather than over-excited or attention-seeking. Artworks are common
throughout the building, adding to the design repertoire, and complement-
ing the high-quality furnishings, which largely eschew plastic or synthetic
materials where possible, though they can still be easily cleaned. Up to 300
people can gather safely in this space for a choral concert or other event. At
lunchtimes the space is exceptionally busy. Ten years after it opened the
‘street’ is as light and airy as it was on the first day, largely because as Bishop
said ‘we firmly resisted filling the space with unnecessary signage, promo-
tional literature, or anything that distracts from the emotional experience
of this open and welcoming space’.
All of the inpatient rooms are on the ground floor, each with its own
en-suite wet room with bathroom and shower, and each opening out onto a
well-maintained garden area. The gardens, with large and small planting,
help provide both sun and shade, patterns and shadows, supplemented with
simple water features – so there is constant movement as well as chang-
ing patterns of light. They are large rooms, designed with medical support
amenities, but decorated subtly to harmonise with the colours of the gar-
dens outside. The rest of the ground floor is reserved for therapy rooms,
laundry, sluice rooms, staff rooms, catering, and a quiet room known as ‘The
Retreat’: this is reserved for reflection and composure, with a lower ceiling
height and carpeted floor, distinct from all of the other public spaces. There
is no mortuary, which now seems to be common in most hospices built since
2000. All of the administration and fund-raising takes place in a series of
open-plan offices on the first floor.
Architect, Nic Hoar, remembered the early challenge:
The site’s narrow width presented quite a challenge – how do we suc-
cessfully navigate those arriving at the hospice to inpatient and outpa-
tient services without them needing to travel along lengthy corridors?
The “Street”, as it became known, therefore took on several func-
tions; a warm, welcoming and vibrant ‘front door’ to the hospice (meet-
ing the client’s vision of a building that was open to the community),
and a space which clearly guides the visitor via site lines, markers and
forms, to their destination rather than relying on signage.
With views towards a courtyard garden to one end, natural daylight
floods the space via a sequence of large rooflights. Steve Geliot’s art-
work (commissioned as part of an art strategy for the new building)

-- 139 of 203 --

o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e 	125
runs full length of the space reflecting and refracting daylight via roll-
ing slithers of steel and prisms and with the tracking sun changes the
nature of the space throughout the day
The atrium-type arrangement also provided an opportunity in the
building’s section to physically and visually link the first floor, desk-
based staff with the predominantly clinical activities of the ground
floor – historically seen as distinct and unrelated. Furthermore, the
hospice wanted to be open about the range of services it provides, so in
bringing the elements of the service together it was clear to the visitor
how the charity operated.
The gardens
The surrounding gardens make use of an impressive sloping height to the
rear to display wildflower meadows, while the courtyard gardens within
the hospice ground-plan, are more formal but strongly structured with
raised beds and planters and evergreen shrubs. The courtyards which serve
the patient rooms are flush to the room floor so that beds can be wheeled
out in good weather, as well as being accessible to those visiting patients.
The extensive rear garden has been structured as a bund from soil exca-
vated from the site, capturing water for the apple orchard and more formal
gardens at ground level.
The gardens at St Wilfrid’s are an integral part of the hospice’s ethos and
support. There are three main areas. The Courtyard Garden sits in the
centre of the inpatient unit and is primarily for use by patients and their
Figure 7.3 Raised water-retaining bund of wildflower grassland surrounding
the Orchard Garden at rear of St Wilfrid’s Hospice, Eastbourne.

-- 140 of 203 --

126 	o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e
visitors, with the facility to easily move patient beds into the courtyard.
This garden is a haven of peace and tranquillity. The Orchard Garden at
the rear of the building has some patient rooms facing onto it and is a riot of
grass meadow and wildflowers on its banks, looking down to a star-shaped
planted area and gazebo. There is also a resident beehive. This garden has
mixed use by patients and staff. The Wellbeing Garden at the front of the
building is a mix of trees, herbaceous borders and planters and is used by
outpatients and general visitors to the hospice, as well as staff and volun-
teers. This garden is often used for events and a tree was planted there in
2021 by The Duchess of Cornwall for the Queen’s Green Canopy project
celebrating the Platinum Jubilee.
The value of natural landscapes and those of the built environment, good
or bad, have long been recognised as having a profound impact on a per-
son’s sense of wellbeing, and at St Wilfrid’s these effects are seen in abun-
dance. As Rick Rowbotham, the designer of the landscape at St Wilfrid’s,
has written:
Gardens and man-made landscapes have been used as places for emo-
tional expression, psychological and spiritual reconciliation, medita-
tion, relaxation, and restorative contemplation throughout the history
of mankind, and healthcare has often been synonymous with planned
garden spaces, giving gave rise to the creation of what are now termed
‘therapeutic gardens’ in many healthcare facilities.
The key considerations in designing such landscapes in my expe-
rience are:
 	a landscape led masterplan determining the disposition of built struc-
ture within a site-wide landscape
 	a design narrative that is poetic, engaging, and familiar
 	a predominately ‘green floriferous’ outlook for calming primordial
impact
 	hierarchy of spaces from the public, to semi-private to private
 	the use of intimate enclosure, courtyard spaces, for privacy, contem-
plation and sanctuary
 	sheltered external positions to offer protection during inclement
weather
 	a secular space for meditation and prayer
 	private outer space accessible to bed-bound IPU patients
 	provision for all users from family members who will be the primary
users, to patients, staff and visitors. There should be inter-visibility
between internal and external space
 	private space for staff members
 	use of running water and reflective water
 	planting design for seasonality, colour and smell and perhaps some ex-
otics to evoke memories of foreign travel
 	animation of the site through biodiverse landscapes to attract wildlife,
birds, and insects

O P E N TO T H E W O R L D A N D TO L I F E 	127
It clearly helped that the architect and landscape architect had worked
together before, as they had with some of the senior hospice management
team – in short, they had prior knowledge of each other’s work. These pro-
fessional relationships and networks – often regional – seem to provide a
surer footing for successful working relationships, as the success of the new
St Wilfrid’s demonstrates.
Maggie’s Southampton was opened in 2021 in the corner of the car park
of University Hospital Southampton. The initiative to build it came from a
local campaign which had joined forces with the national Maggie’s office, by
then keen to site one of its innovative cancer care centres on the UK’s south
coast. The architect selected for the scheme, Amanda Levete of AL_A, de-
scribed the site as the ‘most unprepossessing site we have ever had’. With
very few exceptions, all Maggie’s Centres are sited in the grounds of hos-
pitals with large oncology units, from whom most referrals initially come.
AL_A’s vision for Southampton was ‘to bring a bit of magic to the place: to
imagine that a piece of garden had been transported from the nearby New
Forest into the midst of the concrete landscape’.
An early coup was commissioning landscape architect Sarah Price to em-
bank the perimeter of the site at the outset. This enabled the planting of an
extensive range of semi-mature trees and shrubs which rapidly provided a
protective shield to the building underway, thereby securing the new Cen-
tre’s own unique arcadian setting. Once inside the finished single-storey
building, which is extensively glazed on all sides, the views all look out onto

128 	o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e
rich woodland. The result is that once inside, the large and rather forbid-
ding hospital estate only a hundred yards away is soon forgotten.
The temptation to build high, or in some other way dramatise the build-
ing’s presence on the site was resisted in favour of creating this small wood-
land demesne. ‘The scale of these buildings – Maggie’s Centres – is incredibly
small, but their power is extraordinarily big’, Levete says, demonstrating an
impressive exercise in ‘weak power’, where it is not size or massing which
impresses but the more traditional virtues of ‘commodity and delight’. If this
and many other Maggie’s Centres belong to an architectural tradition, it is
that of the garden pavilion, but so hybridised in its functions and outcomes to
be, as Charles Jencks has cited before: an ‘emergent building type that is not
quite a museum, church, hospital or home but has aspects of each’. In terms
of its internal layout Jencks sees it as sharing some characteristics of 1880s
domestic architecture in the USA and the UK known as the Shingle Style,
later adopted by Frank Lloyd Wright, ‘a flowing space, centred around the
inglenook and fireplace … with open and closed rooms … as a continuous
sequence’.1
As can be seen from the floor plan, the layout is based on a pinwheel
arrangement, by which the same irregular shape is repeated in a circular
sequence, with the four principal walls, made of ceramic bricks, extending
from the central area out into the garden. Inside these walls take on the
appearance of fluting, familiar from many of Alvar Aalto’s interiors, out-
side they appear as serrated white fins. The exterior walls, where not large
glazed panels, are finished in mottled stainless steel, which shimmers and
reflects the light and shadows filtering through the surrounding woodland.
The floor is polished concrete. It and the ceramic are materials that
can be outdoors as well as in, which, together with the views of nature,
create an atmosphere that is indoors and outdoors at once. Ceramic
and concrete together create a world of reflections, in which patches of
sky and foliage are folded into the walls and floor and light bounces up
Figure 7.4 Maggie’s Southampton, view into interior. Architect: Al_A, with
landscape by Sarah Price Landscapes.

-- 143 of 203 --

o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e 	129
on to the ceiling. Light white curtains filter the sunshine, their vertical
folds echoing the rhythms of the ceramic fins. 2
While the size of the largely open-plan interior is only 360m 2 , the sight-
lines and internal rhythms of the spaces – together with the multiple views
out into the surrounding woodland – create a sense of a world within a
world, and at the same time, of endless horizons. The open-plan design cre-
ates a large central area with a kitchen sphere to one side. Leading off this
central court are a number of small and medium-sized rooms and niches
with folding screen doors that can be closed off for meetings or discussion
groups. The majority are without doors, but an intricate pattern of internal
walls protects these intimate spaces from direct sight-lines. It is another
keynote of Maggie’s Centres that there should be no need for signage any-
where, and that all spaces should disclose their use informally, rather than
through notices and labels.
Those using the Centre can come and go as they wish. The office is
located by the front entrance so that staff and volunteers can see visitors ap-
proaching the building and greet them at the door and quickly make them
feel welcome and at home. Close inter-personal relations are the key to the
success of the Maggie’s ethos: yet these seemingly informal relationships
are neither ad hoc nor improvised but discreetly planned, and the interior
layout is specifically designed to achieve this. The central open-plan con-
course is focused around two large tables – one in the kitchen area, and one
in the main area. The former is the first station of arrival and there is always
a trained member of staff there to introduce users to each other, old and
new, to offer refreshments and to support conversation and the exchange
of experiences. The architect and critic Charles Jencks, along with Maggie
Keswick Jencks the original driving force behind the Maggie’s project,
described the core ethos of Maggie’s Centres as being ‘kitchenism’, drawing
on a distinctly anthropological understanding that in most iterations of the
Figure 7.5 Maggie’s Southampton: open plan interior.

-- 144 of 203 --

130 	o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e
domestic home throughout history, the fire around which food is prepared
and eaten is the centre of the social universe.3 Jencks describes this idea
because,
Informal, like a home, a Maggie’s Centre is meant to be welcoming,
domestic, warm, skittish, personal, small-scaled and centred around
the kitchen or place to make coffee and tea. The centrality of food and
drink allows people to enter and exit without declaring themselves, try
things out, listen or leave without being noticed.4
The material palette of the interior design, and its colour range, is based on
the work and experience of Maggie’s central design team, in conjunction
with the architect. Here is a short extract from an admiring review of the
Southampton project by architectural critic, Rowan Moore:
Throughout, the overall impression is of thoughtfulness and calm.
The palette, apart from a bright yellow kitchen unit in the heart of the
space, is warm but muted, with a light pinkishness prevailing. Then,
outside, there is a bit of glitz. Here, walls are clad in mirror-polished
stainless steel, its surface mottled and disturbed like wind-blown wa-
ter. In these panels, the interior’s play of reflections becomes less sub-
tle: the garden becomes an impressionist blur of green and blue, the
straight lines of the ceramic walls liquefy into op-art. 5
Having seen the interiors of a number of other Maggie’s Centres, the as-
semblage is becoming familiar, but always exercised anew with flair and

energy. There is nothing bland about it. No pastel shades, but plain bold
colours, large robust armchairs, solid bench seating and window seats, large
wooden tables and chairs, built-in cupboards, brightly coloured rugs, with,
so far as I noticed, no flowers or sentimental apothegms. Nor is there any
digital technology on display, nor a clock. The Head of Centre, Gilly
Howard-Jones, has described it as a ‘pot-pourri free zone’, and perhaps
more interestingly, as an ‘asexual environment’. It is 1960s Scandinavian
modernism before Pop Art and the Memphis Group took over.
A pocket forest
Landscape architect Sarah Price was involved right from the very
beginning – somewhat unusual in many new projects, where landscaping
too often functions as a ‘making good’ process at the end or, even worse, as
an afterthought using up whatever is left in the total budget. In this case the
integrity of the design shows. For Price, a key motif was making a reference
back to the New Forest which is inscribed in the local imagination, and this
gave her a way of engaging with the main client group as well as passers-by
visiting the main hospital, already intrigued by this unexpected and rapid
outgrowth. The idea of a pocket forest was that it would be a landscape form
that helped shield the Maggie’s Centre building from the surrounding heat
island of the car park, but also to temper the reflective warmth given off
by the building’s glass and ceramic exterior. Price told me that she wanted
to create a self-contained world but with just enough glimpses out – to the
neighbouring suburban streets, and the hospital setting – so that it avoided
turning in on itself and becoming wholly detached.
Self-containment was principally achieved by the earth embankment
along the site perimeter, other than providing for an entrance gateway. This
involved importing large amounts of topsoil to sit on the pre-existing base
Figure 7.8 Cross-sections of Maggie’s Southampton with raised embanked
garden to provide a feeling of self-containment.

Lark Hill Retirement Village, now the largest retirement village in Eu-
rope comprising 327 one-bedroom and two-bedroom apartments, is the
most recent development from the ExtraCare Charitable Trust based on
Coventry, which now operates 16 not-for-profit retirement villages in the
Midlands and North of England. The Trust was established in 1988, and
grew out of the Coventry Churches Housing Trust. Its Corporate Plan for
22–27 explains quite emphatically that ‘the charity was founded to provide
an alternative to the prevalent, ageist and rather depressing societal view of
getting old and later living’.
Case Study 3: Lark Hill Retirement Village,
Clifton, Nottingham
Building type: 	Retirement village
Location 	Clifton, Nottingham
Size of plot 	24 acres
Client 	The ExtraCare Charitable Trust

of hardcore and building rubble, with the embankment rising to 1 – 1.5
metres. This was sufficient to plant fast-growing pines, birches, cherry and
apple trees in root-balls, and this was all done in a two-week gap between
national lockdowns caused by the Covid virus. Other than the siting of a
weather-proof outdoor lunch table and seating, there are no other fixed or
static elements – whether sculptures, standing stones, or water features –
that generally might be expected in an ornamental or ‘therapeutic’ garden.
For Price, this garden is wholly ‘a celebration of nature and life’. Move-
ment, light, birdsong, growth and decay, are ever-present and, she suggests,
should not be end-stopped or disrupted by ideas of fixity or solidity. Life is
flow, both within the centre and outside in the leafy curtilage. There is an
unexpected amount of wildlife in the ‘forest’, and the delicacy of the trees
chosen produce an ever-changing pattern of light both in the garden and
in the centre itself (supported by the reflective quality of the building’s
exterior facings).

-- 148 of 203 --

134 	o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e
The Trust’s mission statement says that:
The ExtraCare Charitable Trust is committed to developing inspirational retire-
ment villages for the over 55s. We enable older people to enjoy healthy, active, and
independent lifestyles in their later years. ExtraCare retirement villages are not
just about providing new places to live. They’re about giving their residents a new
lease of life. We believe that health, age or financial means should be no barrier to
an enhanced quality of life.
The Lark Hill village occupies a hillside, 24 acre greenfield site on the out-
skirts of Nottingham, with its own bus-stop and a nearby tram link into the
city centre. The development is a mixture of one and two-bedroom bunga-
lows and apartments, 40% for sale, 40% shared ownership and 20% rented,
though these percentages can vary according to changing local circum-
stances, whether of need or demand. To be eligible to apply for residence, ei-
ther the applicants or a close family member must be resident in Nottingham
and pay Council Tax to that authority, and applicants should be over 60 (or
55 in exceptional circumstances). It is, in the words of one resident ‘a commu-
nity for independent living, not an old people’s home’. Opened 13 years ago
with a wholly new cohort of residents, today the average age is 83 years, yet
the village still exhibits a liveliness and busyness that belies its age profile.
What strikes the first-time visitor is the spaciousness of the whole de-
velopment, with generous wheelchair-accessible pedestrian through-fares
both inside the complex and outside on the estate, the latter marked by
well-maintained pedestrian routes, lawns and public gardens, making it
a genuine garden village. The public grounds of Lark Hill and the com-
mon spaces around the main buildings are all beautifully designed and
Figure 7.9 The ‘garden village’ aesthetic of Lark Hill ExtraCare Retirement
Village, Nottingham.

maintained. Residents have not only acquired a home of their own, in an en-
viable parkland setting, but also access to an extraordinary range of health
and leisure services and facilities provided by the not-for-profit charity.
There is an almost equal number of one-bedroom and two-bedroom
apartments, the former with a GIA (gross internal area) of 53 m 2 , and the lat-
ter with a GIA of 65 m 2 , located in blocks with two and three floors, all with
wheelchair-accessible lifts. Each apartment has its own front door leading
on to a wide public walkway or thoroughfare, and all the apartments have
kitchens with windows which look out on to this neighbourhood ‘street’.
The facilities offered to residents, and to visitors and nearby neighbours
who can pay to use them, include:
 	Café Bar
 	Craft Room
 	Enriched Opportunities Suite (to support residents with dementia)
 	Fitness Suite with Gym

The Covid pandemic came at a time when the large café, village hall and
exercise studio – formerly all separate areas – were in the process of being
upgraded, and advantage was taken of opening all three rooms into one
vast indoor concourse temporarily. This meant that people could continue
to meet with the appropriate social distancing, and continue to engage in
exercise, dance and yoga classes. This flexibility is now being maintained,
and rather than reinstating the former walls, they are being replaced with
folding room dividers, so that in future large-scale events – concerts, stage
performances, dances – can operate successfully. Another facility which
proved its worth during the pandemic was the village shop, sparing resi-
dents from having to travel outside during the lockdown periods.
Security for the residents is an important feature of the village. All
homes, common rooms, lifts, toilets, and other shared amenities have per-
sonal alarm systems, and the whole site itself is covered by CCTV. In one
way it is an enclave, but the generosity of the spaces and the gardenesque
landscape across the whole site give the community an open aspect and
feeling. The ethos of each of the ExtraCare Trust villages is to encour-
age activity and wellbeing, through extensive programmes of events, ac-
tivities and support for voluntary groups and particular interests, such as
