woodworking, gardening, maintaining a large wildlife pond (with 89 spe-
cies of flora and fauna recorded), bowls, choral singing, line dancing and
yoga. The facilities for residents at Lark Hill to engage with all of these ac-
tivities and interests are impressive and well used, some contributing to the
community in practical ways: in food-growing and in maintenance work for
example. A paid activities organiser and two paid ‘wellbeing’ workers (one
nurse and one adviser) support residents as and when needed.
As residents age, the Trust claims that ‘we can support significant as-
sessed care needs, including dementia for nearly all of our residents, and so
most will have a home for life’. The Trust provides a model combining some
of the best elements of ‘lifetime homes’ and ‘ageing in place’.
United St. Saviour’s Charity was founded in 1541 to support the needs of
the people of Southwark, and has done so ever since. Over the past two dec-
ades the charity has acquired and now manages two almshouses: Hoptons
Almshouses located in Southwark and established in 1752 (but not acquired
by the charity until 2011), and St Saviour’s Court in Purley, near Croy-
don, built in 2006. The new almshouse, which will be owned and managed
‘in perpetuity’, is called Appleby Blue, developed as part of a Section 106
Agreement, more commonly known as ‘planning gain’, where a developer
agrees to part-fund a social project in return for planning permission for
private or commercial development elsewhere. USSC will subsidise the cost
of living at Appleby Blue, meaning that the amount residents pay towards
living at the Almshouse will be affordable and within current Housing Ben-
efit limits. It therefore remains true to the principles of the commissioning
charity, while also being forward-looking and open-minded in its design.
A founding principle of the design of the new building was that it would
encourage residents to remain active in the community where most had
lived for much of their lives (many of them in social housing paying a ‘fair
rent’), by supporting a strong culture of personal independence. To this
end, the design of the ‘front of house’ of the building is that of a social hub
or ‘public lounge’. The street in which it now sits, and to a degree now

commands, remains a much-loved and busy thoroughfare, ‘a meandering

In her study of the design process of Appleby Blue, Professor Juliet Davis
points to the urban design strategy employed by the architects to achieve
this double-facing building, which looks inwards and outwards at the same
time.
Rather than using buildings to bound the site, thereby communicat-
ing protection and privacy, the architects sought to “flip” the idea of
retreat and enclosure, creating porosity between the urban life of the
high street and the social spaces of the interior.
“Flipping” the idea of retreat and enclosure is reflected in a num-
ber of different aspects of the project’s composition. As though pressed
with a thumb, the main high street elevation is not straight but cranked,
seeming to subtly weave together the façade with the widened pave-
ment in front of the building’s entrance and convey the sense of a
blurred boundary rather than a hard edge.7
The almshouse provides 57 new homes – 51 one-bedroom flats and 6
two-bedroom flats – all spacious and precisely designed. Eleven of the
homes are wheelchair accessible. Appleby Blue is ‘care-ready’, in the words
of the commissioning charity, offering lifetime homes that promote inde-
pendence for as long as possible, and allows for residents’ existing and fu-
ture health and care needs. From a purpose-built ‘community kitchen’ to
bring people together, to the courtyard, gardens and multitude of shared
community spaces and resources throughout the development, the Alms-
house will be a place where residents are firmly at the centre of life around
them. Martyn Craddock, CEO of United St Saviour’s Charity, told me that
the almshouse is also a community project as well as serving its core func-
tion as residential care home. The first cohort of residents will be in a wide
age range, from mid-50s up to and perhaps beyond mid-80s. This mix of
ages allows greater diversity of interests and abilities, and also avoids a sin-
gle cohort ageing and losing physical abilities and levels of social independ-
ence at exactly the same time.
The main public feature, apart from its significant presence on the street,
is a double-height public lounge at the point of entry into the building. This
is not an empty foyer or waiting room, but is intended as a meeting place,
where residents, family, friends and other residents in the area can come
and go freely, where there are refreshments on sale, events, cookery classes,
art exhibitions and occasional performances. At the rear of the lounge there
is easy access to a courtyard garden, again open to all users, unless oth-
erwise designated for private purposes. According to the architects, this
garden court, around which many of the apartments are arranged, ‘shares
the dimensions and focus of the traditional coaching inn courts along the
nearby Borough High Street’ (for which that street remains famous). 8
In her interview with the principal architect, Stephen Witherford, David
cited him as thinking hard about managing the balance or relationship be-
tween the advanced and fairly ubiquitous technology now required in care
homes and an atmosphere of comfort and informality. As Witherford told
her: ‘You’ve got to have CCTV, you’ve got to have security, fire detection,

-- 155 of 203 --

o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e 	141
automatic opening vents, telecare systems, signage and high-tech lighting
levels … it’s incredibly technical’. But, he went on, it was equally important
to ensure that ‘something else is more present, which is life – the rhythms of
people, the garden and sunlight, joyful things you feel each day’.9
USSC, the client, believe that:
Appleby Blue Almshouse will set a new benchmark for the quality of older
people’s social housing and services. It has been specifically designed for
today’s generation of older people who want to lead an active life in the
heart of the inner city. The Almshouse will combine radical design with
future-proof assisted living technologies to provide self-contained homes
that meet the needs of older residents, including those with emerging
health conditions, within a flourishing and caring community.
The almshouse and courtyard gardens consist of a series of connected ele-
ments, sequenced in degrees of public accessibility, ranging from the fully
public to the wholly private, as the ground plans show. In this as in other
more recent developments in residential care, the dangers of social exclu-
sion of the elderly from the wider community are avoided. Given that Julia
Park and Jeremy Porteous have in their key study, Age-Friendly Housing, con-
cluded that ‘older people are the social glue of communities’, such porous-
ness is in the interests of everybody who wants to see resilient communities
thrive again.10
Ageing in place, and researching in place
Evidence-based health and welfare provision is becoming ever more nec-
essary in fund-raising and governmental circles. The ExtraCare Charita-
ble Trust has enjoyed long-standing partnerships with Aston University

and Lancaster University to track improvements in depression, perceived

of the general findings resulting from the ExtraCare approach based on
research with residents:
 	NHS costs reduce by 38%.
 	Unplanned hospital stays reduced from 8–14 to 1–2 days.
 	46% reduction in routine and regular GP visits.
 	14.8% reduction in depressive symptoms in 18 months.
 	23% decrease in anxiety symptoms.
 	Significant improvements in the level of exercise done by residents
(75%).
 	Increase in walking speed and a reduction of falls over the first two
years.
 	The increase of frailty is delayed or reversed in residents.
 	24% increase in autobiographical and 17% increase in memory recall
tests.
 	86.5% of residents were ‘never or hardly ever’ lonely.11
Research into resident wellbeing is an ongoing process at ExtraCare. Re-
search & Technology Lead at ECCT, Kerry Stack, reports that:
We are very research active at ExtraCare focussing specifically on
projects that are designed to make life better for older people. Some
of our current projects include various health-based studies with uni-
versities and external researchers. For example, we are working on a
project looking at different types of non-intrusive sensors that mon-
itor the home environment enabling residents to remain healthy and
maintain independence. We are also working on a project looking at
the usefulness of Smart devices for automating daily tasks. Part of this
project includes offering residents the opportunity to use these de-
vices for free in their homes through the Smart market. We regularly
promote external researchers work through advertising their project
to residents. And we recently participated in research looking at the
journey of a resident from deciding to make the move to a retirement
village right through to settling in and discussing what made it easier
or difficult.
At Appleby Blue Almshouse, Martyn Craddock and Alison Benzimra have
an ambitious programme of research already underway with their two ex-
isting almshouses, and more planned once the new almshouse opens in
Spring 2023. They will be analysing if there is an increased life expectancy
of almshouse residents compared to the general population. A Dunhill
Medical Trust funded project will examine the resilience of almshouses
at three sites, in co-operation with Whiteley Village and Sir Josiah Mason
Trust. A Surrey University study of wellbeing in retirement communities
includes current provision for residential care by United St Saviour’s Char-
ity. In the pipeline are two new projects, one on community-based food

-- 157 of 203 --

o P e n to t H e w o r L d a n d to L i f e 	143
provision specifically at the new Appleby Blue Almshouse undertaken with
Bournemouth University, and a study with Loughborough University on
heating measures for improved healthy amongst older people.
Such research projects are being increasingly being built into the pro-
gramme of many new developments across the palliative care spectrum,
offering something new and very necessary to the otherwise weakness of
post-occupancy evaluation (POE) processes in the contemporary built en-
vironment. Partnerships with academic bodies can only help the process of
continuous improvement in user-experiences, especially in provision for
the vulnerable or those with complex physical and psychological needs. In
this way a reflexive, informed management culture can help allay if not
avert future crises arising from unexpected events such as pandemics, or
lesser disruptions, to the degree that buildings and places are from time to
time stress-tested for flexibility and modification.

There is a long history of attaching gardens to places of healing or
spiritual care, often religious in symbolism and intent. Hospice gardens are
many-layered in that they have not only to provide sanctuary and places of
refuge and beauty but also retain some reference to the more uncultivated
primal world of ‘nature’, which many people regard as their ultimate home.
The growing popularity of Japanese garden elements attests to the spiritual
qualities which landscape design can provide, though ultimately each hos-
pice garden or landscape must reflect the topography and history of the
site. Rightly, there is a reluctance to introduce any significant elements of
memorialisation into hospice gardens, as they are seen as places for the liv-
ing; similarly, there are dangers in designing gardens which are bland or too
corporate in style.
A return to nature
‘People have died in their beds in the open air, wheeled out onto the ter-
race garden’, I was told at Princess Alice Hospice in suburban Surrey, when
asked whether it was only visitors who enjoyed the serene courtyard garden
that had been designed within the latest wing of the expanding hospice
campus. At Trinity Hospice in London its Chief Executive said that:
A fair number of people die in our garden. We had one patient, a
woman in her 40s, who said she wanted to die with the sun on her face.
So, if it’s a lovely, sunny January day and someone wants to go out,
we’ll wrap them up and take them out to the garden.
This makes it more important that the garden is shielded from visual or
noise interruption from the surrounding streets or neighbourhood.
As we saw in Chapter 7, the pocket forest designed by Sarah Price at
Maggie’s Southampton, with its perimeter embankment lined with semi-
mature trees and shrubs, created its own world, emotionally but not spa-
tially isolated from the surrounding car park. The garden also provided
some protection from the ‘heat island’ effect of large numbers of metal cars
heating up on several acres of tarmac during the hot summer days. Like-
wise, the high bund created at St Wilfrid’s Hospice in Eastbourne not only
in a hospice garden 	8

-- 159 of 203 --

i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n 	145
helps create a more enclosed, and dramatic landscape, it also allows a higher
level of water retention to support the orchard and other plantings. In both
cases these structures provide that ‘predominantly “green floriferous” out-
look for calming primordial impact’, that landscape architect Rick Row-
botham intended at St Wilfrid’s in his masterplan.
The quality of the landscape is a vital element in the success of any hos-
pice, and the independence in budget-making and commissioning powers,
which voluntary status brings to the hospice movement, means that they
sometimes have greater freedom in choosing sympathetic sites to develop
when the opportunity arises. This provides an occasion to put landscape
and gardenesque elements to the fore. In this they are continuing a long
history of connecting medical care with close proximity to beautiful or
tranquil gardens. As Nancy Gerlach-Spriggs has written:
Such gardens have played an integral role in the evolution of human
care. Gardens have a mythology, a poetry, and a history, strongly
linked to life cycles and the process of healing, renewal, and ultimately
dying. The persistent appearance of healing gardens in places and
times of medical innovation suggest that beyond the aesthetic human
beings feel a biological need for contact with the natural.1
An important feature of the pioneering St Christopher’s Hospice in Syd-
enham was not just the quiet assembly of low and medium rise wards and
administrative and education buildings in a leafy suburban street, but also
that the gardens were regarded as being equally important to the success
Figure 8.1 Canopied walkway connecting old and new buildings at St Chris-
topher’s Hospice.

-- 160 of 203 --

146 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
of the original ‘hospice’ ethos. This largely remains the case wherever new
hospices are built or added to, and this is clearly the case at St Christopher’s
where the new CARE building is integrated into the existing site through
improved landscaping and way-finding systems.
It is now becoming accepted within the wider parameters of
‘evidence-based design’ that a view of an attractive landscape surrounding
buildings housing the sick or the elderly can bring psychological benefits.
These landscapes are variously termed ‘therapeutic gardens’, ‘restorative
gardens’ or ‘healing environments’. Furthermore, a distinctive body of
research evidence now exists within the health sector – most famously
exemplified in Roger Ulrich’s ‘View Through a Window’ essay, and subse-
quent studies – which claims that views of greenery and, even better, access
to gardens, aids the recuperation of patients following surgery or treat-
ment. 2 A sympathetic account of this research, and what it means specifi-
cally for hospital and hospice landscape design, is contained in the chapter,
‘Toward a Theory of the Restorative Garden’, by Nancy Gerlach-Spriggs,
much recommended.3
Many new hospitals, GP surgeries and health centres today include a
garden feature in and around the buildings, often justifying the additional
cost in terms of such long-term health benefits and, ultimately, even finan-
cial savings due to the speedier recovery of patients, or their improved sense
of well-being. These ideas are not new, as we know from a study of history:
When the English prison and hospital reformer John Howard (1726–
1790) toured Europe at the end of the eighteenth century, he described
hospitals with gardens for patients in Marseilles, Pisa, Constantinople,
Trieste, and Vienna. He especially commended the garden and open
arcade of the hospital of Santa Maria Nuova in Florence. In all these
hospitals he admired the flow of fresh air, the chance for patients to see
gardens through their windows and doorways, and the opportunity for
convalescent patients to walk in the gardens.4
But gardens attached to places of care and respite are more complicated
and many-layered than even these understandings suggest. They draw as
much upon a rich history of religious associations with gardens (especially
walled ornamental gardens) as places of retreat, meditation, and contain-
ers of memento mori (artefacts designed to allow people to reflect upon their
own mortality), as they do upon herbal gardens and naturalistic gardens
designed to emphasise the health-giving properties of the natural world.
The medieval walled garden might have contained sun-dials, water clocks,
arbours, and inscriptions, the latter often alluding to the transience of life,
the hours, the days and the seasons. Such gardens were not regarded as part
of nature but set apart, in some ways as an extension to the church or the
monastery.
They also acted as confessionals, as can be seen in the settings of a num-
ber of scenes in Shakespeare’s plays, in the novels of Jane Austen and in
the plot-lines of many other writers, becoming a key component of popu-
lar narrative devices and traditions. The conceit that the garden is a place

-- 161 of 203 --

I N A H O S P I C E G A R D E N 	147
where secrets are shared and people are brought to a clearer realisation of
their destiny, is common to many faiths and cultures, but has a particular
purchase on the imagination of those designing gardenesque landscapes
with therapeutic diversions in mind. Again, Rowbotham’s masterplan at
St Wilfrid’s had such intentions written into the brief, offering ‘the use of
intimate enclosure, courtyard spaces, for privacy, contemplation and sanc-
tuary’, mentioned in the previous chapter.
Such gardens have to delicately balance this older, meditative and rather
melancholy tradition with a newer sensibility created around the belief that
we are also a part of nature and that in surrendering to the natural world –
rather than placing our hopes in some higher, divine authority – we may
become strengthened and healed. In the hospice, of course, the healing can
only be of the spirit, as the body is by definition in serious decline and
close to death. Even so, many people find comfort in the idea that in dying,
like other species, we return to the earth. Nature, in this view, is our ally
and our ultimate home. Indeed, as the historian Simon Schama has indi-
cated – writing about the landscape paintings of Caspar David Friedrich,
whose evergreen forests appeared to promise hope of resurrection – such
images went ‘directly to the heart of one of our most powerful yearnings:
the craving to find in nature a consolation for our mortality’. 5 The contin-
uing growth of ‘natural’ or ‘woodland’ burial in the UK, again attests to
this modern kind of naturalistic spirituality. For this reason, therapeutic
gardens often include woodland and uncultivated elements, evocative of
a more primal world that was there before we came and may well be there
again long after we have gone.
Getting this balance right brings into play a long-standing debate
amongst garden historians as to whether gardens themselves are actually
de-natured, artificial constructions, too closely defined by human inten-
tionality – and even hubris – to be a part of nature in any meaningful sense
of the word. Certainly, the formal, aristocratic gardens created by the
Figure 8.2 Drawing of Orchard Garden at St Wilfrid’s Hospice.

-- 162 of 203 --

148 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
Elizabethans in Britain, or in Europe during the renaissance and baroque
periods, were exercises in the display of political and social power, as well
as demonstrating a grand-standing human mastery and control over nature.
This is evident in the punishing forms of topiary, espaliered trees, elegant
boulevards and allées, mechanical fountains and regulated water features
still to be seen in many even today. Yet, there is a place for the formal in the
hierarchy of privacy, as was clear in the masterplan for St Wilfrid’s.
The 18th century English ‘picturesque garden’ was a reaction against
this. In the words of William Kent, the new gardening sensibility had ‘leap’d
the fence and seen that all nature is a garden’. Kent had from the beginning
been highly critical of those over-formalised French and Italian gardens
with their terraces and promenades, which he described as like ‘going up
and down stairs in the open air’. 6 Whether gardens are part of nature or
whether nature is itself a kind of universal garden, remains an unresolved
question for landscape historians, philosophers, theologians and the like.
Eden and paradise (etymological origin, the Persian word pairi-daeza and
relatedly the Hebrew word pardes and the Greek paradeisos) represent both
the original natural world as well as an enclosed garden simultaneously in
theological history.7 The Islamic tradition of the ornamental garden was
another rich contribution to the history of what a cultivated landscape can
achieve in creating a place of tranquillity and meditation, and a symbol
of life now and to come. This tradition often employed extensive water
features to link interior and exterior spaces: mirror ponds reflected the
sky, while canals, elongated channels and rills, brought the sound of run-
ning water and a cooling effect to all parts. Paths took the form of sacred
geometric patterns, which the planting made three-dimensional. Yet there
are distinct differences between Christian and Islamic understandings of
what gardens are for and how they relate to human self-consciousness. The
historian Robert Pogue Harrison has dwelt fascinatingly upon this issue at
length, contrasting the Islamic emphasis – and that of other Eastern reli-
gions – on the serenity associated with gardens, compared with the sense
of an ‘unquiet heart’, drama and betrayal inherent in garden mythologies in
the Christian narrative. 8
The late avant-garde artist and film-maker, Derek Jarman, recounting the
creation of his exquisite shingle beach garden at Dungeness – perhaps the
most influential garden of modern times – nevertheless chose to call his
journal, Modern Nature. It is wise to accept that the boundaries between
nature and artifice can never be strictly demarcated. Such was also the view
of architect Charles Jencks of the Maggie’s Centres. Before Maggie Keswick
Jencks’ terminal diagnosis, they had started to work on an ambitious gar-
den at their home on the Scottish borders. His interests were in the latest
scientific findings as to the underlying structures of matter and movement;
hers were based on extensive studies of Chinese gardens. Together they
proposed ‘The Garden of Cosmic Speculation’ which Jencks subsequently
described at length. In the introduction to his book on the garden he wrote:
Thus the garden as a microcosm of the universe as a whole is quite
a familiar idea. The problem is that this cosmic orientation has been

-- 163 of 203 --

i n a Ho s P i C e g a r d e n 	149
obscured by later ideas and a thousand other concerns. I nevertheless
feel it is the most compelling motive behind the creation of a garden.
What is a garden if not a miniaturization, and celebration, of the place
we are in, the universe? 9
Where Jencks and Keswick broke new ground was in basing the garden on
the idea that ‘Patterns common to living and non-living matter are found
at all levels throughout the universe’. This goes well beyond the traditional
gardenesque view that the natural world was in itself entire, and wholly
organic. The ‘Garden of Cosmic Speculation’ weaves together both natural
elements – plants, flowers, shrubs, trees – with steel and concrete elements
which are based on patterns and fractal geometries. This fulfils their inten-
tion that the modern garden must represent the latest knowledge about the
structure of the universe available to humankind. Following her diagno-
sis, Maggie Keswick travelled with nurse and friend, Laura Lee – later to
become the CEO of Maggie’s Centres – to a number of cancer care projects
and ‘healing gardens’ in California. They returned and along with Charles
Jencks determined to put garden design at the centre of her, sadly post-
humous, project to create a British network of cancer care centres, rightly
celebrated today.
Hospice gardens in practice
The most ambitious and extensive landscape setting of all the hospices and
other places of care I have visited over the past two decades, is still to be
found at Vidarkliniken in Sweden. The landscape is stunning: gentle farm-
land interlaced with lakes and woodland. Much of the food served in the
Figure 8.3 Filtration ponds at Vidarkliniken, Sweden.

-- 164 of 203 --

150 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
hospital is grown locally, and most of the organic waste (and water) is recy-
cled naturally, establishing an even greater sense of connection between
the settlement, its patients, and the landscape. Here there is little attempt at
ornamental gardening, since the natural landscape itself is so uplifting and
pervasive. Though Vidarkliniken is owned and managed by an independent
trust associated with the anthroposophical spiritual movement established
by Rudolf Steiner, it nevertheless receives a large proportion of its funding
from the state, as it offers both conventional and anthroposophical treat-
ments. The integration of architecture and landscape is compelling.
Vidarkliniken is unique. Most hospice gardens employ a mixed assembly
of landscape and gardening elements, all of which help to meet a variety
of practical and psychological needs. In most modern hospice buildings,
each patient room opens out onto a flat terrace, and these are designed to
be fully accessible, even, as we have seen, to patients confined to bed. Often
these immediate outdoor elements are partitioned as small private gardens,
where bird-feeders and wind-chimes often abound. This design template,
in which the single patient room on the ground floor opens out onto its own
small patio, or partitioned terrace, is widely adopted. In America, Marion
Wilson, one of the pioneers of modern hospice care at the Texas Medical
Centre, outlined this spatial arrangement in fine detail when she estab-
lished the hospice in the early 1980s:
Nature had always played such an important role in my life. As a child,
trees and flowers had been what had connected me to the whole of the
universe and I felt that our patients would benefit physically, emotion-
ally and spiritually if they could see and have easy access to nature.
I envisioned all patient rooms to be on the ground floor. Each room
Figure 8.4 Lakeside pavilion at Vidarkliniken, Sweden, designed by Arne
Klingborg.

-- 165 of 203 --

i n a Ho s P i C e g a r d e n 	151
would have French doors that would allow even the beds to be rolled
out and on to the garden paths or if the patient was unable to be moved,
the doors could be opened and patients could see, hear and feel the
beauty of nature. I wanted bird feeders to be placed throughout the
garden so that the sounds of their songs and their joyful chirping could
be easily heard by our patients.10
The close inter-relationship between indoor and outdoor spaces to be found
in many hospital pavilion wards and hospices draws on one of the more
positive aspects of modernism, which was the intention to connect the two
(though the principle was already at work in the Arts and Crafts move-
ment). It was evident in the design of such iconic private houses as Serge
Chermayeff’s Bentley Wood in Surrey (built 1938) which, as Alan Powers
has written,
achieved the Modernist desire to merge indoor and outdoor space. The
big timber-framed sliding windows ran in recessed tracks, so that there
was no sill at ground level, and the stone of the terrace was continued
as a strip indoors.11
Similar detailing can be seen in a number of recent hospices, where indoor
and outdoor floor surfaces continue across wall boundaries. An additional,
and excellent, detail can be seen at St Richard’s Hospice in Worcester,
where all doorways leading from the patient rooms into the garden are also
fitted with an insect screen, so that the door can be left open without flies,
wasps or other insects entering the patient room.
It was also evident in the design of open-air schools across Europe,
where ground level terraces and upper floor balconies could be opened up
Figure 8.5 Rear gardens and patios at St Richard’s Hospice, Worcester.

-- 166 of 203 --

152 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
to children by the use of sliding windows and moveable wall units, as well
as in sanatorium pavilions. Furthermore, it was for a while a central princi-
ple of the design of nurseries and kindergarten that the space for children
should be allocated as one-third entirely self-enclosed, one-third canopied
and one-third open garden, allowing children to move freely between three
different distinct environments. This espousal of partly sheltered spaces
which combine indoor and outdoor elements was given a Japanese inflexion
by Dan Pearson at Maggie’s London, in Hammersmith.
The inter-relationship between indoors and outdoors can be achieved
practically and visually by other means too. Interior courtyard gardens –
some accessible, some not – can be used to break up and provide light in
inner areas of a building. Canopies, pergolas, balcony overhangs, loggias,
and other elements help break down the strict boundaries between indoors
and outside, as does the use of indoor plants, water features and light wells.
The innovative concept of the ‘Macmillan Green’ was developed by the
Architects Design Partnership in 1987 as a design model for Macmillan
Cancer Care Centres.12 It proposed that each building should have a cen-
tral activity area in a large open-plan inner piazza, where there would be a
coffee bar, dining room, waiting lounge, fireside area, TV corner, all con-
nected to an outdoor terrace and gardens which might include aviaries, fish
ponds, raised flower beds and herb gardens.
In North America, the University of Wisconsin Cancer Center was
designed by architects OWP/P as,
…an inside-out kind of building. By acknowledging the landscape, the
building becomes a part of it. People actually take their treatments
outside. By pulling the building’s components apart to allow light in,
the sky and the landscape become as much a part of the palette as the
carpet or brick.13
The extensive wall glazing at Maggie’s Glasgow, designed by Rem Kool-
haas, and at Amanda Levete’s Maggie’s Southampton, for example achieve
precisely this inter-relationship between indoors and outdoors. The for-
mer looks out on to a garden design by Lily Jencks, the latter, as discussed,
designed by Sarah Price. There have been other historical precedents.
Dutch architecture was especially innovative in the 1920s and 1930s in pro-
moting the breakdown of the divide between internal and external spaces
in hospital buildings, a characteristic which architect Herman Hertzberger
traced back to the fascination of painters such as Vermeer and De Hooch for
interiors which are open on all sides by windows and doors, often revealing
small, rear gardens, just visible through a front door.
This pre-occupation with harmonising the world of the house and the
world of the garden was also distinctive in the work of Frank Lloyd Wright,
whose frequent use of canopies, overhangs, and long horizontal structures
which knitted together interior and exterior, remains influential. The
influence of Frank Lloyd Wright is explicitly acknowledged at St Oswald’s
Hospice near Newcastle upon Tyne, with its intertwining verandahs and
courtyards. Wright himself enjoyed the challenge of a difficult site – an

-- 167 of 203 --

i n a Ho s P i C e g a r d e n 	153
issue discussed in an earlier chapter – because ‘no house should ever be on
any hill or on anything. It should be of the hill. Belonging to it. Hill and
house should live together, each the happier for the other’.14
Further away from the buildings, hospice gardens tend to become more
naturalistic, and indeed may include wooded areas where children (of both
visitors and patients) can play. Where possible the most important elements
of the landscape prior to development are conserved, including mature
trees, shrubs, ponds and other existing landscape features. At St Richard’s
Hospice in Worcester, the gardens were developed around a badger sett
in order to preserve it. At the same time a wildflower meadow adjacent
to the main garden was retained and enhanced as part of the agreement
to develop. At Princess Alice Hospice in Esher, the rear gardens open out
on to a wooded bluff, beyond which lies the local golf course. St Francis
Hospice near Berkhamsted is set in traditional mixed farmland, at some
distance from the nearest town, and is modelled on a traditional farmhouse
enclave appropriate to its setting and that of its neighbours. In Cork, Mary-
mount University Hospital and Hospice has been quietly settled into one
small element of an extensive and long-established field system.
Floral symbolism
A number of other common themes have emerged. These elements com-
bine together to form a common palette in the design of gardens and land-
scapes which emphasise the wider relationship between the environment
and a sense of human wellbeing. In traditional public gardens or ornamental
landscapes, the bedding out of colourful annuals in neat displays according
Figure 8.6 Maggie’s Glasgow: glazed walls looking out on to woodland.

-- 168 of 203 --

154 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
to a pre-designed plan, no longer seems appropriate to the hospice gar-
den, which seeks to embody the principles of slow adaptation and change.
A modern concern with sustainability and ecology, with plant systems and
eco-systems that largely self-manage themselves, also provides a basis for
garden designs that are plant-based rather than pattern-based, or modelled
on a design imported from elsewhere. Of course, in many places of residen-
tial palliative and social care, both can be found. At Lark Hill Village, the
estate contains allotments, large vegetated ponds, as well as formal planting
close to the flats and bungalows. Squeezed on to a tight site in Bermond-
sey, the high-density Appleby Blue almshouse has only room to offer for-
mal courtyard and rooftop gardens with a high level of structural planting,
which will doubtless need to be kept in close order, rather than let run wild.
It is apparent, for example, that Japanese gardening traditions have been
successfully emulated in many places, notably evident in the adroit assem-
bly of water features, rocks, stones, raked gravel and occasional outbreaks
of trimmed shrubbery. Such self-contained gardens are to be looked at, not
entered, and form places for reflection rather than immersion or explo-
ration. As Sarah Price told me, speaking of her work at Maggie’s South-
ampton, she is personally sceptical about using fixed or static elements in a
garden designed to replenish the emotions of those for whom life’s finitude
is now confirmed, preferring trees, plants and shrubs which emphasise flow,
change, shifting patterns of light, the impious movement and sound of small
birds, and the shock and drama of sudden rainfall or strong winds.
Landscape gardener, Dan Pearson, confirmed this in connection with his
involvement in the design of Maggie’s London. He was most emphatic on
the importance of the Japanese tradition in modern garden design, particu-
larly where matters of privacy and reflection are concerned, arguing that
Figure 8.7 Lark Hill ExtraCare apartments with floral landscaping.

-- 169 of 203 --

i n a Ho s P i C e g a r d e n 	155
the Japanese garden exemplifies a concentration on ‘only the things that
matter’, in which emotions and feelings are ‘finely tuned’. They are ideal
compositions to be contained within the grounds of a hospice, where emo-
tions can run high. Another writer on the aesthetics of place-making, Alain
de Botton, has shrewdly summarised what it is about the Japanese tradition
that now seems to make it especially relevant to hospice garden design:
In medieval Japan, poets and Zen priests directed the Japanese
towards aspects of the world to which Westerners have seldom publicly
accorded more than negligible or casual attention: cherry blossoms,
deformed pieces of pottery, raked gravel, moss, rain falling on leaves,
autumn skies, roof tiles and unvarnished wood. A word emerged, wahi,
of which no Western language, tellingly, has a direct equivalent, which
identified beauty with unpretentious, simple, unfinished, transient
things.15
This predilection for the sculptural rather than the chaotic, for the mini-
malist rather than the over-ripe, for the palette of muted colours evocative
of the desert or the seashore rather than the luscious and riotous colours of
the jungle, is becoming much more acceptable today in Western gardening
traditions. A predisposition towards the cooler, quieter colours seems now
to be preferred, as against the blood-red roses, brilliant orange and fiery
colours which are too often associated with passion, drama and destruc-
tion. Rose gardens have their heady delights but in their slow decay can
be depressing. Although there was ‘a modernist attempt to jettison such
elitist elements as allusion, metaphor and allegory’, according to landscape
historian Katie Campbell, it is surely truer to say that there is a new spirit
of natural philosophy and ecological allegory abroad.16 The modern public
garden seeks to imbue each individual with a sense of personal grace and
occasion, through elements of natural allegory and poetics, rather than to
impose a fixed or preconceived meaning upon things.
At Maggie’s London, Pearson emphasised the importance of commis-
sioning and placing sculptures and artworks in such gardens, as they also
give cause for open-ended stimulation and reflection. The carved stones
by Hannah Bennett announce a solidity and grounding of the building in
the approach pathway. At Maggies Glasgow, Charles Jencks designed and
installed a DNA sculpture, while at Maggies Inverness he has designed a
set of garden mounds adjacent to the building designed by architect David
Page which ‘represent cells sending messages back and forth to harmonise
imbalances in the organism’.17 In the Inverness example, the landscape has
been described as twisting ‘in to David Page’s interior spaces making a sin-
gle entity of the inside and outside’.18
One of the most artfully designed gardens can be found at Trinity Hos-
pice in Clapham, London, where in 1983 the entire site was designed and
laid out by John Medhurst and David Foreman as a memorial to the highly
regarded landscape gardener Lanning Roper, who died that year.19 The
hospice garden is surrounded by a high wall and feels deeply enclosed; it
is also large enough for people to walk in it and lose sight of the hospice

-- 170 of 203 --

156 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
building itself, ‘allowing families to step back and review their circum-
stances with a little objectivity’, according to its gardener. 20 A variety of
seating areas are provided, both sheltered and out in the open, and a small
meadow has been created sown with wildflower seeds, including cowslips,
primrose, dog daisies, fritillarias, as well as flowering cherries and daffodils.
Patients are encouraged to make leaf rubbings, and to inscribe soft copper
tags which are temporarily hung on a wishing tree (the folklore ascribing
spirits to trees is almost universal).
In Ireland, at the old Marymount Hospice in Cork and at Our Lady’s
Hospice in Dublin, both sets of gardens contain a number of religious
statues and grottoes. To the non-religious they can seem wholly inappro-
priate, yet there is a sense in which they have become an indelible part of
the Irish (and indeed Catholic Mediterranean) landscape, where roadside
shrines, statues of the Blessed Virgin Mary, and crucifixes are ubiquitous.
So deep is the Christian tradition embodied in metaphors of the garden as
a place of both suffering and self-doubt (Gethsemane), as well as of ulti-
mate innocence and happiness (Eden), that it remains almost impossible
to entirely erase these associations which come down to us through books,
poems, films and paintings, whether we subscribe to Christian narratives
or not.
Some have claimed that these deep attachments are hard-wired. The
geographer Jay Appleton has argued – somewhat controversially – the
neo-Darwinian case that our relationship to various kinds of landscape is
instinctual. It is, he says, related to atavistic concerns with survival, though
with the added twist that the pleasure principle is also at work in alerting
us to what we like and dislike in adaptive and self-preserving ways. Thus,
Facets of behaviour like seeing, hiding, sheltering, escaping and so on,
are central to our strategy for survival and the recognition of opportu-
nities to put this strategy into practice causes us pleasure. 21
Whether our instinctive reaction to the open savannah or the deep forest,
which is palpably real, can be scaled down to a Darwinian set of responses to
the light and shade of the domestic garden remains debatable. Nevertheless,
there are many other ways as we have seen – cultural, bio-evolutionary,
religious, even eco-political – that tie the human psyche to the experience
of landscape and garden.
There are also practical issues, which in turn have their psychological
and symbolic aspects too. Trees and flowers which bloom and die quickly,
scattering dead petals and leaves everywhere, may not be the best thing
to plant immediately outside a dying patient’s room. The use of cut flow-
ers and formal floral arrangements poses cultural difficulties for others.
My own grandmother adamantly opposed their use, believing that flow-
ers belong only where they grow. Outside they are a part of living nature,
inside, ornamentally arranged they become part of a ritual setting, replete
with other meanings, including the certainty of mortality – which is why
so many paintings of flowers are imbued with a sense of transitory life and
splendour before becoming nature morte. A number of hospices make use of

-- 171 of 203 --

i n a Ho s P i C e g a r d e n 	157
flowers recently used ornamentally in funeral wreaths, which are deliv-
ered to them following funerals by undertakers or crematorium staff. They
are, however, re-arranged before the patients see them. Symbolically such
flowers are brought in at the back door – from which the hearses come and
go – before they are made into new arrangements to decorate tables, halls
and individual rooms.
Back in the garden, however, the drift planting of large swaths of per-
ennials – rosemary, lavender – is more effective than the traditional bed-
ding out of annuals which, when taken out for replacement, was once
described by the eminent Victorian gardener, William Robinson, as ‘the
dreadful practice of tearing up the flower beds and leaving them like new-
dug graves’. 22 Hardly the right impression to make in a hospice garden.
Swathes of long-lasting muted colours in curvilinear sweeps of perennials
and tall grasses not only fill the air with scents, but are more sustainable
both in ecological and practical terms. This tradition of dense planting of
perennials, once a feature of gardens designed by Gertrude Jekyll, and in
recent times brought to a new art by Dutch gardener, Piet Oudolf, is likely
to become more common, though it should not be confused with the low-
cost shrub-planting now a feature of shopping mall car parks, where dense
shrubbery acts as a trap for wind-blown rubbish and is rarely ever attended
to again once planted even if it is occasionally litter-picked.
People have considered these issues before, especially in relation to the
symbolic and psychological effects of certain plant-types, tree-stock and
configurations of garden elements. Here is the German writer, Christian
Cay Lorenz Hirschfield (1741–1792) writing on the matter in the late 18th
century:
Sad conifers should not be used but trees with light and coloured
leaves and flowering and fragrant shrubs and flowers. A hospital gar-
den should have everything to encourage the enjoyment of nature and
to promote a healthy life. It should help forget weakness and worries
and encourage a positive outlook; everything in it should be serene
and happy. No scene of melancholy, no memorial of mortality should
be permitted to intrude. 23
Running water is always preferable to still (let alone stagnant) water, as are
plants, shrubs and trees which bow and sigh in the wind to be preferred
to those which sag or collapse in a heap upon themselves. Light, wind and
water are all sources of energy, and can rally people and enliven them, some-
thing static hard-surfaced landscapes fail to do. 24 Perhaps the finest artis-
tic allusion to these hidden energies is the kinetic sculpture, ‘Four Open
Squares’, by American artist, George Rickey, mounted in the large pond
in the gardens of Trinity Hospice, Clapham, London. The ever-changing
configuration of patterns of these overlapping metal shapes, driven only by
wind and air currents, is almost hypnotic. The same garden also contains a
fan-shaped bronze weir designed by sculptor William Pye, which bubbles
and cascades constantly, the sounds drifting through the open doors and
windows of the neighbouring day centre. 25

-- 172 of 203 --

158 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
These gardens have to reflect seasonal change, changes in the weather,
and provide a constantly moving and sensory experience of the natural world
at its most artful and consolatory. Not all gardens are entirely successful,
certainly in the eyes of practised landscape designers and garden-lovers.
Where there is complaint it is usually that the design is too conventional –
‘bland modernism’ one critic wrote to me – alluding to imported patterns
and features from what is currently fashionable in modern patio gardening,
and which pay too little attention to the specific qualities and history of
the site.
Where a modernist approach to garden design is successfully achieved, as
I think it is in the separate walled garden features at New Farleigh Hospice
in Chelmsford and at the Northern Ireland Children’s Hospice in Belfast,
then it brings many rewards. The garden at New Farleigh Hospice designed
by Jill Lawrence was executed by landscape architect, Mark Bentley, and
the beautiful glass sphere located at its centre, donated by Alison Armour.
Though somewhat detached from the immediate garden surrounding the
hospice buildings themselves, the walled garden has a very strong identity
and ambience of its own. The walled memorial garden in Belfast is equally
strong, yet serene.
It is the fact that ‘there is no repetition in nature’, that in the organic
world everything is constantly changing, that makes the garden such a
constant source of fascination. 26 It is the antithesis of the indoor world and
its routinised time patterns, clocks, visiting schedules, and unvarying col-
ours, walls and corridors. Time spent outdoors is qualitatively different
from time spent indoors, and supports the phenomenological view that
Figure 8.8 ‘Four Open Squares’: sculpture by American artist, George Rickey,
in the gardens of Trinity Hospice, Clapham, London.

-- 173 of 203 --

i n a Ho s P i C e g a r d e n 	159
‘the conventional distinction between time and space is untenable’. 27 In a
garden time and space are interwoven, threaded by the scents, sounds and
physical impressions of the natural world. Waiting, primarily an indoor
experience marked out by mechanical time, is transformed into inhabit-
ing, and being in touch with a world that – with its weathers and seasons,
living, dying and renewal processes – preceded human history, and will
endure long after.
Nevertheless, in spite of these deep attachment people feel for the nat-
ural world at times of crisis, nearly all hospice managers resist requests to
plant commemorative trees or shrubs following the deaths of patients, or
allow plaques or benches with inscriptions to be donated and installed in
the gardens. What the hospice garden cannot be, I was told time and time
again, is a memorial garden. It has to be imbued with a sense of life, change
and hope for the future, and ‘the journey ahead’. For such reasons the hos-
pice garden might be said to be a new garden type, mixing elements of the
ornamental, the naturalistic and the ceremonial, while attempting to avoid
too obvious references to more established memorialising traditions.
Indeed, an increasing number of these memorialising practices now hap-
pen elsewhere in the landscape beyond the boundaries of the hospice setting,
whether it is in a cemetery, a crematorium garden, or through the planting
of a memorial tree or placing a commemorative bench in a local park. The
practice of removing ashes from the crematorium for private disposal at a
favourite beauty spot, or even interred in a domestic garden, is now wide-
spread, notably in the UK. In a number of European countries however the
practice remains outlawed. Some anthropologists are now suggesting that
the once-vernacular landscape is becoming increasingly memorialised as
Figure 8.9 Walled garden with globe at New Farleigh Hospice, Chelmsford,
designed by Jill Lawrence, with Mark Bentley.

-- 174 of 203 --

160 	i n a H o s P i C e g a r d e n
people desert the familiar bounded landscapes of the cemetery in favour of
other local sites and acts of informal commemoration.
While most hospice gardens have been designed by professional land-
scape architects, their ongoing maintenance is usually in the hands of
volunteers – not always an entirely satisfactory situation. There is a strong
case to be made for accepting that a coherent plan and planting programme
for a designed landscape can only improve and strengthen in time, and
needs to be understood in terms of its original conceptual framework. A
beautiful garden can, like an elegant building, be easily compromised or
spoiled by inappropriate additions, changes, or modifications. These are
long-term issues, only now being addressed.
The rapid growth of the modern hospice movement suggests that it is
meeting medical and spiritual needs which conventional institutional pro-
vision for the sick and the dying are no longer seen to provide – if they ever
did. It also reflects new anxieties about the place and time of death which
in a largely post-religious society have not been fully understood. It is evi-
dent from the many visits that I have made over the past two decades that
the care attending the dying and their friends and families in the majority
of hospices is exceptional, and humbling to observe. One element of this
care at the end of life, is a clear consideration of the benefits to be gained
by views of, and access to, the sights and sounds of the natural world –
even if this version of ‘nature’ is enhanced by the cultivation of a sensitively
designed if sometimes over-cultivated garden. One can only envy those
who do spend their last hours in the open air, in close contact with elements
of the natural world.
As new materials, technologies and design principles develop, which
stress not only that buildings can heal, but can also sit lightly on the earth,
then another piece fits into the jigsaw as to how we may inhabit the earth
meaningfully. This argument has been neatly phrased in an essay by Bill
Walsh in a recent book on environmentally-friendly healthcare design:
Indeed, how long will it be before we expect our buildings to be living
buildings that are net contributors to the communities they populate,
generating their own energy with renewable resources, capturing and
treating all of the water used on-site, and using all materials effica-
ciously to maximize the health and beauty of our world? Not long.
This is the nature of the tectonic shift in public policy toward health
and environment that is foreshadowed by the trailblazers, strate-
gies and case studies encountered in this book (Sustainable Healthcare
Architecture). 28
We have already seen how the decision made at St Francis Hospice in
Berkhamsted to supply their own water from a borehole excavated deep
into the landscape on which the building stands, ended up possessing a rich
symbolism of its own. There will be more symbolism of this kind to come
as the design of buildings becomes ever more driven by the imperative to
make a pact with the ecology of the earth and its long-term future.
