While the hospice movement continues its mutation, post-Covid, from a
predominantly inpatient facility to an outward-looking community locus
of palliative care, ethical issues remain with regard to the time and condi-
tions in which people die. This raises questions as to how design can shape
a sense of privileged ritual and ethos within what are sometimes perceived
as functional, formally medicalised buildings. It may no longer be enough
simply to add a chapel or a sanctuary space to a conventional residential
setting; somehow, the hospice setting in its entirety has to be imbued with
a sense of place and occasion. However, modernism itself has had a problem
with accommodating ritual, especially rituals associated with ill-health and
mortality, being a future-oriented project rather than one drawing lessons
largely from the past. Nevertheless, many hospices – and other forms of res-
idential and palliative care provision discussed in this book – are designing
buildings and interiors which foster hope and human communion and bring
assurance to people and a sense of meaningful time and appropriate ending.
Continuing to expand across the world, palliative care has become one
of the great social innovations of modern times. Yet, it is also clear that as
new treatments develop, which either stabilise terminal conditions or pro-
long remission, then the role of hospice care changes too. There was a time
when HIV/AIDS was anticipated as requiring a whole new network of ded-
icated hospice buildings. However, new drug programmes and other sup-
port systems have largely obviated the need for such a major construction
programme, even though hospice services continued to grow. Regrettably,
this has not been the case in many poorer countries where HIV continues to
lead to debilitating sickness and early death, and where the hospice move-
ment still struggles to grow in conditions of inadequate funding and even
official hostility in some countries or regions.
The UK hospice movement has changed character remarkably too,
even in a relatively short time. No longer a place of no return, hospices
continue to provide a therapeutic sanctuary to which many hundreds
of people come and go as day-patients, or are visited in their homes, as
part of a network of community care. Architect Ian Clarke described St
Oswald’s Hospice near Newcastle upon Tyne as a ‘living village’, with
up to 200 out-patients visiting each day to access a wide range of activ-
ities, treatments and therapies, in an atmosphere of general wellbeing.
Cicely Saunders said the same of St Christopher’s, that, ‘We’re a village.
The evening land 	9

-- 178 of 203 --

164 	t H e e V e n i n g L a n d
You don’t necessarily know everybody’s name in the house, but you know
their faces. We’re a reaction against the impersonal medical city’.1 The
same is even more obviously true of settlements such as Lark Hill Retire-
ment Village, and many almshouse developments. Inpatient care is now a
smaller part of the work of the modern hospice, and even then, up to half
of all inpatients return home to die within a managed programme of pain
relief and pastoral care.
As programmes change, so designs for buildings change or adapt accord-
ingly. The traditional day care room, formerly a large room filled with
chairs arranged in circles, is disappearing. It is being replaced by open-
plan café-courts, from which out-patients go to attend various programmes,
including complementary therapies such as aromatherapy, massages, along
with the use of hydrotherapy pools and other treatments. At the new St
Wilfrid’s Hospice in Eastbourne, discussed in Chapter 7, according to
writer and researcher, John Harries, ‘everyone arriving at the hospice walks
past the window of a gym to get into the building’. 2 This would have been
unimaginable 20 years ago, yet gym provision is now symbolic of the new
commitment to a new ethos of ‘re-enablement’ as a central element of hos-
pice care today.
‘There is a new wind blowing through the hospice movement’, Harries
continues, ‘which embraces concepts of wellbeing such as eudaimonia
(human flourishing) and existential mobility, and looks to “help patients
live as fully as possible until death”’. This emphasis on mobility and pur-
poseful activity rather than a restful retreat from life should apply to all
forms of caring for older people some now believe – and it has important
design implications. If this trend from ‘dwelling’ in the philosophical (Hei-
deggerian) sense to mobility is continued – or as Harries puts it, rather more
elegantly, making a choice between ‘the wellbeing of rest and containment
in dwelling, and the pleasures of activity and movement’ – then this repre-
sents a new chapter in the story of later life care.
‘Hospices have pioneered a better way of dying,’ Professor Sheila Payne
has argued, noting that:
They have concentrated on cancer deaths, but only 25% of us die from
cancer: so what about the other 75%? Don’t they deserve a good death
too? And hospices also tend to deal with younger people, while older
people are the ones more likely to be dying. So what we’re doing at the
moment is looking at how to extend the excellent care people get into
hospices into the other places where people die.3
This has timely relevance in the post-Covid world, from which we learned
belatedly that residential care homes at times might be places overwhelmed
by sudden high levels of mortality, with little experience or resources to rise
to the challenge. In the past commentators have urged the hospice move-
ment to widen its social catchment and reaching out to hidden reservoirs of
need, noting that too often ‘hospices appear as white, middle class, Chris-
tian institutions, serving a carefully selected group of patients’.4 In the dec-
ade and a half since the first edition of Modern Hospice Design was published,

-- 179 of 203 --

t H e e V e n i n g L a n d 	165
most hospices have greatly widened their user profile through ‘hospice at
home’ programmes, as well as greater day-patient services.
It is likely that in future the hospice model will gain much of its prestige
and continuing sense of purpose through the ability to inspire and improve
end-of-life care across the widening range of institutions – almshouses,
care homes, hospital wards and retirement villages – where the elderly and
the terminally ill are now cared for, rather than going it alone. This was
the opinion of Julia Neuberger, who has said that the hospice movement
has already been a catalyst for change in other sectors of provision, includ-
ing many nursing homes, and this influence is likely to grow. 5 Residential
care homes for the elderly have much to learn from hospices, as the former
expand to keep up with demographic demand, and become the most com-
mon place in which people die. Neuberger expects the form and funding of
such provision to vary too, with changing roles for the private, public and
not-for-profit sectors in encouraging innovation and better design based on
the success of the hospice model.
The continuing development of other initiatives within the specialist
palliative care sector – such as the palliative care units now operated by
Macmillan Cancer Support, the hospices and services run by Marie Curie,
as well as the growing network of Maggie’s Centres – have provided a wel-
come incentive to the design agenda. As Ian Clarke once admitted, ‘We need
the innovation provided by the Maggie’s Centres to keep us architecturally
on the spectrum’. They now form part of that long continuum of provision
for those suffering from life-limiting illnesses, whether in the form of chil-
dren’s hospices and family respite centres, adult drop-in centres, inpatient
units in hospices, palliative care wards in general hospitals, and extensive
outreach programmes undertaken by hospices and hospitals everywhere.
In turn this movement is part of a wider concern with the quality of life of
the elderly, now gathering interest in political and medical welfare circles,
discussed in the opening chapter. 6 The ‘right to a good death’ is becoming
part of a new kind of social contract that is not going to go away.
Some of this certainly relates to architectural and design matters, and
the growing influence of the perceived success of ‘evidence-based design’,
summarised in Chapter 2. The UK Department of Health Estates and
Facilities Division (formerly NHS Estates) together with The King’s Fund
collaborated on a pioneering refurbishment programme called Enhancing
the Healing Environment Programme 2003–2005 which detailed 23 case studies
in hospital and hospice improvements to existing buildings using imagi-
native art, architectural, design and landscape initiatives.7 Similar such
programmes could be expanded at relatively little cost and to great effect
in hospitals and care homes throughout the UK. The same is true of the
consistently thoughtful work done by the Hospice Friendly Hospitals Pro-
gramme of the Irish Hospice Foundation, leading up to the publication of
Design & Dignity Guidelines for Physical Environments supporting End-of-Life
Care, in 2008. 8 Both support the conclusion of the report by Bryan Lawson
and Michael Phiri for NHS Estates, that most of the benefits which accrue
from improved design ‘appear to be generic place-making features rather
than hospital-specific factors’.9

-- 180 of 203 --

166 	t H e e V e n i n g L a n d
A time and a place to die
Yet, while the buildings improve, and those providing mainstream hospi-
tal services learn some of the lessons of hospice care and culture, equally
urgent ethical issues underlie the debate about what it means to die within
an institution. This is particularly the case where the institution is a place
where medical treatment sometimes conflicts with more existential issues
as to the time and place of death, along with the inherent ambiguities sur-
rounding the desire to prolong life at any cost rather than admit the ‘failure’
of medical science to suspend or defer mortality. As clinician Atul Gawande
reminds us, ‘Death, of course, is not a failure’.10
According to Gawande, patients who receive palliative care may well
live longer than those ‘who fight death with technology and medication’.11
Furthermore, hospice or almshouse care costs less than being kept alive
in a hospital ward wired up to monitors and dependent on feeding tubes,
with emergency trips to the CPR suite for dramatic resuscitation. Yet still
in many mainstream hospitals, a fraught battle still ensues on occasions
between the competing values of human resignation and solace in the face
of imminent death and technological can-do wizardry. In the USA, how-
ever, private medical provision often has a vested interest in prolonging
medical treatment, the costs being recouped from claims against the expen-
sive insurance policies of their patients. According to Gawande, ‘We’ve cre-
ated a multi-trillion-dollar edifice for dispensing the medical equivalent of
lottery tickets (despite) the near certainty that those tickets will not win’.12
Even so, ethical problems remain. As one eminent American doctor has
noted,
Few can turn quietly to the wall to go peacefully. For one thing, hos-
pitals are too expensive a place to await a quiet death. For another, we
doctors and nurses are not good at waiting. Our house staff are young
and confident, eager to prove their mettle. In intensive care units the
dying find no rest, but are kept in motion by machines blowing up their
lungs and beating their hearts; electrical connections sometimes seem
more important than the bodies they serve. There are few last words
from dying patients because most have tubes in their throats; the staff
are too busy shouting orders to hear them anyway.13
A doctor friend once told me, with a degree of self-criticism on his part,
that ‘Medicine is a performative art – it has to be seen to be doing things’.
The design and culture of rooms, wards or suites of medical services where
everything is geared to sudden and unexpected emergency procedures, is
another world to that where people have accepted to abide in company and
loving care for an inevitable conclusion. The design of the places where
people are helped to come to terms with approaching death, needs to be
clear as to how the palliative and clinical functions are demarcated, or made
to fit in congruence with each other. This principle naturally applies more
to hospitals than to hospices.

-- 181 of 203 --

t H e e V e n i n g L a n d 	167
From the experience of personally attending the final moments of two
close elderly relatives – at separate times and in two different hospitals –
both remain lodged in memory as deeply distressing occasions. Each
occurred in the frenetic conditions of an emergency suite, where medi-
cal staff were in open disagreement with nursing staff over the desirability
of resuscitation. Family members were left as bystanders, hovering at the
edges of the room uncertain as to how and when the ultimate decision was
going to be made – and by whom. This is not what anybody wanted. In both
cases mature female nursing staff were in conflict with young male doctors.
It was clear to all of us which of the two parties in disagreement had the
experience and deeper understanding of what was happening in that room,
to which the dying person was not party, though we were.
As one learns through visiting hospitals and hospices over the years,
there are distinct gender issues and conflicts at work in both architecture
and medicine around issues of design, care, and human wellbeing. Many
architects and doctors are men; most of those working in hospitals and hos-
pices are women. Nevertheless, one writer has declared that ‘hospices are a
female sphere of influence’.14 For male professionals, the hospital or hospice
is more likely to be perceived technically as a place where bodies are trans-
ported, diagnosed, accommodated, fed, washed, treated, and sent out again
or removed for disposal; the building is a shell for complex systems and
procedures. For women working as nurses or care staff the same places are
more likely to be perceived and experienced as places of familial care and
solicitude: the building is a set of domestic spaces. The emotional economy
of the hospice is largely in the hands of women. While clinicians may worry
that infection is contagious, nursing staff may equally worry that ‘distress
is contagious’ too, and that responding to the distress or grief of people
in a hospital or hospice is just as important clinically.15 There are design
implications here too.
There is a genuine dilemma for hospitals here obviously, and is dealt with
as a key issue in the Irish report, Death and Dying in an Acute Hospital, where
it was noted that
The focus in acute medicine was on treating people rather than deal-
ing with the reality of dying. One of the most popular funeral poems
is Christina Rossetti’s poem, ‘Let Me Go’, which precisely entreats her
friends to remember her and to ‘Miss me, but let me go’. The inherent
tension between ‘letting people go’ and ‘doing everything we can’ was
an ongoing challenge.16
By common agreement the principal function of hospitals is to save lives, to
repair broken bodies, or to treat other conditions with drugs and therapy,
returning people to the outside world as well as possible. It is not yet con-
sidered part of their core activity to help people die, let alone die well. This
is why the philosopher Norbert Elias concluded his pessimistic work on
modern dying with the observation that, ‘It is perhaps not quite superfluous
to say that care for people sometimes lags behind care for their organs’.17

-- 182 of 203 --

168 	t H e e V e n i n g L a n d
A senior nurse in a Stockholm hospital admitted this dilemma when she
told me that it ‘was simply not possible to die with dignity if the patient is
wired up to machines’. On the other hand, it was also thought too alarming
to family members keeping vigil to start switching off machines or dis-
connecting patients who were clearly in their last hours or minutes, as this
might look uncaring or precipitate, even though it might allow death to
come more speedily and naturally. The religious commentator Karen Arm-
strong has written of the despair into which she sank during the last days
of her mother’s life in hospital. According to Armstrong, her mother’s final
days were ‘deflected from its course’. She went on to write that, ‘Without
drips and antibiotics her ordeal would have been over weeks ago. She had
to wait until the nurse could no longer find a vein to medicate her and
she could die of an awful bowel infection’.18 Armstrong described this grim
period as a testing time for faith: ‘The religions tell us that life is sacred; but
my mother’s plight was an affront to that holiness’.
There are other forms which medical intervention may take in the future
in the treatment of the seriously ill and the dying, notably in the use of
hallucinogenic or psychotropic drugs. Trials have already been conducted
in administering psilocybin to patients with terminal cancers, and the his-
torian (and futurologist), Francis Fukuyama, sees the expansion of mind-
altering drugs as an inevitable part of the medicine of tomorrow.19 This
raises profound issues for those involved in the design of the hospitals and
hospices of the next generation, as it could be asked in decades to come, why
change the environment when you can simply alter the mind inhabiting it?
Over-medication is not uncommon when staffing and resources are limited.
Palliative care is not just a building-based practice, but an experience
where the philosophy of what it is to be a mortal human is tested to the
full – as is the process of dying. Professor Allan Kellehear has been fiercely
critical of contemporary cultures of dying in modern societies, which he
claims reduce this most profound period in any person’s life to a technical
matter of faulty bodily parts and physical entropy. ‘Now’, he has written,
recognition, even the definition of dying, becomes the domain of for-
mal organisations such as nursing homes or governments. In this insti-
tutional shift, millions of people in nursing homes are not officially
defined as ‘dying’ people but merely chronically ill or disabled. 20
For Kellehear, dying is an existential event in life that may take years –
rather than days or months – to reach completion, and requires an appro-
priate social and cultural response on the part of others. He repeats the
frequently heard complaint that we have hidden the dying away from
everyday life and domestic settings, and implies that, as a result, the society
we live in is diminished in the process. This comes back to the issue raised
at the beginning of this book, that the elderly and the dying today lack
social status, are indeed the ‘clandestine’ beings described by Stephen Ver-
derber & Ben J. Refuerzo, cited in Chapter 2. In the modern world, Kelle-
hear writes, dying ‘is only recognised if it is viewed as an end-of-life care
experience under formal medical supervision’. 21

-- 183 of 203 --

t H e e V e n i n g L a n d 	169
If this is the case, then both the hospice and the hospital – though espe-
cially the latter – need to attend more strenuously to matters of ritual, time,
being, and, ultimately, non-being. In one Irish hospital visited, I was told
that a preoccupation with health and safety now prevented the opening
of windows manually, the lighting of candles, the use of venetian blinds to
block out miserable views of the local cemetery, or the taking of a number
of other little initiatives with which people wished to temporarily person-
alise the single rooms in which their friends or relatives were dying. The
opportunity to quickly create a different mise-en-scène through the use of
lighting, personal belongings (such as family photographs), the addition of
flowers – so common to the dramaturgy of religious ritual – is now too
often disallowed. Yet, at the end of life it is, as Hannah Arendt once wrote,
the petit bonheur of small private spaces and things, which ultimately comfort
those in distress or suffering.
In her ‘Manifesto for Old Age’, Rabbi and member of the UK House of
Lords, Julia Neuberger, wrote that:
Sometimes concern with making peace with God and human beings
is stronger than worry about pain or discomfort. Sometimes a desire
to perform a ritual is more important than the need to be washed,
or to take drugs. The reason for this is obvious in one way. Making
one’s peace with one’s maker, or whatever psycho-spiritual powers one
believes exist – if one believes at all – becomes paramount amongst
those who are terminally ill. This is not to over-dramatise the situ-
ation, but to point out that in caring for the terminally ill, spiritual
urgency has to be taken into account. 22
This re-assertion of the importance of the spiritual aspects of hospice
and palliative care generally, may appear to be compromised by what one
hospice senior manager told me was a growing pressure – within the UK
certainly – for greater secularisation. This is not as straightforward as it
sounds, for spirituality and secularism are not mutually excluding catego-
ries of human activity or belief. As more government funding comes into
the hospice movement, it does so on the assumption that hospices offer their
services to people of all faiths and as well as none. While respecting the
Christian origins of many of them, it is likely that future public funding
programmes would not wish to see an over-emphasis on Christian ico-
nography and symbolism in the newer designs and buildings. For the most
part, the people I talked to in the hospices and hospitals I visited generally
approved of the move towards secularism or religious neutrality, and felt
patients thought similarly. For many, death provides its own religion.
Even so, secularism in design and the provision of care and treatment,
does not require people to disengage with the deeper issues of ritual and
occasion. What anthropologists mean by ritual are those acts or rites which
enable one to pass through a place or time of great danger, in safety, as one
common definition so succinctly puts it. 23 The sociologist Douglas Davies
has argued that all anthropology is about the way people use ritual to make
death disappear, or to tame it and rid it of its psychological violence. Later

-- 184 of 203 --

170 	t H e e V e n i n g L a n d
on we may make marks in the landscape to make it appear again, but in
ordered and controlled ways. 24
Both architecture and landscape design have long histories of creating
spaces which are also imbued with a sense of stillness, a slowing down of
time, as well as allowing movement and processional: in brief, in making
places out of spaces. As the architectural writer Katherine Shonfield once
wrote, ‘Architecture can break spells’. 25 It can dissolve despair, but it can
also cast spells for the good too. It is important to remind ourselves once
again, as discussed earlier, that architecture is as much about time as it
is about space, and in the words of cultural historian Robert Pogue Har-
rison: ‘Architecture creates the places where human time takes place’. 26
Few people would now wish to separate out the spatial and the temporal
in the design of new buildings, particularly with regard to their ritual and
processional elements. The Dutch architect, Aldo van Eyck has similarly
expressed a view on this issue when he wrote that, ‘whatever time and space
mean, place and occasion mean more’. 27
Imbuing a new building with a sense of the spiritual is not easy. Think
of the disaster of the Millennium Dome in London, intended to be a great
spiritual monument to the dawn of a new era in human history, but which
ended up as a gimcrack fairground and commercial rock music venue. Was
it the fault of the building or the programme, or was it a complete failure to
understand the inter-relationship between the two? As someone said of the
sheer vacuity of the space, it was as if a group of people had built a cathedral
without having invented Christianity first. The bricks and mortar, timber
and glass, concrete and steel that we use to shelter our humanity have in
the end to enclose and support patterns of use – to create their own micro-
cosmos as Christian Norberg-Schulz has written – that expand to fill every
space with human intentionality. 28 Only this makes the building complete.
The philosopher Wittgenstein, also an architect, once wrote that ‘Where
there is nothing to glorify there can be no architecture’. 29 The need to elab-
orate an architecture that is rich in presence and a sense of occasion is as
much a problem for a secular society as it is for one in which many faiths
co-exist.
A place apart?
There is of course a room or a space in every hospital or hospice deliber-
ately designed to embody a sense of the sacred or the spiritual, and that
traditionally has been the chapel, though it is interesting how little they
are dealt with in most contemporary studies of hospital architecture. 30
Today, these rooms or spaces are sometimes shaped and named in other
ways – as sanctuaries or contemplation rooms. Once at the centre of the
hospital or historic hospice – as the chapel still remains at St Joseph’s,
Hackney, and in many of the 18th and 19th century Irish hospitals and hos-
pices I visited – these gathering places for the those in need of a place that
is quiet and uplifting are now more ambiguous in their form and function.
Many people today find that too much stained glass, chandelier lighting, or

-- 185 of 203 --

t H e e V e n i n g L a n d 	171
purple and pomp in the design of such rooms, creates the danger of ‘over-
orchestrating the emotions’, according to one person I interviewed. ‘Too
much mannerism in style disempowers the user’, another told me. If the
chapel or sanctuary is too big – a common complaint – the single mourner
or lone supplicant may feel abandoned. If it is too small, a family group
may feel they are colonising a space and excluding others. There were
commonly heard criticisms.
Today, such rooms are often used for informal gatherings of family mem-
bers or close friends, though in my experience many were occupied during
the visits I made by lone individuals, including staff members, seeking peace
and quiet. This they found in a room designed to provide a heightened
sense of occasion through its shape, double-height ceiling, artistic window
designs and slightly more solemn interior furnishings. Hospice staff told me
how recently bereaved individuals frequently came in at all times of the day
or night to sit in the sanctuary room for a while, and that this need to visit
could continue for up to two years following the actual death. There is no
doubt that it is an important space, but there is also a wide range of opinion
as to the size this space should be and what emotional effects it should be
trying to amplify, complement or possibly assuage.
Occasionally, the sanctuary room was used as a viewing room for the
body shortly after death – this was the case in one children’s hospice vis-
ited. The room itself was both simple and calming, a double-height cir-
cular space, with white-washed interior walls, a single wall tapestry, and
evenly lit from a circular roof-light. It was little larger than a patient room,
and even when empty, felt intimate and peaceful. However, the use of such
rooms for the viewing of bodies is generally rare. Formal religious memo-
rial services are now equally rare it seems, and no longer greatly encour-
aged in a number of hospices. The interior design language of such rooms is
a kind of subdued Anglican asperity.
These rooms also occupy a key position in the public face of the hospice
building, either taking the form of a double-height rotunda or octagonal
outcrop, often with vertical stained-glass windows, usually located close
to the front entrance. The chapel or sanctuary building is one of the main
signalling devices that is used to distinguish the hospice from a residential
home. There is of course a danger in restricting the idea of the sacred or the
spiritual to just one room – almost an over-concentration of effect – when
most people I have talked to feel that the whole of the hospice building and
its setting should be imbued with a sense of the dignifying and sacramental.
Yet, anthropologists insist that one definition of the sacred is that it is a place
apart.31 It can be argued that the hospice itself is a place apart, distinct from
the everyday world and townscape, while the Maggie’s Centres, though
located within the boundaries of large hospitals, retain a critical distance,
and in many ways gain from the juxtaposition of the ‘industrial-medical’
and the reflective and therapeutic.
For the families and friends of those who have needed inpatient care
in hospices, and especially for those who died there, the building itself
assumes great symbolic importance, as some hospice managers have
realised to their cost. In his pioneering study of the architectural and

-- 186 of 203 --

172 	t H e e V e n i n g L a n d
therapeutic affordances of hospice buildings, John Harries discusses the
case of Pilgrims Hospice, Canterbury.32 This was established after a popu-
lar fund-raising effort in 1982, acquiring a large Victorian mansion which
was converted into an inpatient hospice with 16 beds. In 2014, the hospice
trust managers announced that due to continuing deficits the building must
close and hospice services should focus more on home-based palliative
care. The public response came fast and furious, especially when it was said
that a common complaint heard amongst management was that we should
‘stop spending on bricks and mortar’.33 If for some hospice buildings are
simply ‘bricks and mortar’, this does suggest a poor understanding of the
symbolic significance and affordances of architecture, especially so when
it comes to buildings associated with the great rituals of human life, at all
times and in all cultures.
Harries took a random sample of 200 signed petitions in which people
gave reasons for their opposition to the closure, in which half of them men-
tioned that they had had relatives or friends who had died in the Victorian
building. Two main reasons given were that neither hospitals nor many
homes were suitable places for very sick people to die in, and that buildings
themselves ‘mobilised fund-raising support without which the organisa-
tion would collapse’. Harries noted that ‘Emotional appeals focused on the
building as a reliable place of safety should it be needed and as a memorial
for those who had died there’.
In detailing this unhappy episode, Harries concluded that:
The hospice building has profound significance for four groups of peo-
ple. For thousands of people in the community who are looking back it
is the locus of memories of the last days of loved ones and a memorial
to them. For those working in hospices as employees or volunteers the
building is a place of meaningful and fulfilling work. For those being
treated in the hospices and for their loved ones it is a place of safety
and companionship, of relief of suffering and, to some extent, fear. For
those in the community living in ‘the shadow of the future’ it holds out
the prospect of sanctuary should they need it and alleviates anxieties
about their own death.34
Today, Harries argues, hospices have acquired the status of ‘sacred spaces’,
almost irrespective of their origins, whether as adapted buildings or as
intentionally designed architectural expressions of sanctuary and care.
One definition of sacred in relation to buildings is that their destruction
is not simply a utilitarian act but one of defilement, for in Harries’s words,
‘I would argue that if Pilgrims Canterbury was not recognised as a sacred
space to begin with it became so during the attempted process of dese-
cration’. We might now wish the same for all institutions offering care to
the elderly and vulnerable, that they embody something of the sacred, for
as Alvar Aalto once reminded us, ‘Architecture should defend Man at his
weakest’.35
The problem that hospices have had with the architectural language
of these chapel or sanctuary spaces in particular (rather than the overall

-- 187 of 203 --

t H e e V e n i n g L a n d 	173
design language of the main building complex), echoes those encountered
in the early days of another new, not entirely unrelated building type of the
20th century: the crematorium. The practices and rituals associated with
cremation grew in public esteem as a result of being thought to be more
scientific and rational, combining efficiency with a strong sense of civic
welfare and concern. Death and bodily disposal were regarded as sensitive
matters that could be handled with dignity, even without religion. For a
long time, cremation was resisted by many religions, some of which refuse
to countenance it even today. Nevertheless, advocates of cremation – of
which town planners were amongst the most fervent, fearing ever larger
amounts of green belt land being taken up for burial – had serious problems
in finding an appropriate architectural language in which to express these
new ideals.36
The early crematoria were designed emulating a Gothic style, to reassure
people that these buildings really were just another kind of church. Later, a
Lombardy Romanesque style was adopted, with its associations of Renais-
sance humanism. On the post-war period what is now sometimes called
Scandinavian welfare modernism became the signature style of many new
civic buildings, including crematoria. The fact that town planners were so
keen on cremation, and that many of the buildings were designed by munic-
ipal architects, tended to produce an overly civic architectural style which,
to be fair, has in most cases lasted well.
Even so, the consensus amongst architectural critics, is that some were
mediocre. The debate about what an appropriate architectural style might
best suit this unusual building type has raged now for nearly a hundred
years, without satisfactory resolution, as it may yet do with the hospice.
Both building types suggest how difficult it is to find a secular design style
when it comes to the enduring human need for ritual ceremony. If it is a
problem for secularism, this is partly because it is a problem for modern-
ism too, though one which the Maggie’s Centres seem to have successfully
resolved. The architectural critic Edwin Heathcote, whose poor experi-
ences of hospital were cited in the opening chapter, has written that:
Thus the modern architecture of death is an eclectic reflection of the
pluralism of the late-twentieth century and of the uncertainty which
death brings in its wake. Modernism has always had a deep and pro-
found problem with the architecture of death.37
Another critic puts it more brutally, describing a recently built cremato-
rium in England:
A marriage of the industrial and the domestic perfectly describes any
crematorium’s grim banality. This scheme’s self-effacing anonymity is
in the 20th century tradition of crematorium design, which appears to
prize the unthreatening and suburban above all else. It could easily be
a primary school, a visitor centre or an overgrown bungalow. Surely
death is deserving of being marked in a more powerful and distinctive
way.38

-- 188 of 203 --

174 	t H e e V e n i n g L a n d
There is a sense in which the same criticism could be levelled at some hos-
pices, particularly the earlier ones dating from the 1960s and 1970s. While it
is true that hospices go out of their way to emphasise that they are about liv-
ing well to the end, and not death-focused, they have nevertheless become
part of our familiar built landscape. The public have unselfconsciously
adopted them with some affection and admiration, without being in denial
that these are places where people go to die. Others may learn from this, for
while the first edition of this book focused on hospices almost entirely, in
this new iteration I have tried to demonstrate their invaluable influence in
recent decades across the whole palliative and social care sector.
What they offer in particular as much as a quality of space is a quality
of time. They have travelled far beyond the eerily silent places of the ger-
iatric ward, where time once hung heavy and a sense of hopelessness per-
vaded the atmosphere like dust. The philosopher Henri Bergson famously
divided time into two distinct types: clock time and endurance. Most of our
lives are ordered by the clock, providing a structure and a sense of onward
movement that keep us relatively positive about the direction of our lives.
But there are also times, usually of great anxiety or stress, when we expe-
rience time as a form of endurance, a meaningless waiting imbued with a
general sense of dread. The Italians have a phrase for those kinds of days
where nothing seems to happen, and where ‘nothing is combined’. Ameri-
can writer, Raymond Carver, published a heart-breaking short story called
‘A small good thing’, about a couple whose child is fatally injured in a road
accident. As they wait in the hospital, being moved from room to room, as
the situation in the operating theatre worsens, Carver draws out the differ-
ent experiences they have of waiting, and how they try to come to terms
with the agonised passing of time that seems now to have no meaningful
past nor future.
Waiting can be a long, drawn-out agony, or it can become a form of med-
itation or absorption into the flow of life. In her essay, ‘A Place to Die’,
Cicely Saunders wrote of the ideal hospice as being ‘…a place where loss is
constantly being overcome by creativity and where the continual partings
are best expressed in words from T.S. Eliot’s Four Quartets: “Not fare well,
But fare forward, voyagers”’.39 This profoundly captures the strange ambi-
guity we have about the temporal position of the dead: are they ahead of us
or behind us? Do they wait for us somewhere in the metaphysical future, or
are they now behind us, having played their part in the saga of human his-
tory and mutability? Of Saunders herself the writer Kate Kellaway observed
that,
When Dame Cicely Saunders talks about death, it is as if she had died
many times herself without ill-effect. Her tone is of a well-travelled
person, comfortable with her subject, who can tell us what to pack,
what to say, how to prepare for another country.40
Eliot also famously wrote of those moments when time is suspended and we
seem to live at ‘the still point of a turning world’. Such times are occasions of
utter absorption, when the integrity of the personality seems undivided and

-- 189 of 203 --

t H e e V e n i n g L a n d 	175
complete, whether they occur in a quiet sunlit room, or in a garden alive
with birdsong. At the Maggie’s Centres, there are no chapels or sanctuary
rooms; instead the focal point of each and every building is a fire, a hearth
and a large kitchen table. People come and go as they wish, sit by the fire or
at the table, talk with others or sit and dream.41 This is architecture at its
best, where – confident in the mood of a beautiful building, in spaces which
allow emotions to expand and range freely – everything that matters and
mattered in life is gathered in.
